Elementary characterisation of ℚ p \mathbb{Q}_{p} by its absolute Galois group — A modern perspective
Leo Gitin, Jochen Koenigsmann, Benedikt Stock

Abstract
. In 1927, Artin and Schreier showed that a field is real closed if and only if its absolute Galois group has order two. Inspired by this characterisation and drawing on earlier work of Neukirch, Pop conjectured the following p p -adic analogue: a field is p p -adically closed if and only if its absolute Galois group is isomorphic to that of ℚ p \mathbb{Q}_{p} . In 1995, the conjecture was independently solved by Efrat for p ≠ 2 p\neq 2 and by Koenigsmann in full generality. Using novel techniques in the theory of valued fields developed over the last 25 years, we give a new elementary and self-contained proof of this theorem, which does not rely on local class field theory and Galois cohomology. We further highlight connections to the recent work of Jahnke-Kartas on perfectoid fields and model-theoretic transfer techniques. We provide a systematic account of all of our methods to encourage further investigations.



1.
Introduction

The main theme of Galois theory is the duality between extensions of fields and their associated Galois groups.
The
absolute Galois group
of a field
K
,

G_{K}\coloneq\Gal(K^{\sepc}/K),

is the Galois group of the separable closure of
K
over
K
. It is the largest possible Galois group associated to
K
, and moreover, a profinite group endowed with a natural (Krull) topology. Every Galois group of a finite Galois extension of
K
occurs as a quotient of
G_{K}
by some open subgroup of
G_{K}
.
Naturally, one might want to determine the structure of the absolute Galois group
G_{K}
for fields
K
of arithmetic interest—and conversely, perhaps more subtly, one might ask how much of the arithmetic of
K
is captured by
G_{K}
. This question lies at the heart of
anabelian geometry
. More generally, anabelian geometry seeks to understand how much of the information about a variety
X
is encoded in its étale fundamental group.

Perhaps the first result in this direction, even though it is not usually construed as such, is the classical theorem of Artin and Schreier that answers the above question for the case of finite absolute Galois groups.

Theorem 1.1

(Artin-Schreier 1927,
[
5
,
6
]
)
.

Let
K
be a field. The absolute Galois group
G_{K}
is finite if and only if

(1)

|G_{K}|=1
, in which case
K
is separably closed, or

(2)

|G_{K}|=2
, in which case
K
is real closed, i.e., a field that admits an ordering which does not extend to any proper algebraic extension of
K
.
1
1

1

Equivalently,
K
is real closed iff no quadratic sum
a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}+\ldots+a_{n}^{2}
in
K
is equal to
-1
, any polynomial of odd degree has a root, and for any
a\in K
, there exists
b\in K
such that
a=b^{2}
or
a=-b^{2}
.

Traditionally, the starting point of this investigation is considered to be the following result.

Theorem 1.2

(Neukirch-Uchida-Ikeda-Iwasawa)
.

Let
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
be algebraic number fields. Any topological isomorphism
G_{K_{1}}\cong G_{K_{2}}
is given by conjugation by a field isomorphism

\sigma:K_{1}^{\alg}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}K_{2}^{\alg}

restricting to
\sigma|_{K_{1}}:K_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}K_{2}
.
In particular, working within a fixed algebraic closure
\mathbb{Q}^{\alg}
of
\mathbb{Q}
, any two number fields
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
satisfy

(1.1)

K_{1}\cong K_{2}\Longleftrightarrow G_{K_{1}}\cong G_{K_{2}}.

In this result, the absolute Galois group
G_{K}
determines
K
uniquely up to isomorphism within the (rather restrictive) class of number fields.
The search for larger classes for which (
1.1
) would hold, leads—perhaps surprisingly—to considerations in mathematical logic.

Indeed, if we look back at Theorem
1.1
, the class of real closed fields may look unusual at first sight. However, the reader familiar with mathematical logic will recognise that the defining properties of real closedness are first-order properties of fields. Indeed, at the advent of
model theory
, Tarski identified the real closed fields as precisely those structures that are elementarily equivalent to
\mathbb{R}
, i.e., satisfying all the same first-order sentences as the structure
\mathbb{R}
in a language with symbols for ring operations
\mathcal{L}_{\ring}=\{0,1,+,-,\cdot\}
.

Theorem 1.3

(Tarski 1931/51)
.

A field
K
is real closed if and only if
K\equiv\mathbb{R}
, i.e.,
K
is elementarily equivalent to
\mathbb{R}
.

See
[
41
, 3.3]
for a modern presentation of Tarski’s Theorem. Crucially, note that if
K
is real closed, the formula
\exists z\,(y-x=z^{2})
defines the unique ordering
x\leq y
that makes
K
an ordered field. This is an instance of the phenomenon that
G_{K}
codes
extra structure
on
K
.

Corollary 1.4
.

For any field
K
,

1<|G_{K}|<\infty\quad\text{if and only if}\quad K\equiv\mathbb{R}.

From a model-theoretic point of view, the arithmetic of
K
is described by the set of first-order sentences that hold in
K
.
Viewed through this lens, the preceding result shows that
G_{\mathbb{R}}
fully determines the arithmetic of
\mathbb{R}
.

Likewise, the arithmetic of the
p
-adic field
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is well-understood by the following celebrated result of Ax, Kochen, and Ershov
[
8
,
21
]
.

Theorem 1.5

(Ax-Kochen, Ershov 1965)
.

A field
K
is elementarily equivalent to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
if and only if it admits a (Krull) valuation
v
such that:

(1)

(K,v)
is henselian of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
;

(2)

v(p)
is minimal positive;

(3)

the value group
vK
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-group, i.e.,
vK\equiv\mathbb{Z}
;

(4)

the residue field is equal to
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

This theorem thus describes the class of
p
-adically closed fields
. Note that a valuation satisfying (i)–(iv) is always unique and given by a uniform formula in the language of rings, see (
4.4
). Hence, axioms (i)–(iv) can be rewritten as a set of
\mathcal{L}_{\ring}
-sentences.

Abstractly speaking, there are
p
-adically closed fields of any infinite cardinality by the Löwenheim-Skolem Theorem. However, this class also includes natural algebraic examples.

Example 1.6
.

(1)

The field of algebraic
p
-adic numbers
k=\mathbb{Q}_{p}\cap\mathbb{Q}^{\alg}
is
p
-adically closed.

(2)

The field of Puiseux series over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,

K=\bigcup_{n>0}\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\!(t^{1/n})\!),

is
p
-adically closed. This is perhaps the simplest example of rank two.

In an early advance towards the Galois characterisation of
p
-adically closed fields, Pop obtained a formative result extending Neukirch’s within a restriction of the class of
p
-adically closed fields
[
44
, Thm. E.11]
.

Theorem 1.7

(Pop 1988)
.

Let
K
be a field with
K^{\alg}=K\mathbb{Q}^{\alg}
. Then

G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\Longleftrightarrow K\equiv\mathbb{Q}_{p}.

Throughout, we will tacitly assume that isomorphisms between profinite groups are topological group isomorphisms.

Pop conjectured that his theorem holds without any extra restrictions on
K

[
44
, E.7]
. The conjecture was independently solved by Efrat
[
15
]
(for
p\neq 2
) and Koenigsmann
[
30
]
(for all primes
p
).

Main Theorem

(Efrat/Koenigsmann 1995)
.

For any field
K
,

G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\text{$K$ is $p$-adically closed.}

Perhaps the most notable feature of the Main Theorem is the reappearance of the same phenomenon we have encountered in the case of real closed fields: the absolute Galois group of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}

encodes extra structure on
K
—namely, that of a valuation (of any potential rank), satisfying the axioms (i)–(iv) in Theorem
1.5
. This provides a striking piece of evidence for the point of view that higher rank valuations appear “in nature”—even outside of model theory.

The main goal of our paper is to give a new and elementary proof of the Main Theorem. A first sketch of such an elementary proof was given by the second author in a lecture “Encoding arithmetic in Galois groups” at Oxford in Trinity Term 2022. Efrat and Koenigsmann’s original arguments
[
15
,
30
]
build on Pop’s earlier work
[
44
]
, which analyses Galois groups using a range of methods, including Galois cohomology and class field theory.
Their new key ingredient is the “creation” of valuations from so-called rigid elements (an idea that had its origins in quadratic form theory).

There are several reasons why we propose a revised proof of the Main Theorem. First, the original arguments rely heavily on more advanced machinery (Galois cohomology). In particular, no self-contained exposition currently exists in the literature. Secondly, some of the underlying techniques were only recently understood, and modern developments shed new light on certain key steps. We replace many of the old arguments by more elementary considerations; the proof presented here avoids cohomological tools entirely.
Thirdly, we offer a modern perspective that shows interactions with contemporary developments.

Proof strategy

The main ingredients in our proof are:

(1)

A systematic use of coarsenings (Standard Decomposition).

(2)

The Galois characterisation of henselianity; in particular,

•

the creation of valuations, and

•

going-down statements for henselianity.

(3)

Transfer results of Krasner-Kazhdan-Deligne type.

(4)

Explicit constructions replacing abstract cohomological considerations.

(5)

Pop’s Lemma on mixed characteristic henselian valued fields with absolute Galois group of finite
p
-rank.

The proof proceeds as follows. We begin by constructing a henselian valuation
v_{K}
on
K
using the Galois characterisation of henselianity. Combining explicit criteria inspired by Galois cohomology with a careful analysis of the roots of unity in
K
, we then show that
(K,v_{K})
has mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. This enables us to apply the Standard Decomposition, expressing
v_{K}
as a composition of three valuations, with the middle component
\overline{v_{p}}
having rank one and mixed characteristic. Utilising Pop’s Lemma together with transfer principles of Krasner–Kazhdan–Deligne type, we demonstrate that
\overline{v_{p}}
has value group
\mathbb{Z}
and residue field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Finally, we translate these structural properties back to the original valuation
v_{K}
and verify that it satisfies all four axioms of Theorem
1.5
.

Structure of this paper

Chapter
2
is a survey of valuation theory, aimed at the reader familiar with rank 1 (i.e. real-valued) valuations—those that correspond to absolute values on fields—but not familiar with higher rank (i.e. Krull) valuations that take values in an ordered abelian group. Some parts of our exposition do not appear in standard textbooks; for example, the Standard Decomposition of a valuation (Theorem
2.46
), which has become increasingly relevant.

Chapter
3
connects valued fields to their absolute Galois groups. Section
3.1
deals with most of the computational aspects around
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. In Section
3.2
, we prove Pop’s Lemma. In Section
3.3
, we present both explicit and elementary criteria for distinguishing fields of characteristic 0 from those of positive characteristic
p
. Although motivated by ideas from Galois cohomology, the results are self-contained and rely only on Hilbert’s Theorem 90. In Section
3.4
, we prove the Galois characterisation of henselianity, providing a method to recover henselian valuations from absolute Galois groups.

Chapter
4
contains a survey of transfer methods between fields of different characteristic. The main result that we will need for the proof of the Main Theorem is the Transfer Lemma (Corollary
4.10
), for which we give two proofs. In Section
4.2
, we give a new proof based on the tilting construction.
In Section
4.3
, we introduce the Saturation-Decomposition Method—recently employed by Jahnke-Kartas
[
25
]
in a spectacular fashion to understand the model theory of perfectoid fields—and explain how it yields a second proof of the Transfer Lemma. We will motivate this method using ramification theory and provide optimal conditions under which it applies (this is the Taming Theorem
4.23
). As a consequence, we obtain a new proof of a result of Kuhlmann-Rzepka
[
37
]
on roughly deeply ramified fields (Corollary
4.26
).
In Section
4.4
, we sketch how Jahnke and Kartas’ proofs fit into this framework.

Chapter
5
contains the proof of the Main Theorem.

2.
Methods in valuation theory

This chapter will provide us with our basic toolbox. It is not meant to replace textbook treatments such as Engler-Prestel
[
20
]
and Efrat
[
17
]
. Rather, we aim for a transparent synopsis. Our main goals in this section are:

(1)

Introduce the three equivalent ways of thinking about valuations on a field
K
, as indicated by the following bijections:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{$v$ valuation on $K$}\}/{\sim}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{$\mathcal{O}$ valuation ring on $K$}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\text{$\varphi$ place on $K$}\}/{\sim}}

Our treatment emphasises the places point-of-view (Section
2.1
).

(2)

Explain how valuations behave under algebraic extensions of fields and the associated ramification (Hilbert-Dedekind) theory (Section
2.2
).

(3)

Examine how valuations interact with one another when several valuations are present on a given field (Section
2.3
):

(a)

Explain coarsenings, and how they give rise to the concept of decompositions (Section
2.3.1
).

(b)

Introduce natural choices of valuations in a general abstract setting—first, the coarsenings of the Standard Decomposition (Section
2.3.2
) and,

(c)

secondly, the canonical henselian valuation (Section
2.3.3
).

(4)

Explain the creation of abstract valuations from rigid and
p
-rigid elements (Section
2.4
).

2.1.
Valuations, valuation rings, and places

2.1.1.
Ordered abelian groups

Abstract valuations on fields take values in ordered abelian groups, generalising real-valued valuations.

Definition 2.1
.

A
convex
subgroup
\Delta
of an ordered abelian group
(\Gamma,\leq)
is a subgroup such that for any positive
\delta\in\Delta
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
0\leq|\gamma|\leq\delta
implies
\gamma\in\Delta
.
We write
\Gamma_{>\gamma}
for the interval
(\gamma,\infty)
in
\Gamma
, and

\Conv(X)\coloneqq\{\gamma\in\Gamma:|\gamma|\leq\delta\text{ for some $\delta\in\langle X\rangle$}\}

for the
convex hull
of a set
X\subseteq\Gamma
(here,
\langle X\rangle
denotes the group generated by
X
in
\Gamma
).
We further write
\Conv(\gamma)\coloneqq\Conv(\{\gamma\})
for
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Any convex subgroup
\Delta
naturally gives rise to an ordering on the quotient group
\Gamma/\Delta
. Crucially, the set of convex subgroups of
\Gamma
is linearly ordered by inclusion. The
(archimedean) rank
of
\Gamma
is the cardinality of the set of proper convex subgroups.
The following is classical:

Fact 2.2

(Hölder)
.

Any ordered abelian group that is archimedean
2
2

2

Strictly speaking: Eudoxian. Archimedes attributes the theory of magnitudes to Eudoxus, but this has been lost to history.
, i.e., of rank 1, embeds into the ordered group of reals.

For ordered abelian groups, we have an intrinsic notion of infinitesimals:

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\Gamma
and
\gamma\in\Gamma_{>0}
. We say
\varepsilon
is
infinitesimal with respect to

\gamma
(in symbols:
\varepsilon\ll\gamma
), if
n|\varepsilon|<\gamma
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

To any
\gamma\in\Gamma_{>0}
, we associated two canonical convex subgroups: the minimal convex subgroup containing
\gamma
and the maximal convex subgroup not containing
\gamma
. These are, respectively,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{+}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Conv(\gamma)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{-}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\varepsilon\in\Gamma:|\varepsilon|\ll\gamma\}.

Fact 2.4
.

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma_{>0}
, the ordered quotient group
\Gamma^{+}_{\gamma}/\Gamma^{-}_{\gamma}
is of rank 1.

2.1.2.
Valuations and valuation rings

Basic examples of discrete valuations are the
p
-adic valuation on
\mathbb{Q}
and the order of vanishing at
X
in a field of rational functions
k(X)
. Abstract (Krull) valuations generalise this notion.

Definition 2.5
.

A
valuation
on a field
K
with values in an ordered abelian group
(\Gamma,\leq)
is a surjective map

v:K\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma\cup\{\infty\}

that satisfies the three properties

(1)

v(x)=\infty
if and only if
x=0
;

(2)

v(x\cdot y)=v(x)+v(y)
(additivity);

(3)

v(x+y)\geq\min\{v(x),v(y)\}
(ultrametric triangle inequality);

where the relations
\gamma<\infty
and
\gamma+\infty=\infty+\gamma=\infty+\infty=\infty
are assumed for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
.
The pair
(K,v)
is called a
valued field
.

Associated to any
(K,v)
are the following basic objects:

(1)

\Gamma
is called the
value group
of
v
and is alternatively denoted by
vK
or
\Gamma_{v}
.

(2)

\mathcal{O}_{v}\coloneqq\{x\in K:v(x)\geq 0\}
is the
valuation ring
of
v
.

(3)

\mathcal{M}_{v}\coloneqq\{x\in K:v(x)>0\}
is the unique maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{v}
.

(4)

\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{v}
is the
residue field
of
v
and denoted by
Kv
. We sometimes write
xv
,
\overline{x}
, or
\res(x)
for the residue class
x\mathcal{M}_{v}
of
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
.

(5)

The
rank
of
(K,v)
is the archimedean rank of its value group
\Gamma
.

(6)

The pair
(\charK K,\charK Kv)
is the
characteristic
of
(K,v)
.

(7)

The
valuation topology
is induced by basic open sets of balls

B_{\gamma}(x)\coloneqq\{y\in K:v(y-x)>\gamma\},\quad\text{where $\gamma>0$, $x\in K,$}

making
K
into a topological field. Whenever we refer to topological properties of a valued field, it is with respect to this topology.

Fact 2.6
.

The invariants above form canonical short exact sequences

(2.1)

\begin{gathered}1\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}\longrightarrow K^{\times}\longrightarrow vK\longrightarrow 1\\
1\longrightarrow 1+\mathcal{M}_{v}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}\longrightarrow(Kv)^{\times}\longrightarrow 1.\end{gathered}

The valuation
v
can be recovered solely from the ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}
, and, moreover, axioms
1
–
3
correspond to abstract properties of the ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}
.

Definition 2.7
.

An (abstract)
valuation ring
is an integral domain
A
such that for any non-zero element
a\in\Frac(A)
,
a\in A
or
a^{-1}\in A
.

Let
K=\Frac(A)
. If
A
is an abstract valuation ring, then
K
can be endowed with a canonical valuation with valuation ring
A
. This is the quotient map

v_{A}:K^{\times}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow K^{\times}/A^{\times},

where the latter becomes an ordered abelian group by declaring

xA^{\times}\leq yA^{\times}:\Longleftrightarrow\tfrac{y}{x}\in A

and switching from multiplicative to additive notation (in particular,
1A^{\times}
is identified with the neutral element 0). Clearly,
\mathcal{O}_{v_{A}}=A
. Conversely, given a valued field
(K,v)
, the valuations
v
and
v_{\mathcal{O}_{v}}
are
equivalent
in the sense of the following commutative diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\cup\,\{\infty\}$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\cup\,\{\infty\}$\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\mathcal{O}_{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

2.1.3.
Places

We give a third and final description of the concept.

Definition 2.8
.

A
place
on
K
is given by a field
k
together with a surjective map

\varphi:K\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow k\cup\{\infty\}

that satisfies the three “ring homomorphism” properties

\displaystyle\varphi(x+y)=\varphi(x)+\varphi(y),

\displaystyle\varphi(x\cdot y)=\varphi(x)\cdot\varphi(y),

\displaystyle\varphi(1)=1,

where addition and multiplication on
k
are extended to
k\cup\{\infty\}
subject to the relations

a+\infty=\infty+a=\infty\quad\text{and}\quad a\cdot\infty=\infty\cdot a=\infty\cdot\infty=\infty

for any
a\in k
. We leave
\infty+\infty
,
0\cdot\infty
, and
\infty\cdot 0
undefined and the conditions on
\varphi
vacuous in these three cases.

The relationship between valuations and places can be described as follows. Given a valuation
v
on
K
with residue field
Kv=\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{v}
, taking residues induces a place
\varphi_{v}
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{v}:K

\displaystyle\longrightarrow Kv\cup\{\infty\}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto xv\coloneqq\begin{cases}x\mathcal{M}_{v}&\text{if $x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}$}\\
\infty&\text{if $x\notin\mathcal{O}_{v}$.}\end{cases}

Conversely, given a place
\varphi:K\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow k\cup\{\infty\}
, it induces a valuation

v_{\varphi}:K\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow K^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}^{\times}\cup\{\infty\}

via the valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{\varphi}\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}(k)
.

These two constructions are mutually inverse—except that we need to identify two valuations
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
and places
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
according to the following commutative diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\cup\,\{\infty\}$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{2}\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\cup\,\{\infty\}$\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{1}\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\cup\,\{\infty\}$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\cup\,\{\infty\}$\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}

Under this identification,
v
and
v_{\varphi_{v}}
, respectively,
\varphi
and
\varphi_{v_{\varphi}}
, become equivalent.

It will be crucial to translate between these three equivalent ways of viewing valuations.

2.1.4.
Extensions of valuations

Let
L/K
be a field extension, and assume that
L
and
K
come equipped with valuations
w
and
v
, respectively. We say that
(L,w)/(K,v)
is an
extension of valued fields
, and that
w
is a
prolongation
of
v
, if
w|_{K}
is equivalent to
v
, or, correspondingly,
\mathcal{O}_{v}=\mathcal{O}_{w}\cap K
. Conversely, any valuation on
(K,v)
can be restricted to any subfield
F
of
K
such that
(K,v)/(F,v|_{F})
is an extension of valued fields.

The following facts are fundamental (for proofs, see e.g.
[
20
, Chap. 3]
):

Theorem 2.9

(Chevalley)
.

Let
L/K
be an arbitrary extension of fields. Then any valuation
v
on
K
extends to some valuation
w
on
L
.

Fact 2.10
.

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
and
L/K
a purely inseparable extension. Then any valuation
v
on
K
extends uniquely to
L
via
v(x^{1/p^{n}})=\frac{1}{p^{n}}v(x)
for any
x^{1/p^{n}}\in L
.

Fact 2.11
.

Let
(L,w)/(K,v)
be an algebraic extension of valued fields. Then any element in
wL/vK
has finite order, i.e.,
wL
is contained in the divisible hull of
vK
, and
Lw/Kv
is an algebraic extension of residue fields.

Fact 2.12
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field and
L/K
an algebraic extension. If
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
denote valuations on
L
extending
v
, then
\mathcal{O}_{w_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{w_{2}}
implies
\mathcal{O}_{w_{1}}=\mathcal{O}_{w_{2}}
.

In particular, it follows from the above that if
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are not equivalent, then
\mathcal{O}_{w_{1}}\not\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{w_{2}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{w_{2}}\not\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{w_{1}}
, i.e., the valuation rings of
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
are
incomparable
.
Incomparable valuation rings satisfy the following form of the Chinese remainder theorem:

Fact 2.13

(Weak approximation theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{v_{n}}
be pairwise incomparable valuation rings on
K
. Then, for any tuple
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{v_{i}}
, there exists
x\in K
such that
x-x_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{v_{i}}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.

The following describes the most basic connection to Galois theory:

Theorem 2.14

(Conjugacy Theorem)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field and
L/K
a normal algebraic extension. Then
\Aut(L/K)
acts transitively on the set of extensions
w
of
v
, i.e., for any two extensions
w
and
w^{\prime}
on
L
, there exists an automorphism
\sigma\in\Aut(L/K)
such that
\mathcal{O}_{w^{\prime}}=\sigma\mathcal{O}_{w}
.

Beyond that, one can show that the conjugate roots of a polynomial
f(X)
vary continuously in the coefficients of
f(X)
.

Fact 2.15

(Continuity of roots)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field and
\tilde{v}
an extension of
v
to
K^{\sepc}
. Assume
f(X)=a_{n}X^{n}+\ldots+a_{1}X+a_{0}
is a polynomial over
K
with pairwise distinct roots
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. Then for any
\gamma\in vK
, there exists
\delta\in vK
and a permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, such that for any polynomial
g(X)=b_{n}X^{n}+\ldots+b_{1}X+b_{0}\in K[X]
with roots
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
satisfying

\min_{1\leq i\leq n}v(a_{i}-b_{i})\geq\delta,

we have

\min_{1\leq i\leq n}\tilde{v}(x_{i}-y_{\sigma(i)})\geq\gamma.

Remark 2.16
.

Note that if
\gamma>\max_{i\neq j}\tilde{v}(x_{i}-x_{j})
, then additionally,

\tilde{v}(y_{\sigma(i)}-y_{\sigma(j)})=\tilde{v}(x_{i}-x_{j}).

The following refines Fact
2.11
into a quantitative form:

Fact 2.17

(Fundamental inequality, simple form)
.

Let
(L,w)/(K,v)
be a finite extension of valued fields. Assume that

A=\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq e}\subseteq L^{\times}B=\{b_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq f}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{w}^{\times}

are given such that
wA
is a set of representatives for
wL/vK
and
Bw\subseteq Lw
is
Kv
-linearly independent. Then

A\cdot B=\{a_{i}b_{j}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq e\\
1\leq j\leq f\end{subarray}}

is
K
-linearly independent.
In particular,

(2.2)

[L:K]\geq(wL:vK)[Lw:Kv].

We have the following nomenclature for extensions of valued fields.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
(L,w)/(K,v)
be a finite extension of valued fields. The basic ramification invariants of the extension are:

(1)

the
ramification index

e(w/v)\coloneqq(wL:vK)<\infty
, and

(2)

the
degree of inertia

f(w/v)\coloneqq[Lw:Kv]<\infty
.

We say that the extension
(L,w)/(K,v)
is

(3)

immediate
if
e(w/v)=f(w/v)=1
;

(4)

inert
if
f(w/v)=[L:K]
;

(5)

unramified
if it is inert and
Lw/Kv
is separable;

(6)

totally ramified
if
e(w/v)=[L:K]
;

(7)

tamely ramified
if
e(w/v)=[L:K]
and
p\nmid e(w/v)
whenever
\charK Kv=p>0
;

(8)

tame
if
e(w/v)f(w/v)=[L:K]
,
p\nmid e(w/v)
, and
Lw/Kv
is separable (
\charK Kv=p>0
);

(9)

purely wild
if
e(w/v)
is a power of
p
and
Lw/Kv
is purely inseparable (
\charK Kv=p>0
).

For any of the above properties
P
, we say that an algebraic extension
(L,w)/(K,v)

is
P
if all finite subextensions are
P
. In residue characteristic 0, by definition, all algebraic extensions are tame, and there are no non-trivial purely wild extensions.

2.2.
Structure theory

Ramification theory (sometimes also called Dedekind-Hilbert theory) relates the structure of Galois groups of extensions of valued fields to the structure of their basic invariants, the value group and residue field. For proofs, we point the reader to
[
17
, Chap. 15–17]
. For valuations of higher rank, the theory is due to Krull and Deuring.

2.2.1.
General ramification theory

We associate to any Galois extension
(L,w)/(K,v)
of valued fields a filtration

\Gal(L/K)\supseteq D_{w}(L/K)\supseteq I_{w}(L/K)\supseteq R_{w}(L/K)\qquad\text{(shortened: $G\supseteq D\supseteq I\supseteq R$)}

of the Galois group
G=\Gal(L/K)
.

Definition 2.19
.

We define

(1)

the
decomposition group

D\coloneqq\{\sigma\in G:\sigma\mathcal{O}_{w}=\mathcal{O}_{w}\}=\{\sigma\in G:\sigma(x)-x\in\mathcal{O}_{w}\text{ for all $x\in\mathcal{O}_{w}$}\};

(2)

the
inertia group

I\coloneqq\{\sigma\in G:\sigma(x)-x\in\mathcal{M}_{w}\text{ for all $x\in\mathcal{O}_{w}$}\}
;

(3)

the
ramification group

R\coloneqq\{\sigma\in G:\sigma(x)-x\in x\mathcal{M}_{w}\text{ for all $x\in\mathcal{O}_{w}$}\}
.

We denote the corresponding fixed fields by
L^{D}
,
L^{I}
, and
L^{R}
, respectively.

The groups
D
,
I
, and
R
encode structural information about the extension
(L,w)/(K,v)
.

Fact 2.20

(Decomposition group
D
)
.

Let
(L,w)/(K,v)
be a Galois extension of valued fields. Then

(1)

(L^{D},w|_{L^{D}})/(L,v)
is immediate.

(2)

w
is the
unique
prolongation of
w|_{L^{D}}
to
L
.

(3)

v
has exactly
r=[L^{D}:K]=(G:D)
prolongations of
v
to
L
.

Fact 2.21

(Inertia group
I
)
.

The homomorphism

\displaystyle\Phi:D

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Aut(Lw/Kv)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\longmapsto\bigl(\overline{x}\longmapsto\overline{\sigma(x)}\bigr)

is well-defined. It induces a short exact sequence

(2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut(Lw/Kv)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

which we call the
First Exact Sequence
. In particular,
I
is normal and closed in
D
.
Moreover,

(1)

L^{I}w|_{L^{I}}/Kv
is relatively separably closed in
Lw/Kv
.

(2)

L^{I}/L^{D}
is inert.
In particular,
w|_{L^{I}}L^{I}=vK
.

Fact 2.22

(Ramification group
R
)
.

Let
\mu_{Lw}
denote the set of roots of unity in
Lw
. Then, the homomorphism

\displaystyle\Psi:I

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Hom(wL/vK,\mu_{Lw})

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Bigl(wx+vK\longmapsto\overline{\sigma(x)/x}\Bigr)

is well-defined and induces a short exact sequence

(2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(wL/vK,\mu_{Lw})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

which we call the
Second Exact Sequence
. Moreover,

(1)

R
is a normal closed subgroup of
I
;

(2)

R
is the unique normal Sylow
p
-subgroup of
I
, whenever
\charK Kv=p>0
. It is trivial if
\charK Kv=0
.

(3)

L^{R}/L^{I}
is tamely ramified and maximal with this property.

We can summarise these findings in a diagram.

Galois group
field
value group

residue field

(char.
p>0
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lw}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{1}{p^{\prime\infty}}vK\cap wL}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lw\cap(Kv)^{\sepc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vK}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lw\cap(Kv)^{\sepc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vK}

Figure.
Diagram summarising the properties of the decomposition subgroup
D
, the inertia group
I
, and the ramification group
R
, as well as their respective fixed fields and corresponding value groups and residue fields (Facts
2.20
,
2.21
,
2.22
). For
\charK Kv=0
, the first two rows collapse.

Not all extensions
L/K
of valued fields are tame, or, more generally, satisfy
[L:K]=ef
.

Example 2.23
.

Consider
K=\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)^{1/p^{\infty}}=\mathbb{F}_{p}(t,\ldots,t^{1/p^{n}},\ldots)
with the
t
-adic valuation and
L=K(\alpha)
with
\alpha^{p}-\alpha-t^{-1}=0
. Then
L/K
is an immediate Artin-Schreier extension of degree
p
, so
[L:K]>ef=1
.

Ramification theory gives rise to yet another multiplicative invariant called the
defect
, which measures the failure of the fundamental inequality. It does not prominently appear in local class field theory, because separable extensions of discretely valued fields always satisfy the fundamental equality
[L:K]=ef
, cf.
[
42
, Chap. II, (6.8)]
.

Corollary/Definition 2.24

(Lemma of Ostrowski)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field and
L/K
a finite extension such that
v
has a unique prolongation
w
to
L
. Then

d(w/v)\coloneqq\frac{[L:K]}{e(w/v)f(w/v)}

is a power of
p
, whenever
\charK Kv=p>0
, and equal to 1 if
\charK Kv=0
. The invariant
d(w/v)
is called the
defect
of
L/K
.
The extension is called
defectless
if
d(w/v)=1
.

One proves the Lemma of Ostrowski by passing to a splitting field and applying the following consequence of the Conjugacy Theorem
2.14
and ramification theory:

Corollary 2.25

(Fundamental equality)
.

Let
(L,w)/(K,v)
be a finite Galois extension of valued fields. Let
r
be the number of prolongations of
v
to
L
(all of which are conjugate).
Then

[L:K]=refd,

where
e,f,d
are the ramification invariants of
(L,w)/(L^{D},w|_{L^{D}})
.

As our main concern is with absolute Galois groups, we now consider the case
L=K^{\sepc}
.

Definition 2.26
.

For a fixed prolongation
\tilde{v}
of
v
to
K^{\sepc}
, write

\displaystyle D_{K}

\displaystyle\coloneqq D_{\tilde{v}}

\displaystyle(K^{\sepc}/K)

\displaystyle I_{K}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\,I_{\tilde{v}}

\displaystyle(K^{\sepc}/K)

\displaystyle R_{K}

\displaystyle\coloneqq R_{\tilde{v}}

\displaystyle(K^{\sepc}/K)

to denote the
absolute
decomposition, inertia, and ramification groups, respectively. Furthermore, we say that
(K,v)
is

(1)

henselian
if
D_{K}=G_{K}
(equivalently,
K=L^{D}
);

(2)

algebraically maximal
if every finite immediate extension is trivial;

(3)

henselian defectless
if it is henselian and any finite extension is defectless;

(4)

separably tame
if
K
is henselian and
R_{K}=1
(equivalently, any finite separable extension is tame);

(5)

tame
if
K
is perfect, henselian, and
R_{K}=1
(equivalently, any finite extension is tame);

(6)

absolutely unramified
3
3

3

It is customary to use the term
unramified
to denote a henselian valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
with
vp
minimal positive; hence we avoid this terminology here.
if
K
is perfect, henselian, and
I_{K}=1
(equivalently, any finite extension is unramified).

In the absolute case, we have the following splitting result (cf.
[
17
, Cor. 22.1.2, Prop. 22.1.3]
):

Theorem 2.27
.

Let
L=K^{\sepc}
. There is a semi-direct product decomposition

D_{K}/R_{K}\cong\Hom(wL/vK,\mu_{Lw})\rtimes G_{Kv},

where
G_{Kv}
acts naturally on the left. Moreover,

\Hom(wL/vK,\mu_{Lw})\cong\prod_{q\neq p}\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{r_{q}},

where
q
runs over primes away from
p=\charK Kv
, and

r_{q}\coloneqq\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(vK/qvK)

denotes the
q
-rank
of vK.

We can use the above results to calculate concrete absolute Galois groups.

Example 2.28
.

(1)

Consider
K=\mathbb{C}(\!(t)\!)
with the
t
-adic valuation. It has value group
\mathbb{Z}
and residue field
\mathbb{C}
. As a complete field of rank 1,
\mathbb{C}(\!(t)\!)
is henselian (see Section
2.2.2
).
By Fact
2.22
2
,
R_{\mathbb{C}(\!(t)\!)}=1
. Moreover,
\Aut(\mathbb{C}^{\sepc}/\mathbb{C})=1
.
Therefore, combining the First (
2.3
) and Second Exact Sequences (
2.4
) with Theorem
2.27
, we obtain

G_{\mathbb{C}(\!(t)\!)}=D_{K}\cong I_{K}\cong\prod_{q}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\cong\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}.\\

Note that any finite field has absolute Galois group isomorphic to
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\cong G_{\mathbb{C}(\!(t)\!)}
!

(2)

Let
k
be an arbitrary field of characteristic 0. Consider the field of Puiseux series

K=\bigcup_{n>0}k(\!(t^{1/n})\!)

with the
t
-adic valuation. The value group is
\mathbb{Q}
and the residue field is
k
. The valuation on
K
is again henselian. Here, the First and Second Exact Sequences yield
I_{K}=1
and therefore

G_{K}=D_{K}\cong\Aut(k^{\sepc}/k)=G_{k}.

Proposition 2.29
.

Fix rational primes
p\neq q
. Then any Sylow
q
-subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

Proof.

Let
G_{q}
be a Sylow
q
-subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, and denote by
(F_{q},w_{q})
the fixed field of
G_{q}
with prolongation
w_{q}
of
v_{p}
. As
G_{q}
is a pro-
q
group, we must have
R_{F_{q}}=1
by Fact
2.22
2
. Moreover,
w_{q}F_{q}=\mathbb{Z}_{(q)}
and
G_{F_{q}w_{q}}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}
by infinite Galois theory. Therefore, Theorem
2.27
yields

G_{q}\cong G_{q}/R_{F_{q}}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}.\qed

Remark 2.30
.

The group action in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is given by
\phi_{\tau}(\sigma)=\sigma^{p}
, where
\tau
is the Frobenius element topologically generating the right copy of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. This is the natural action given by Theorem
2.27
, cf. also
[
17
, Ex. 22.1.4]
. In particular,
\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is non-abelian.

The structure of Sylow
p
-subgroups of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is also known, though somewhat more complicated. It was determined by Labute
[
40
]
, building on earlier work of Shafarevich
[
53
]
, Demushkin
[
14
]
, and Serre
[
50
]
.

2.2.2.
Henselianity

We have already introduced the single most important notion in valuation theory in passing: a valued field
(K,v)
is called
henselian
if
D_{K}=G_{K}
. Equivalently,
(K,v)
is henselian if and only if
v
extends uniquely to any fixed algebraic closure
K^{\alg}
of
K
(recall that by Chevalley’s Theorem
2.9
, there exists at least one such extension, and valuations extend uniquely to inseparable extensions, see Fact
2.10
). We point the reader to
[
20
, Sec. 4.1]
.

It is possible to construct a henselian valued field from any given one in a universal fashion:

Definition 2.31
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field. Fix a separable closure
K^{\sepc}
of
K
and a prolongation
\tilde{v}
of
v
to
K^{\sepc}
. We define
K^{h}\coloneqq(K^{\sepc})^{D}
to be the fixed field of
K^{\sepc}
with respect to
D=D_{\tilde{v}}(K^{\sepc}/K)
and call it a
henselisation
of
K
.

By construction,
K^{h}
is henselian.
A henselisation is only unique up to conjugation by an automorphism
\sigma\in G_{K}
—nonetheless, one often speaks of “the” henselisation of
K
. The extension
K^{h}/K
is separable and immediate (Fact
2.20
1
), and further satisfies the universal property that for any henselian valued field
(L,w)
extending
(K,v)
, there exists a factorisation
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists}

of valued fields.
The terminology arises from the fact that henselian valued fields are precisely those valued fields that satisfy
Hensel’s Lemma
, which relates the roots of a polynomial
f(X)\in\mathcal{O}_{v}[X]
with the roots of its coefficient-wise reduction
\overline{f}(X)\in(Kv)[X]
. Hensel originally proved his lemma for
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

Lemma 2.32

(Hensel’s Lemma)
.

A valued field
(K,v)
is henselian if and only if it satisfies the following property: given a monic polynomial
f(X)\in\mathcal{O}_{v}[X]
and
a\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
satisfying

v(f(a))>2v(f^{\prime}(a)),

then
f(X)
has a unique root
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
with
v(\alpha-a)>v(f^{\prime}(a))
. In particular, if
f(X)
is such that the reduced polynomial
\overline{f}(X)\in(Kv)[X]
has a simple root
\overline{a}\in Kv
, then
f(X)
has a unique root
\alpha\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
lifting
\overline{a}
.

The reasons why henselianity is a central notion are manifold. It plays a role analogous to that of completeness in algebraic number theory: there, instead of the henselisation, one would consider the completion of a global field with respect to a non-archimedean absolute value. Indeed, for all valued fields of rank 1, completeness implies henselianity. Moreover, one may develop ramification theory in an
absolute
sense: working with a henselian valued field
(K,v)
and a fixed Galois extension
L/K
, the invariants and diagrams of Section
2.2
do not depend on the choice of a valuation
w
on
L
.
Neukirch’s classical text
[
43
]
adopts this perspective, formulating ramification theory not just for local fields but more generally for henselian valued fields of rank 1.

Another avatar of henselianity is the following equivalent condition, which links the properties of distance (in the sense of the valuation topology) and algebraicity. It is remarkably useful in practice.

Lemma 2.33

(Krasner’s Lemma)
.

A valued field
(K,v)
is henselian if and only if it satisfies the following property: given a separable element
x\in K^{\sepc}
with conjugates
x=x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
and any algebraic element
y\in K^{\alg}
, the condition

v(y-x)>\max_{2\leq i\leq n}v(x_{i}-x),

implies
K(x)\subseteq K(y)
.

Perhaps the most far-reaching reason for the importance of henselian valued fields is the fact that henselianity is a
first-order
property (it can be expressed as an axiom scheme via Hensel’s Lemma
2.32
). This allows the class of henselian valued fields (structures) to be studied via model theory. In contrast, completeness is not a first-order property (as it entails a cardinality bound).
In model-theoretic practice, henselianity is used—among other things—to lift residue field embeddings to valued field embeddings, which has model-theoretic consequences through methods pioneered by A. Robinson. One key formative result in this direction is the Ax-Kochen-Ershov Theorem
4.1
, which we will mention at a later stage.

We conclude with two applications of Krasner’s Lemma.

Proposition 2.34
.

Let
(K,v)
be a henselian valued field of rank 1. Then
G_{\widehat{K}}\cong G_{\vphantom{\widehat{K}}K}
.

Proof.

Note that
\widehat{K}
is henselian as a complete rank 1 valued field.
By density of
K
in
\widehat{K}
and Krasner’s Lemma
2.33
,
K^{\sepc}\cap\widehat{K}=K
; hence
K^{\sepc}
and
\widehat{K}
are linearly disjoint. Therefore, we obtain an injective map between sets of finite Galois extensions over
K
and
\widehat{K}
,

(2.5)

\{\text{$E/K$ fin. Galois ext.}\}\longrightarrow\{\text{$F/\widehat{K}$ fin. Galois ext.}\},\quad E\longmapsto E\widehat{K},

inducing isomorphisms
\Gal(E/K)\cong\Gal(E\widehat{K}/\widehat{K})
.

If we can show that (
2.5
) is surjective, then passing to inverse limits yields

G_{K}\cong\varprojlim_{\text{$E/K$ fin.\,Gal.}}\Aut(E/K)\cong\varprojlim_{\text{$F/\widehat{K}$ fin.\,Gal.}}\Aut(F/\widehat{K})\cong G_{\widehat{K}}.

So let
\widehat{K}(\alpha)/\widehat{K}
be a finite Galois extension, and let
f(X)\in\widehat{K}[X]
be the minimal polynomial of
\alpha
. By continuity of roots (Fact
2.15
), we may find a monic polynomial
g(X)\in K[X]
of the same degree sufficiently close to
f(X)
, such that
g(X)
has a unique root
\beta
satisfying

v(\beta-\alpha)>\max\{v(\sigma(\alpha)-\alpha):\sigma\in\Gal(F/\widehat{K})\}.

By Krasner’s lemma once again, this implies that
F=\widehat{K}(\beta)
is generated by an element algebraic over
K
. In particular, we map
K(\beta)
to
F=K(\beta)\widehat{K}
.
∎

Corollary 2.35

(“Fundamental Theorem” of non-archimedean analysis)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a henselian valued field of rank 1 with
\charK K=0
. Then the completion of the algebraic closure of
K
is algebraically closed.

2.2.3.
p
-henselianity

An important weakening of henselianity in the context of pro-
p
groups is the notion of
p
-henselianity
.

Definition 2.36
.

A valued field
(K,v)
is called
p
-henselian
if
v
extends uniquely to any finite Galois extension of
p
-power degree, or equivalently, to the maximal pro-
p
Galois extension of
K
, which we denote by
K(p)
.

Hensel’s Lemma still holds with the same statement as in Lemma
2.32
for polynomials
f(X)\in\mathcal{O}_{v}[X]
that split in
K(p)
.
The notion of
p
-henselianity admits the following useful characterisation:

Lemma 2.37

(
[
20
, Thm. 4.2.2]
)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field. Then
v
is
p
-henselian if and only if
v
extends uniquely to any Galois extension of degree
p
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let
F/K
be a finite Galois extension of
p
-power degree, and assume that
v
extends uniquely to any degree-
p
Galois extension of
K
. By the Conjugacy Theorem
2.14
,
G=\Gal(F/K)
acts transitively on the set of valuation rings
\{\mathcal{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{r}\}
of
F
extending
\mathcal{O}_{v}
. If
r>1
, then
D=D_{\mathcal{O}_{1}}(F/K)\lneq G
, and we may choose a maximal proper subgroup
D\leq N<G
. For any finite
p
-group, the maximal proper subgroups are precisely the normal subgroups of index
p
, see
[
47
, Thm. 4.6(ii)]
. So
N\vartriangleleft G
is of index
p
. By the Galois correspondence,
F^{N}/K
is a Galois extension of degree
p
, so
\mathcal{O}_{v}
extends uniquely to
F^{N}
by assumption. Thus,
N=\Gal(F/F^{N})
acts transitively on
\{\mathcal{O}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{r}\}
as well. But now
D
is the stabiliser of
\mathcal{O}_{1}
with respect to the action of either
G
or
N
, so

|G|=r\cdot|D|=|N|,

by the Orbit-Stabiliser Theorem, which is a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 2.38
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field. If
G_{K}
is a pro-
p
group, then
v
is henselian if and only if
v
extends uniquely to any
C_{p}
-extension.

Corollary 2.39

(
[
20
, Cor. 4.2.4]
)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field such that
\charK(Kv)\neq p
and
\zeta_{p}\in K
. Then
v
is
p
-henselian if and only if
1+\mathcal{M}_{v}\subseteq K^{\times p}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
. Fix
a\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
and consider the polynomial
f(X)=X^{p}-(1+a)\in\mathcal{O}_{v}[X]
, which splits in
K(p)
. By the
p
-analogue of Hensel’s Lemma, it has a root in
K^{\times}
.

\Leftarrow
. By Lemma
2.37
and Kummer theory, it suffices to show that
v
extends uniquely to
K(x^{1/p})
for any fixed
x\in K^{\times}\setminus K^{\times p}
. If
vx\notin pvK
, then

(2.6)

w\left(a_{0}+a_{1}x^{1/p}+\ldots+a_{p-1}x^{(p-1)/p}\right)=\min_{0\leq i\leq p-1}w\bigl(a_{i}x^{i/p}\bigr)\quad\text{for all $a_{0},\ldots,a_{p-1}\in K$},

holds for any extension
w
of
v
and thus determines it uniquely.
If
vx\in pvK
, we may assume without loss of generality that
vx=0
. We must have
\overline{x}\notin(Kv)^{\times p}
, for otherwise,
1+\mathcal{M}_{v}\subseteq K^{\times p}
would imply
x\in K^{\times p}
. But then again, the identity (
2.6
) will hold because
\bigl\{1,\overline{x}^{1/p},\ldots,\overline{x}^{(p-1)/p}\bigr\}
is linearly independent over
Kv
. In this case,
v
also extends uniquely.
∎

2.3.
Coarsenings and decomposition

Fix a field
K
. One may endow
K
with several different valuations (equivalently, valuation rings). The following relations are of particular significance.

Definition 2.40
.

Let
v,w
be valuations on a field
K
. Then
w
is a
coarsening
of
v
(and
v
is a
refinement
of
w
) if
\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{w}
. Moreover, we say
v
and
w
are
dependent
if
\mathcal{O}_{v}
and
\mathcal{O}_{w}
have a non-trivial common coarsening, i.e., the compositum
\mathcal{O}_{v}\mathcal{O}_{w}
is not equal to
K
. Otherwise, we say
v
and
w
are
independent
.

The following two facts provide a first indication of the usefulness of these notions. Note that Fact
2.42
is a partial strengthening of the Weak Approximation Theorem (Fact
2.13
).

Fact 2.41

(
[
20
, Sec. 2.3]
)
.

Let
v,w
be valuations on a field
K
. Then
v
and
w
induce the same topology on
K
if and only if
\mathcal{O}_{v}
and
\mathcal{O}_{w}
are dependent.

Fact 2.42

(Approximation Theorem,
[
20
, Thm. 2.4.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{v_{n}}
be pairwise independent, non-trivial valuation rings on
K
. Given tuples
(x_{i})\in K^{n}
and
(\gamma_{i})\in\prod_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}K
, there exists
x\in K
such that

v_{i}(x-x_{i})>\gamma_{i}\quad\text{for $i=1,\ldots,n$.}

The full strength of coarsenings only becomes apparent when we shift our focus back to the valuation and place formalism.

Lemma 2.43
.

Let
v,w
be valuations on a field
K
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{w}

(2)

\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{w}^{\times}

(3)

\mathcal{M}_{w}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{v}
[sic]

(4)

there exists a convex subgroup
\Delta\leq\Gamma_{v}
such that
w
is given by the composition
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{v}/\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{w},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

and
v
induces a valuation
\overline{v}
on
Kw
with
\overline{v}(Kw)=\Delta
and
(Kw)\overline{v}=Kv
.

Proof (sketch).

Note that

K=\mathcal{O}_{v}\sqcup\mathcal{M}_{v}^{-1}=\mathcal{O}_{w}\sqcup\mathcal{M}_{w}^{-1}

can be written as the disjoint union of elements of valuation
\geq 0
and
<0
. Taking complements, this implies the—at first sight counterintuitive—inclusion
\mathcal{M}_{w}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{v}
.

Given a convex subgroup
\Delta\leq\Gamma_{v}
, the composition of
v
with the natural quotient map
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w:K^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{v}/\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

is a valuation with valuation ring

\mathcal{O}_{w}=\{x\in K:w(x)\geq 0\}\supseteq\{x\in K:v(x)\geq 0\}=\mathcal{O}_{v}.

Conversely, given a coarsening
\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{w}
, we obtain a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{w}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\cong
\cong

where the dashed arrow
q^{\prime}
is a quotient map induced by
q
and the two isomorphisms. Unravelling definitions, one sees that
\Delta\coloneqq\ker q^{\prime}=v(\mathcal{O}_{w}^{\times})
and
w=q^{\prime}\circ v
. Furthermore, note that
v
restricts to a homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{w}^{\times}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\Delta
, which induces a well-defined valuation
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v}:(Kw)^{\times}=\mathcal{O}_{w}^{\times}/\mathcal{M}_{w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta,}

where
\mathcal{M}_{w}=\{x\in K:v(x)>\Delta\}
is the coset of value
\infty
with respect to
\overline{v}
.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v}}=\overline{\mathcal{O}_{v}}=\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{w}\subseteq Kw=\mathcal{O}_{w}/\mathcal{M}_{w}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\overline{v}}=\overline{\mathcal{M}_{v}}=\mathcal{M}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{w},

and consequently,
(Kw)\overline{v}=Kv
.
∎

2.3.1.
Composition of places

The second—and perhaps most practical—description of coarsenings is given by the formalism of places. Indeed, for a valued field
(K,v)
with a coarsening
\mathcal{O}_{w}\supseteq\mathcal{O}_{v}
, one can separate the structural information encoded in
v
into two parts using the coarsening
\mathcal{O}_{w}
. In the mixed characteristic case, there are two canonical choices of coarsenings, leading to the notion of
Standard Decomposition
that we will introduce in Section
2.3.2
.

Let us now explain in more detail what is meant by “structural information” and how decompositions are obtained.

As seen above, the valuation
w
can be written as a composition
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w:K^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{v}/\Delta\cong\Gamma_{w},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

where
\Delta=v(\mathcal{O}_{w}^{\times})
is a convex subgroup of
\Gamma_{v}
, and
v
induces a valuation
\overline{v}
on the residue field
Kw
. How can we express this setup in terms of places—namely, what is the relationship between
\varphi_{v}
,
\varphi_{w}
, and
\varphi_{\overline{v}}
, the places associated to
v
,
w
, and
\overline{v}
, respectively?

Let
a\in K
. By construction, these places act on
a
according to the following rules:

a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{w}}}{{\longmapsto}}\begin{cases}\text{if $a\in\mathcal{O}_{w}:$}&a\mathcal{M}_{w}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{\overline{v}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\begin{cases}a\mathcal{M}_{v}&\text{if $a\in\mathcal{O}_{v}$}\\
\infty&\text{if $a\notin\mathcal{O}_{v}$}\end{cases}\\
\text{if $a\notin\mathcal{O}_{w}:$}&\infty\end{cases}

Therefore,
\varphi_{v}
is—as a matter of fact—the composition of
\varphi_{w}
and
\varphi_{\overline{v}}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kw\cup\{\infty\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv\cup\{\infty\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\overline{v}}}

(Note that here we extend
\varphi_{\overline{v}}
by the additional assignment
\infty\longmapsto\infty
.)

Conversely, the composition of two places
\varphi_{w}:K\longrightarrow Kw\cup\{\infty\}
and
\psi:Kw\longrightarrow k\cup\{\infty\}
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kw\cup\{\infty\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\cup\{\infty\},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

gives rise to a coarsening
\mathcal{O}_{w}=\varphi_{w}^{-1}(Kw)
of the valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}\coloneqq\varphi_{w}^{-1}(\psi^{-1}(k))
. Again, these two constructions are mutually inverse (subject to the same proviso that equivalent places are identified; see the end of Section
2.1.3
).

Slogan
.

Any coarsening can be decomposed as a composition of places.

We note that the discussions of Sections
2.1.2
,
2.1.3
,
2.3
, and
2.3.1
can be succinctly summarised in categorical terms:

Theorem 2.44
.

Let
K
be a fixed field. The following thin categories are equivalent:

(1)

the poset category of valuation rings
\mathcal{O}
on
K
ordered by inclusion
\subseteq
;

(2)

objects are valuations
v
on
K
; morphisms between
v
and
v^{\prime}
are pairs
(q,\overline{v})
, such that
q
is a factoring of
v
through the value group of
v
and
\overline{v}
is the induced valuation on
Kv^{\prime}
;

(3)

objects are places
\varphi
on
K
; morphisms between
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are factorings
\psi
of
\varphi
through the residue field of
\varphi^{\prime}
.

In symbols (omitting
\infty
for readability):

\{\mathcal{O}\mid\;\subseteq\}\simeq\left\{\smash[b]{\begin{tabular}[ht]{@{}c@{}}$v:K\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma$\\
valuations\end{tabular}}\ \middle|\ \phantom{\varphi^{\prime}:\;}\hbox to104.85pt{\vbox to32.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 52.42374pt\lower-16.22331pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right\}

Once and for all, we remark that, by abuse of notation, we will use valuation rings, valuations, and places interchangeably whenever there is no danger of confusion.
As a final remark, we note that one of the key properties of decompositions is that they preserve and reflect henselianity.

Fact 2.45

(
[
20
, Cor. 4.1.4]
)
.

Let
v
be a valuation that decomposes as
v=\overline{v}\circ w
. Then
v
is henselian if and only if
\overline{v}
and
w
are henselian.

2.3.2.
Standard Decomposition

For any parameter in the maximal ideal of a valued field, there are two canonical coarsenings. Our description will be place-theoretic. The main theorem is:

Theorem 2.46

(Standard Decomposition at
\varpi
)
.

Let
(K,v)
be any valued field and
\varpi\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
a fixed non-zero parameter. Then
v
can be written as the composition of three places,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v:K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{0}\cup\{\infty\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{\varpi}\cup\{\infty\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv\cup\{\infty\},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v_{\varpi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}

where

(1)

v_{0}
is the finest coarsening such that
v_{0}\varpi=0
;

(2)

v_{\varpi}
is the coarsest coarsening such that
v_{\varpi}\varpi>0
;

(3)

\overline{v}
is the valuation induced by
v
on
Kv_{0}
;

(4)

\overline{v_{\varpi}}
is the valuation induced by
v_{\varpi}
on
Kv_{0}
;

(5)

\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}
is the valuation induced by
v
on
Kv_{\varpi}
.

Furthermore, these valuations satisfy the following properties:

(1)

The valuations
\overline{v}
and
\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}
have value groups

\Gamma_{0}\coloneqq\Gamma^{+}_{v{\varpi}}=\Conv(v{\varpi})\Gamma_{\varpi}\coloneqq\Gamma^{-}_{v{\varpi}}=\{\gamma\in vK:|\gamma|\ll v{\varpi}\},

respectively, and moreover,
\overline{v_{\varpi}}
is of rank 1 and
\overline{v}=\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\circ\overline{v_{\varpi}}
.

(2)

The valuation ring of
v_{0}
is given by
\mathcal{O}_{v_{0}}=\mathcal{O}_{v}[\varpi^{-1}]
.

(3)

The valuation rings of
\overline{v}
and
\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}
are given by

\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v}}=\mathcal{O}_{v}\mbox{\larger\big/}\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\varpi^{n}\mathcal{O}_{v}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}=\mathcal{O}_{v}/\rad(\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v})\cong(\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v})_{\Red},

where
\rad(\cdot)
denotes the radical of an ideal and
(\cdot)_{\Red}
the reduction of a ring.

(4)

The valuation ring of
v_{\varpi}
is given by
\mathcal{O}_{v_{\varpi}}=\{x\in K:x^{n}\varpi\in\mathcal{O}_{v}\text{ for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$}\}
.

(5)

If
\mathbb{Z}v\varpi
is cofinal in the value group of
v
, then
v_{0}
is the trivial valuation.

(6)

If
\overline{v}
is of rank 1, then
\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}
is trivial.

Proof.

We begin by choosing two convex subgroups
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{\varpi}
of
\Gamma=vK
. Let
\Gamma_{0}\coloneqq\Gamma_{\varpi}^{+}
be the convex hull of
\mathbb{Z}v\varpi
and
\Gamma_{\varpi}\coloneqq\Gamma_{\varpi}^{-}
the maximal convex subgroup not containing
v\varpi
. In particular, we have
\Gamma_{\varpi}\subsetneq\Gamma_{0}
. Coarsening by these convex subgroups, we obtain valuations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}:K^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma/\Gamma_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\varpi}:K^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma/\Gamma_{\varpi}.}

The operation of coarsening by convex subgroups preserves inclusion, so
v_{0}
and
v_{\varpi}
are as in (a) and (b).
By our discussion in Section
2.3.1
, the valuation
v
can be decomposed as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{\varpi}\cup\{\infty\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv\cup\{\infty\}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\varpi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}

Since
v_{0}
is a coarsening of
v_{\varpi}
, we can decompose
v_{\varpi}
further, which yields the decomposition into three places as stated in the theorem.
The value group of
v_{\varpi}
is
\Gamma/\Gamma_{\varpi}
. The value group of the induced valuation
\overline{v_{\varpi}}
must consequently be
\Gamma_{0}/\Gamma_{\varpi}
, which is of rank 1 by Fact
2.4
.

Consider the three maximal ideals
\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{v_{\varpi}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{v}
of
\mathcal{O}_{v_{0}}\supseteq\mathcal{O}_{v_{\varpi}}\supseteq\mathcal{O}_{v}
.
4
4

4

Recall that passing from valuation rings to their maximal ideals reverses inclusion.
The place
\overline{v}
is given by

x\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}\longmapsto\begin{cases}x\mathcal{M}_{v}&\text{if $x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}$}\\
\infty&\text{if $x\notin\mathcal{O}_{v}$},\end{cases}

whereas
\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\circ\overline{v_{\varpi}}
is the assignment

x\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}\longmapsto\begin{cases}\text{if $x\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{\varpi}}:$}&x\mathcal{M}_{v_{\varpi}}\longmapsto\begin{cases}x\mathcal{M}_{v}&\text{if $x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}$}\\
\infty&\text{if $x\notin\mathcal{O}_{v}$}\end{cases}\\
\text{if $x\notin\mathcal{O}_{v_{\varpi}}:$}&\infty\longmapsto\infty.\end{cases}

Clearly, these two maps are the same, and hence
1
follows. By definition,
\mathcal{O}_{v_{0}}
must be the finest coarsening for which
\varpi
becomes invertible—this proves
2
. Further note that
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v}}=\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}=\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{v_{\varpi}}
, where the ideals involved are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}=\{x\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{0}}:v_{0}(x)>0\}=\{x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}:v(x)>\mathbb{Z}v\varpi\}=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\varpi^{n}\mathcal{O}_{v}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{v_{\varpi}}=\{x\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{\varpi}}:v_{\varpi}(x)>0\}=\{x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}:v(x)>\Gamma_{\varpi}\}=\rad(\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}).

This yields
3
and
4
.

If
\mathbb{Z}v\varpi
is cofinal in
\Gamma
, then
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}
and
v_{0}
is trivial. If
\Gamma_{0}
has rank 1, then
\Gamma_{\varpi}=\{0\}
and therefore
v=v_{\varpi}
, so the induced valuation
\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}
must be trivial. This proves
5
and
6
.
∎

To simplify notation even further, we will from now on omit the symbol “
\infty
” when working with places.

Remark 2.47

(Standard Decomposition for mixed characteristic)
.

For valued fields of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
, the key distinguished example is the
Standard Decomposition at
\varpi=p
.
In this case, the decomposition can be written as follows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0{,}0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma/\Gamma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma/\Gamma_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0{,}\,p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{0}/\Gamma_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p{,}\,p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{p}}

Each arrow is labelled with the value group of the corresponding valuation. The three middle arrows are additionally labelled with the characteristic of the valuations.

The valued field
(Kv_{0},\overline{v})
is often called the
core field
of
(K,v)
.
Note that here,

(1)

v_{0}
is the finest coarsening with residue characteristic 0, and

(2)

v_{p}
is the coarsest coarsening with residue characteristic
p
.

What is distinguishing about the parameter
\varpi=p
is that it isolates the place at which a change in characteristic occurs for valuations of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
.

Slogan
.

The
(0, ​0)
and
(p,p)
-equicharacteristic places in the Standard Decomposition are chosen maximally possible, thus creating a rank 1 place in the middle.

Fortunately, the above notation is consistent with writing
v_{p}
for the
p
-adic valuation on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

2.3.3.
Canonical henselian valuation

Fix a field
K
that is not separably closed.
We will introduce the tree structure of the set of henselian valuations on
K
and determine a distinguished element
v_{K}
in it, which we call the
canonical henselian valuation
.
We will show that the canonical henselian valuation admits a “going-down” statement (Theorem
2.54
). More specifically, for the proof of the Main Theorem,
v_{K}
will be the source of a non-trivial valuation that gives
K
the structure of a
p
-adically closed field.

Everything that follows builds on F. K. Schmidt’s Theorem, initially proven for complete rank 1 valued fields, but easily adapted to the henselian setting. Recall that two valuations
v
and
w
are
independent
if any common coarsening is trivial, i.e.,
\mathcal{O}_{v}\mathcal{O}_{w}=K
(equivalently: they induce different topologies on
K
).

Theorem 2.48

(F. K. Schmidt)
.

If
K
admits two independent and non-trivial henselian valuations, then
K
must be separably closed.

Proof.

Let
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
be independent non-trivial henselian valuations and assume
K\neq K^{\sepc}
.
Furthermore, let
f(X)\in K[X]
be a separable irreducible polynomial and
g(X)\in K[X]
any polynomial of the same degree
n>1
. If
g(X)
is sufficiently close to
f(X)
in each coefficient (with respect to a henselian valuation), then by continuity of roots (Fact
2.15
), Remark
2.16
, and Krasner’s Lemma
2.33
,
f(X)
and
g(X)
have the same splitting field. Now let
h(X)
be any polynomial with precisely
n
distinct roots in
K
(i.e., a product of linear factors). By the Approximation Theorem (Fact
2.42
), we may choose
g(X)
sufficiently close to
f(X)
with respect to
v_{1}
and, at the same time, sufficiently close to
h(X)
with respect to
v_{2}
, so that
f(X)
and
h(X)
have the same splitting field. This is a contradiction.
∎

Example 2.49
.

Let
\mathbb{C}_{p}
be the completion of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\alg}
. By Fact
2.35
,
\mathbb{C}_{p}
is algebraically closed. If
q\neq p
is another prime, then there exists a non-canonical field isomorphism
\mathbb{C}_{p}\cong\mathbb{C}_{q}
between algebraically closed fields of characteristic 0 and of size continuum (we forget the topology). In this way,
\mathbb{C}_{p}
can be endowed with a natural
p
-adic topology and an abstract
q
-adic topology induced by the non-canonical isomorphism. The point of F. K. Schmidt’s Theorem is that such an anomaly can only occur if
K
is separably closed.

Definition 2.50
.

Let
K\neq K^{\sepc}
be a field. We partition the set of henselian valuation rings on
K
into two sets

\displaystyle H_{1}(K)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\mathcal{O}_{v}:\text{$\mathcal{O}_{v}$ henselian with $Kv$ \lx@text@underline{not} separably closed}\}

\displaystyle H_{2}(K)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\mathcal{O}_{v}:\text{$\mathcal{O}_{v}$ henselian with $Kv$ separably closed}\}.

Lemma 2.51

(Tree structure)
.

The coarsening relation induces the following structure:

(1)

H_{1}(K)
is non-empty (it contains the trivial valuation) and linearly ordered by
\subseteq
.

(2)

Any valuation in
H_{1}(K)
is coarser than any valuation in
H_{2}(K)
.

(3)

Any non-empty subset of
H_{2}(K)
has a supremum (finest common coarsening) in
H_{2}(K)
.

(4)

If
H_{2}(K)=\emptyset
, then
H_{1}(K)
has a minimum (a finest coarsening).

In short, we get the following picture:

v_{K}
H_{1}(K)
H_{2}(K)
coarser
finer

Proof (sketch).

All valuations we consider will be henselian by virtue of Fact
2.45
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{w}=\mathcal{O}_{u}\mathcal{O}_{v}
be the finest common coarsening of two incomparable henselian valuation rings
\mathcal{O}_{u}
and
\mathcal{O}_{v}
on
K
. The proof boils down to the following observation: the induced valuations
\overline{u}
and
\overline{v}
on
Kw
are—by design—non-trivial independent henselian valuations. By F. K. Schmidt’s Theorem
2.48
, this implies that
Kw
is separably closed, which in turn implies that
Ku=(Kw)\overline{u}
and
Kv=(Kw)\overline{v}
are separably closed as well. This proves (i), (ii), and the fact that any finite subset in
H_{2}(K)
has a supremum in
H_{2}(K)
. One can further show that for any non-empty subset
S\subseteq H_{2}(K)
, its supremum
\mathcal{O}_{S}
(i.e., the ring generated by all
\mathcal{O}\in S
) is given by

(2.7)

\mathcal{O}_{S}=\left\{x_{1}\cdots x_{n}:x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\bigcup_{\mathcal{O}\in S}\mathcal{O},\,n\in\mathbb{N}\right\},

and that it lies in
H_{2}(K)
(cf.
[
20
, Thm. 4.4.2]
). Likewise, one can see that

\mathcal{O}_{1}\coloneqq\bigcap_{\mathcal{O}\in H_{1}(K)}\mathcal{O}

is a valuation ring and the infimum of
H_{1}(K)
.
∎

Example 2.52
.

The canonical henselian valuation of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is the
p
-adic valuation
v_{p}
. The only other henselian valuation on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is the trivial one.

Proof.

As
\mathbb{Q}_{p}v_{p}=\mathbb{F}_{p}
is not separably closed,
\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}
is comparable to all other henselian valuation rings. It does not admit any proper refinement, since such a refinement would induce a non-trivial valuation on
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, which does not exist. Similarly, the value group of any coarsening is a quotient of
\mathbb{Z}
by a convex subgroup. The only non-trivial convex subgroup is
\mathbb{Z}
itself—the corresponding coarsening is the trivial valuation.
∎

Definition 2.53

(Canonical henselian valuation)
.

Let
K\neq K^{\sepc}
be a field. The
canonical henselian valuation

v_{K}
(with valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v_{K}}
) is defined to be

(1)

the coarsest valuation in
H_{2}(K)
if
H_{2}(K)\neq\emptyset
, and

(2)

the finest valuation in
H_{1}(K)
otherwise.

If
H_{2}(K)\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{O}_{v_{K}}
does not lie in
H_{1}(K)
. We further define:

H(K)\coloneqq H_{1}(K)\cup\{\mathcal{O}_{v_{K}}\}

Note that any valuation in
H(K)
is comparable to any other henselian valuation and that
\mathcal{O}_{v_{K}}
is the minimum of
H(K)
.

2.3.4.
“Henseling Down”

By definition, henselianity “goes up”—any algebraic extension of a henselian valued field is henselian. The following partial converse was established only in 2003.

Theorem 2.54

(Henseling Down,
[
32
, Prop. 3.1]
)
.

Let
L/K
be an algebraic extension with
L
not separably closed. Consider a valuation
w
on
L
with
\mathcal{O}_{w}\in H(L)
.
Then, the valuation ring of the restriction
v\coloneqq w|_{K}
lies in
H(K)
in each of the following cases:

(1)

L/K
is normal.

(2)

L/K
is finite.

(3)

G_{L}
is a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G_{K}
for some prime
p
. If
p=2
, additionally assume that no proper coarsening of
w
has real closed residue field.

Proof.

(i) We need to establish two properties: first, that
v
is henselian, and second, that it is a coarsening of
v_{K}
. To prove that
v
is henselian, it suffices to show that
w
is the unique extension of
v
to
L
. So let
w^{\prime}
be any prolongation of
v
to
L
. By the Conjugacy Theorem
2.14
,
w^{\prime}
is henselian. Since
\mathcal{O}_{w}\in H(L)
, it follows that
\mathcal{O}_{w}
and
\mathcal{O}_{w^{\prime}}
are comparable. Hence, by Fact
2.12
,
\mathcal{O}_{w}=\mathcal{O}_{w^{\prime}}
.

For the second part, note that if
\mathcal{O}_{w}\in H_{1}(L)
, then
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in H_{1}(K)
by the definition of
H_{1}(K)
. Thus, it remains to consider the case
w=v_{L}
. All proper coarsenings of
v_{L}
lie in
H_{1}(L)
, and hence their restrictions to
K
(and therefore, all proper coarsenings of
v
) lie in
H_{1}(K)
. This implies that
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in H(K)
for
w=v_{L}
.

(ii) Essentially, the strategy is to pass to the splitting field of
L/K
and apply (i). Let
N
be the splitting field of
L/K
. Then
w
has a unique henselian prolongation
w^{\prime}
to
N
. If
\mathcal{O}_{w^{\prime}}\in H(N)
, the claim follows directly from (i). Otherwise,
v_{N}
is a proper coarsening of
w^{\prime}
, and both
Nv_{N}
and
Nw^{\prime}
are separably closed. Hence, by Fact
2.12
,
v_{N}|_{L}
must be a proper coarsening of
w
as well. Since
\mathcal{O}_{w}\in H(L)
, it follows that
L(v_{N}|_{L})
is not separably closed. However,
Nv_{N}/L(v_{N}|_{L})
is finite, so
L(v_{N}|_{L})
must be real closed by the Artin-Schreier Theorem
1.1
.
Note that
L(v_{N}|_{L})/K(v_{N}|_{K})
is finite, and therefore
L(v_{N}|_{L})=K(v_{N}|_{K})
by the same theorem. In this setup,
v_{N}
restricts to a coarsening of
w
on
L
and to a coarsening of
v
on
K
, giving rise to induced valuations
\overline{w}
and
\overline{v}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(v_{N}|_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lw}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{N}|_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{w}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(v_{N}|_{K})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{N}|_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v}}

By (i),
v_{N}|_{K}
is henselian. Hence, by Fact
2.45
, to show that
v
is henselian, it suffices to prove that
\overline{v}
is henselian. Since
w
is henselian, so is
\overline{w}
(Fact
2.45
, again). Moreover, as
v=w|_{K}
, we have
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v}}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{w}}
, which implies that
\overline{v}
is henselian. We conclude
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in H(K)
as before.

(iii) To show that
v
is henselian, let
F/K
be a finite Galois extension. We must show that
v
has a unique prolongation to
F
. Clearly, one such prolongation is
v_{1}\coloneqq\widetilde{w}|_{F}
, where
\widetilde{w}
denotes the unique prolongation of
w
to
K^{\sepc}
. Let
v_{2}
be any other prolongation of
v
to
F
, and let
\widetilde{v_{2}}
denote a prolongation of
v_{2}
to
K^{\sepc}
. By the Conjugacy Theorem
2.14
, there exists
\sigma\in G_{K}
such that
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{v_{2}}}=\sigma\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{w}}
.

Additionally, set
M\coloneqq LF
. Recall that
G_{L}\in\Syl_{p}(G_{K})
, where
\Syl_{p}(G_{K})
denotes the set of Sylow
p
-subgroups of
G_{K}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\raisebox{1.50696pt}{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.4297,0.1055}\scriptsize$\widetilde{w},\widetilde{v_{2}}\,$}}K^{\sepc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\raisebox{1.50696pt}{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.4297,0.1055}\scriptsize$u_{1},u_{2}\,$}}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\raisebox{1.50696pt}{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.4297,0.1055}\scriptsize$w,v^{\prime}\,$}}L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\raisebox{1.50696pt}{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.4297,0.1055}\scriptsize$v_{1},v_{2}\,$}}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\cap F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\raisebox{1.50696pt}{\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.4297,0.1055}\scriptsize$v\,$}}K}
pro-
\scriptstyle p
prime-to-
\scriptstyle p

Figure.
The diagram of fields for our proof. The coloured decorations are the valuations that are considered for each field.

Since
K^{\sepc}/L
is a pro-
p
extension, so is
K^{\sepc}/M
. Similarly,
L/(L\cap F)
is a prime-to-
p
extension, hence so is
M/F
. Therefore,
G_{M}\in\Syl_{p}G_{F}
.

As
F/K
is Galois, we have
\sigma F=F
, so
F\subseteq\sigma M
. Applying the preceding argument to
\sigma M
in place of
M
(with
\sigma L
replacing
L
), we find that
G_{\sigma M}\in\Syl_{p}G_{F}
as well. By the second pro-finite Sylow Theorem (see
[
45
, Corollary 2.3.6 (c)]
),
G_{M}
and
G_{\sigma M}
are conjugate in
G_{F}
, so there exists
\tau\in G_{F}
with
\tau(M)=\sigma M
. Hence,
