u_{2}\coloneqq\widetilde{v_{2}}|_{\sigma M}\circ\tau
is a henselian prolongation of
v
to
M
, which restricts to
v_{2}
on
F
.

To show that
v_{1}=v_{2}
, it suffices to prove that
u_{1}\coloneqq\widetilde{w}|_{M}
and
u_{2}
coincide. By Fact
2.12
, it is enough to show that
\mathcal{O}_{u_{1}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{u_{2}}
are comparable. This holds if
u_{1}\in H(M)
or
u_{2}\in H(M)
. Suppose, for contradiction, that
v_{M}
is a proper coarsening of both
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
. Then,
v^{\prime}\coloneqq v_{M}|_{L}
is a proper coarsening of
w=u_{1}|_{L}
(again by Fact
2.12
), and hence its residue field
Lv^{\prime}
is not separably closed. Consequently, the residue field extension
Mv_{M}/Lv^{\prime}
is finite with
Mv_{M}
separably closed, which is only possible if
Lv^{\prime}
is real closed. In this case,
[Mv_{M}:Lv^{\prime}]=2
implies
2\mid[M:L]
by the fundamental equality (Corollary
2.25
), so we must have
p=2
. Our standing assumption for
p=2
excludes the existence of the valuation
v^{\prime}
, yielding a contradiction.

Finally, by the same argument as in part (i), we conclude
v\in H(K)
.
∎

Later, we will need an additional technical fact about real closed fields to handle the special case
p=2
in the Galois characterisation of henselianity in Section
3.4
.

Fact 2.55

(
[
20
, p. 109]
)
.

Let
K
be a field and
\mathcal{O}_{w}\in H_{1}(K)
a henselian valuation ring with real closed residue field. Then any henselian refinement
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in H_{1}(K)
of
w
will also have real closed residue field. In particular, if
K
admits at least one henselian valuation with real closed residue field, then it has a coarsest one.

The proof relies on the observation that henselian valuation rings on
K
are convex with respect to any ordering on
K
(see
[
20
, Lem. 4.3.6]
), and that any ordering on
K
induces an ordering on the residue field
Kv
of any convex valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq K
.

2.4.
Creation of valuations

One of our main ingredients, the Galois characterisation of henselianity (Theorem
3.29
), requires us to
create
a valuation on a field
K
based on abstract properties of
K
—out of thin air, as it would seem. The constructions originate in quadratic form theory, developed in the works of Arason, Bröcker, Elman, Jacob, and Ware
[
11
,
24
,
54
,
55
,
4
]
, and later adapted by Hwang, Jacob, and Koenigsmann
[
23
,
30
]
.

Leitfaden
.

The purpose of this section is to explain the proof of Theorem
2.68
, which provides a sufficient condition for the existence of a non-trivial valuation with non-
p
-divisible value group on
K
, based on the interplay between
K^{\times p}
and the additive structure of the field
K
. Historically, the somewhat computational proof was hard to motivate. It turns out that the computations involved work in a much more general setup—this setup, however, is easier to motivate. We follow Efrat’s abstract approach (§11 and §26.4 in his book
[
17
]
) using
S
-compatible valuations (see Definition
2.56
). Later, we will specialise to our case of interest.

2.4.1.
Creating valuations from rigid elements

Certainly, any field
K
can be endowed with the trivial valuation. What we are looking for are abstract properties of
K
that guarantee the existence of a valuation
v
that is
as non-trivial as possible
. This is realised by the idea of
multiplicative stratification
, which is made precise as follows:

Definition 2.56
.

Let
S\leq H
be multiplicative subgroups of
K^{\times}
. Define two sets of valuation rings on
K
,

\Val(S)\coloneqq\{\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq K\text{ val. ring}:1+\mathcal{M}_{v}\leq S\}\text{ \ and \ }\Val(S,H)\coloneqq\{\mathcal{O}_{v}\in\Val(S):\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}\leq H\}.

A valuation
v
is
S
-compatible
if
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in\Val(S)
.

The parameters
S
and
H

stratify
possible valuation rings into the sets
\Val(S)
and
\Val(S,H)
.
If
v
is trivial on
K
, then
1+\mathcal{M}_{v}=\{1\}
and
\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}=K^{\times}
. Hence,
S
and
H
control the non-triviality of
v
.
Strikingly,
\Val(S)
and
\Val(S,H)
fit into a hierarchy analogous to the tree
H_{1}(K)\cup H_{2}(K)
of henselian valuations (cf. Lemma
2.51
). In this sense, the condition
1+\mathcal{M}_{v}\leq S
defining
\Val(S)
can be seen as a weak analogue of henselianity.

Proposition 2.57

(
[
17
, Lem. 11.1.2]
)
.

Let
S\leq H\leq K^{\times}
be multiplicative subgroups. The set
\Val(S)
, ordered by the coarsening relation, has the following properties:

(1)

\Val(S)\setminus\Val(S,H)
is linearly ordered and any
\mathcal{O}_{w}\in\Val(S)\setminus\Val(S,H)
is coarser than any
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in\Val(S,H)
.

(2)

\Val(S,H)
is closed under refinements.

(3)

Any non-empty subset of
\Val(S,H)
has a supremum (i.e., a finest common coarsening) in
\Val(S,H)
. If
\Val(S,H)\neq\emptyset
, we write
v(S,H)
for the coarsest valuation in
\Val(S,H)
. In particular,
v(S,H)
is comparable to any valuation in
\Val(S)
.

v(S,H)
\Val(S)\setminus\Val(S,H)
\Val(S,H)
coarser
finer

In our reasoning, the question now is how to identify suitable candidates for subgroups
S\leq H\leq K^{\times}
such that
S
is sufficiently large,
H
is sufficiently small, and, moreover, to establish a criterion for the non-emptiness of
\Val(S,H)
.
The key to this is the following explicit description of
v(S,H)
.

Definition 2.58
.

Given
S\leq H\leq K^{\times}
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)

\displaystyle\coloneqq(1-S)\setminus H

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{+}(S,H)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{x\in H:x\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)\subseteq\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(S,H)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)\cup\mathcal{O}^{+}(S,H).

Consider the case that
S=1+\mathcal{M}_{v}
and
H=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
for some valuation
v
on
K
. Then

\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)=\mathcal{M}_{v},\ \mathcal{O}^{+}(S,H)=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times},\text{ and }\ \mathcal{O}(S,H)=\mathcal{O}_{v}.

Therefore,
\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)
is supposed to model the maximal ideal,
\mathcal{O}^{+}(S,H)
the units, and
\mathcal{O}(S,H)
the valuation ring of the valuation we aim to construct.

Proposition 2.59

(
[
17
, Thm. 11.3.6]
)
.

The following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\Val(S,H)
is non-empty;

(2)

\mathcal{O}(S,H)
lies in
\Val(S,H)
;

(3)

\mathcal{O}(S,H)
is equal to the valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v(S,H)}
of
v(S,H)
.

By doing computations with
\mathcal{O}(S,H)
, which gives an explicit description of
v(S,H)
, one can derive a criterion for the non-emptiness of
\Val(S,H)
. This, in turn, relies crucially on the notion of
rigidity
coming from the theory of quadratic forms.

Definition 2.60
.

Fix a subgroup
S\leq K^{\times}
. An element
x\in K^{\times}
is
S
-rigid
(or simply:
rigid
) if
S+xS\subseteq S\cup xS
, or equivalently,
1+xS\subseteq S\cup xS
.
Write

A(S)\coloneqq\{-x\in K^{\times}:1+x\notin S\cup xS\}=\{x\in K^{\times}:1-x,1-x^{-1}\notin S\}

for a distinguished set of additive inverses of non-rigid elements, which we use in the proposition below.

The notion of rigidity is in harmony with the ultrametric triangle inequality for valuations: if
S=1+\mathcal{M}_{v}
, and
H=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
, then any element in
K^{\times}\setminus H
is rigid and
A(S)=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
.
Note that our first goal is to formulate a full criterion determining for which
H
(depending on
S
) the set
\Val(S,H)
is non-empty.

Proposition 2.61
.

Let
S\leq H\leq K^{\times}
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\Val(S,H)
is non-empty;

(2)

-1
and
A(S)
are contained in
H
, and
\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)
satisfies the closure property

(
\dagger
)

\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)\subseteq 1-S;

(3)

every
x\in K^{\times}\setminus H
is rigid and
\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)
satisfies
(

†

‣
2
)
.

Proof.

(i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii). See
[
17
, Thm. 11.3.6]
for the proof of this equivalence.

(ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iii). Assume
-1\in H
and
A(S)\subseteq H
. Let
x
be non-rigid, i.e., there exists
s\in S
such that
1+xs\notin S\cup xS
. Then
-xs\in A(S)\subseteq H
, and therefore,
x\in H
.

Conversely, assume that each
x\in K^{\times}\setminus H
is rigid. Note that
-1
is non-rigid, so
-1\in H
. If
x\in A(S)
, then
-x
is non-rigid, hence
x\in H
.
∎

This proposition shows that non-rigid elements and (

†

‣
2
) are the only obstructions to creating valuations in the aforementioned sense, motivating the following:

Definition 2.62
.

Fix
S\leq K^{\times}
. We define

H_{S}\coloneqq\langle-1,S,A(S)\rangle\leq K^{\times}.

Equivalently,
H_{S}
is the group generated by
S
and all non-rigid elements with respect to
S
. We call
H_{S}
the
non-rigid hull of
S
.

The last ingredient we need is an additional fact about property (

†

‣
2
): it allows us to tweak
H
slightly to ensure that (

†

‣
2
) holds.

Lemma 2.63

(
[
17
, Prop. 11.4.2]
)
.

Given
S\leq H_{S}\leq H
, assume that
a,b\in\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H)
violate
(

†

‣
2
)
, i.e.,
ab\notin 1-S
. Then
a^{2}\in S
and
\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,\langle H,a\rangle)
satisfies
(

†

‣
2
)
.

Theorem 2.64

(Creating valuations from rigid elements)
.

The set
\Val(S,H)
is non-empty if and only if

(1)

H_{S}\leq H
in case that
\Val(S,H_{S})\neq\emptyset
, or

(2)

H_{S}<H^{\prime}\leq H
, where
(H^{\prime}:H_{S})=2
,
H^{\prime}=\langle H,a\rangle
, and
a\in\mathcal{O}^{-}(S,H_{S})
is an element as in the preceding lemma (in case that
\Val(S,H_{S})=\emptyset
).

In particular, if no element in
K^{\times}/S
has order two, (ii) cannot hold, and we must have
\Val(S,H_{S})\neq\emptyset
. In that case,
H_{S}\neq K^{\times}
implies that
\mathcal{O}(S,H_{S})
is a non-trivial valuation ring.

Proof.

In essence, this is
[
17
, Thm. 11.4.4]
. The theorem follows directly from the above: Proposition
2.61
shows that
H_{S}\leq H
is a necessary condition and Lemma
2.63
describes how to choose
H^{\prime}
in the case
\Val(S,H_{S})=\emptyset
.
∎

We may now drop the
S
-compatibility requirement for valuation rings and replace it with a condition on the value group:

Corollary 2.65

(
[
17
, Thm. 11.4.5]
)
.

Let
S\leq K^{\times}
be a subgroup such that no element in
K^{\times}/S
has order two. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

there exists a (non-trivial) valuation
v
on
K^{\times}
with
v(K^{\times})\neq v(S)
;

(2)

the non-rigid hull
H_{S}
is a proper subgroup of
K^{\times}
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Let
H=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}S=v^{-1}(v(S))
, which is a proper subgroup of
K^{\times}
since
v(K^{\times})\neq v(S)
. The ultrametric triangle inequality for
v
implies that
A(S)\subseteq H
. Therefore,
H_{S}\leq H
are proper subgroups of
K^{\times}
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). By Theorem
2.64
,
\mathcal{O}_{v}\coloneqq\mathcal{O}(S,H_{S})
is a non-trivial valuation ring. We cannot have
v(K^{\times})=v(S)
, for then
K^{\times}=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}S\leq H_{S}
, but
H_{S}
is supposed to be a proper subgroup. Hence,
v
is non-trivial with
v(K^{\times})\neq v(S)
.
∎

In
[
24
,
54
,
4
,
30
]
,
\mathcal{O}(S,H)
is constructed directly as in Definition
2.58
. Without the context of the tree of
S
-compatible valuations and its canonical member
v(S,H)
, the construction of the non-trivial valuation ring
\mathcal{O}(S,H_{S})
, together with the associated choice of
H_{S}
via rigid elements, can appear somewhat mysterious (see the discussion in
[
17
, p. xi]
).

2.4.2.
Creating valuations from
p
-rigid elements

In the context of the Galois characterisation of henselianity, we do not construct
H_{S}
directly. Instead, we will see that considerations from Kummer theory more naturally lead to the study of subgroups
T\leq K^{\times}
that satisfy a weaker form of rigidity.

Definition 2.66
.

Fix a prime
p
, and let
-1\in S\leq K^{\times}
. We say an element
x\in K^{\times}
is
p
-rigid
if

S+xS\subseteq\bigcup_{i=0}^{p-1}x^{i}S.

Let
T_{S}\leq K^{\times}
be the
non-
p
-rigid hull of
S
, i.e., the subgroup generated by
S
and all non-
p
-rigid elements
x\in K^{\times}
. Equivalently,
T_{S}
is generated by
S
and all
x\in K^{\times}
that satisfy

1-x\notin\bigcup_{i=0}^{p-1}x^{i}S.

Note that any
x\in K^{\times}\setminus S
is
p
-rigid, whenever we consider a valuation
v
on
K
and we choose
S=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}K^{\times p}
. This example trivialises if
vK
is
p
-divisible, because then
\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}K^{\times p}=K^{\times}
.

Since there are more non-rigid elements than non-
p
-rigid elements, it is clear that
T_{S}\leq H_{S}
. The goal is to bound the index
(H_{S}:T_{S})
, so that the non-triviality of
(K^{\times}:H_{S})
can be expressed as a condition on
(K^{\times}:T_{S})
. This final step was nicknamed the “wonderful creation of rigid elements” in
[
32
, Chap. 2.5]
.

Lemma 2.67

(From
p
-rigid to rigid elements)
.

Let
S\leq K^{\times}
be a subgroup that contains
-1
and
K^{\times p}
. If
a,b\in A(S)
, then
a
and
b
have
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly dependent cosets
5
5

5

Note that since
K^{\times p}\leq T_{S}
, the abelian group
K^{\times}/T_{S}
has exponent
p
.
in
K^{\times}/T_{S}
. In particular,
(H_{S}:T_{S})\leq p
.

Proof.

Our proof is an adaptation of
[
17
, Lem. 26.4.2, Prop. 26.4.3]
, which itself is based on
[
23
, Prop. 2.5]
.

Let
x,y\in K^{\times}\setminus T_{S}
represent
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly independent cosets in
K^{\times}/T_{S}
. By assumption,
x,y
, and
xy\in K^{\times}\setminus T_{S}
are
p
-rigid, so we there are unique
i,j,k\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
such that

1-x\in x^{i}S,\quad 1-y\in y^{j}S,\quad 1-xy\in(xy)^{k}S.

We expect some kind of relationship between
i
,
j
, and
k
, which we now determine as a first step. We may write

1-xy=(1-x)+x(1-y)\in x^{i}S+xy^{j}S=x^{i}(S+x^{1-i}y^{j}S).

If
(i,j)\neq(1,0)
, then
x^{1-i}y^{j}\notin T_{S}
is
p
-rigid and hence

1-xy\in x^{i}(S+x^{1-i}y^{j}S)\subseteq\bigcup_{l=0}^{p-1}x^{i}(x^{1-i}y^{j})^{l}S.

Moreover, recall that
1-xy\in(xy)^{k}S
.
Viewing the exponents of
x
and
y
as
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-coordinates in
\langle x,y,S\rangle/S
, we deduce that there exists a unique
l\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
such that

k\equiv i+(1-i)l\equiv jl\pmod{p}.

This implies

i\equiv(i+j-1)l\pmod{p}\Longrightarrow ij\equiv(i+j-1)jl\equiv(i+j-1)k\pmod{p}.

If
(i,j)\neq(0,1),(1,0)
, then
k\neq 0
implies
i,j\neq 0
. In that case, we can rearrange as

(2.8)

(1-i^{-1})(1-j^{-1})\equiv(1-k^{-1})\pmod{p}.

This analysis therefore yields the desired relationship between
i,j,k
, and allows us to define a sign function
\chi:K^{\times}\setminus T_{S}\longrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}
via

\chi(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}1-i^{-1}&\text{if $i\neq 0$ and $1-x\in x^{i}S$}\\
0&\text{if $1-x\in S$.}\end{cases}

From this definition and (
2.8
), we conclude that
\chi
satisfies the properties:

(1)

1\notin\im(\chi)
;

(2)

\chi(x)=0
if and only if
1-x\in S\cup xS
;

(3)

if
x,y\in K^{\times}\setminus T_{S}
have linearly independent cosets in
K^{\times}/T_{S}
, and
\chi(x)\neq 0
or
\chi(y)\neq 0
, then
\chi(xy)=\chi(x)\chi(y)
.

Finally, assume there exist
a,b\in A(S)
that represent linearly independent cosets in
K^{\times}/T_{S}
. We will show that this is impossible by the properties of
\chi
.

Note that (ii) implies
\chi(a),\chi(b)\neq 0
. Using (iii) inductively, we obtain

\chi(a^{p-1}b)=\chi(a)^{p-1}\chi(b)\chi(a^{p-1}b)=\chi(a^{p-1})\chi(b).

But since
\chi(a)^{p-1}=1
, it follows that
\chi(a^{p-1})=1
, contradicting (i).
∎

Putting everything together, we conclude:

Theorem 2.68

(Creating valuations from
p
-rigid elements)
.

Let
p
be any prime, and let
S\leq K^{\times}
be a subgroup containing
-1
and
K^{\times p}
. If
(K^{\times}:T_{S})>p
, then there exists a non-trivial valuation
v
on
K
with non-
p
-divisible value group, i.e.,
vK\neq pvK
.

Proof.

For
p=2
, we have
T_{S}=H_{S}
. By Theorem
2.64
, there exists
H_{S}\leq H^{\prime}\lneq K^{\times}
for which
\mathcal{O}_{v}=\mathcal{O}(S,H^{\prime})
is a non-trivial valuation ring. We cannot have
vK=2vK
, for otherwise,
K^{\times}=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}K^{\times 2}\subseteq H^{\prime}
.

For
p>2
, Lemma
2.67
implies that
(K^{\times}:H_{S})>1
. Note that any element in
K^{\times}/S
has odd order dividing
p
.
Finally, by Corollary
2.65
, there exists a valuation
v
on
K
with

pvK=v(K^{\times p})\subseteq v(S)\neq v(K^{\times})=vK.\qed

As a final remark, let us note that all known proofs of our Main Theorem rely on the creation of valuations: in each case, the above construction serves as the original source of an abstract valuation for fields with absolute Galois group isomorphic to
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

2.5.
First-order languages for valued fields

In Sections
4
and
5
, we view ordered groups, valued fields, etc., as model-theoretic structures.
For the avoidance of doubt, we specify here the precise first-order languages that we use.

We regard fields as structures in the language of rings

\mathcal{L}_{\ring}\coloneqq\{0,1,+,-,\cdot\},

and ordered abelian groups as structures in the language

\mathcal{L}_{\oag}\coloneqq\{0,+,-,\leq\}.

Here,
+,-,\cdot
are binary function symbols, and
\leq
is a binary relation symbol.
Our language for valued fields is

\mathcal{L}_{\text{VF}}\coloneqq(\mathsf{VF},\mathsf{VG},\mathsf{RF};\,v,\res),

a three-sorted language consisting of sorts for the underlying valued field, the value group, and the residue field. For
\mathsf{VF}
and
\mathsf{RF}
we have the same symbols as in
\mathcal{L}_{\ring}
, and
\mathsf{VG}
is given the symbols in
\mathcal{L}_{\oag}
; additionally,
\mathcal{L}_{\VF}
contains two unary function symbols
v:\mathsf{VF}\longrightarrow\mathsf{VG}
and
\res:\mathsf{VF}\longrightarrow\mathsf{RF}
, interpreted as the valuation and residue maps, respectively.

A more economical, one-sorted language for valued fields is

\mathcal{L}_{\val}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}_{\ring}\cup\{\mathcal{O}\},

where
\mathcal{O}
is a unary predicate interpreted as a valuation ring.
The languages
\mathcal{L}_{\VF}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\val}
have the same expressive power; formulas may differ in complexity.

Fact 2.69
.

The
\mathcal{L}_{\VF}
- and
\mathcal{L}_{\val}
-structure of a valued field
(K,v)
are uniformly bi-interpretable. In particular, for any two valued fields
(K,v)
and
(K,v^{\prime})
, we have

(K,vK,Kv;\,v,\res)\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\VF}}(K^{\prime},v^{\prime}K^{\prime},K^{\prime}v^{\prime};\,v^{\prime},\res^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow(K,\mathcal{O}_{v})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{\val}}(K^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}).

For brevity, we will simply write
(K,v)
for the entire
\mathcal{L}_{\VF}
-structure.

3.
Valuations and absolute Galois groups

3.1.
The explicit structure of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}

3.1.1.
Cyclotomic extensions

Definition 3.1
.

Let
F
be a field. Denote the set of roots of unity in
F
by
\mu_{F}
. Whenever
F
contains all
n
-th roots of unity for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
, we denote this set by
\mu_{n}
. Furthermore, write
\zeta_{n}
for a primitive
n
-th root of unity and
\zeta_{q^{\infty}}
for the set of all
q
-power roots of unity.

When several roots of unity are involved, we tacitly assume that they form a compatible system (e.g.
\zeta_{q^{2}}^{q}=\zeta_{q}
).

The following is well-known:

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
L=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\mu_{n})
be a cyclotomic extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

(1)

If
(n,p)=1
, then
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is unramified of degree
k=\ord_{n}(p)
, the order of
p
in
(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
. In particular,

\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cong\Gal(\mathbb{F}_{p^{k}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong C_{k}.

(2)

If
n=p^{r}
, then
L/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is totally ramified of degree
(p-1)p^{r-1}
and
1-\zeta_{p^{r}}
is a uniformiser. Moreover,

\Gal(L/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cong\bigl(\mathbb{Z}/p^{r}\mathbb{Z}\bigr)^{\times}.

In particular, cyclotomic extensions are abelian.

We therefore obtain the following description of the roots of unity in
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

Corollary 3.3
.

In
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, the roots of unity are

\mu_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=\begin{cases}\mu_{p-1}&\text{if $p\neq 2$}\\
\mu_{2}&\text{if $p=2$.}\end{cases}

In the context of our Main Theorem—the Galois-theoretic characterisation of
p
-adically closed fields—we consider the situation where a field
K
satisfies
G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. To draw conclusions about the roots of unity contained in
K
, we seek a purely Galois-theoretic criterion that identifies the roots of unity in
K
. This motivates the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(K,v)
be a henselian valued field, and let
q
be any prime. Assume that

(1)

\charK Kv\neq q
;

(2)

vK
is not
q
-divisible;

(3)

\Gal(Kv(q)/Kv)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, where
Kv(q)
is the maximal pro-
q
Galois extension.

Then, for any
r\geq 1
,

\zeta_{q^{r}}\in K\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\text{$K$ admits a $C_{q^{r}}\times C_{q^{r}}$-extension.}

In particular, the lemma applies to
(K,v)=(\mathbb{Q}_{p},v_{p})
for
p\neq q
.

Proof.

Note that the assumptions hold for
K=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
by the proof of Proposition
2.29
.

Assume
\zeta_{q^{r}}\in K
. By assumption
3
,
K
admits an unramified
C_{q^{r}}
-extension. Moreover, by
2
, we may choose
x\in K
with
vx
not
q
-divisible and consider the totally ramified Kummer extension
K(\!\!\sqrt[q^{r}]{x})/K
, which is well-defined by
1
and the existence of a primitive
q^{r}
-th root of unity. Any unramified and totally ramified extensions of
K
are linearly disjoint, so taking the compositum of these two
C_{q^{r}}
-extensions yields the desired
C_{q^{r}}\times C_{q^{r}}
-extension.

Conversely, assume that
K
has a
C_{q^{r}}\times C_{q^{r}}
-extension
L/K
. Consider the maximal unramified extension
K^{\ur}/K
inside
L
. By assumption
3
,
\Gal(K^{\ur}/K)
must be cyclic and a quotient of
C_{q^{r}}\times C_{q^{r}}
. Therefore,
K
admits a
C_{q^{r}}
-extension
E/K
linearly disjoint from
K^{\ur}/K
. Then, the extension
E/K
is totally ramified, and tamely so by assumption
1
.

Let
w
be the unique prolongation of
v
to
E
. By Hensel’s Lemma and
1
, it suffices to show that
\zeta_{q^{r}}\in Ew=Kv
. We can see that this follows from the Second Exact Sequence (
2.4
):

\Gal(E/K)=I_{w}(E/K)\cong\Hom(wE/vK,\mu_{Ew}).

Indeed,
\Hom(wE/vK,\mu_{Ew})\cong C_{q^{r}}
if and only if
wE/vK\cong C_{q^{r}}
and
\zeta_{q^{r}}\in\mu_{Ew}=\mu_{Kv}
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Observe that if a valued field
(K,v)
satisfies
1
–
3
in the above lemma, and
L/K
is a finite abelian extension with unique prolongation
w
of
v
, then the valued field
(L,w)
also satisfies
1
–
3
.

We will further consider infinite cyclotomic extensions of
K
, to which the following subtle observation applies.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
F
be a field with
\charK F\neq q
. Then
F(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})=F(\zeta_{q})
,
F(\zeta_{2^{\infty}})=F(\zeta_{4})
(if
q=2
)
6
6

6

These exceptional cases do occur: for example, consider the field
\mathbb{Q}(\{\zeta_{q^{n}}+\zeta_{q^{n}}^{-1}\}_{n\geq 1})\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Its only root of unity is
\zeta_{2}
—after adjoining
\zeta_{q}
or
\zeta_{4}
(if
q=2
), it will contain all
q^{n}
-th roots of unity.
, or
\Gal(F(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})/F)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times C
, where
C
is cyclic of order dividing
q-1
or 2 (if
q=2
).

Proof.

Consider
F_{n}\coloneqq F(\zeta_{q^{n}})
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which form a tower of field extensions. Assume
q\neq 2
and that there exists
n\geq 2
such that
\zeta_{q^{n}}\notin F_{n-1}
. If
\zeta_{q^{n+1}}\in F_{n}
, then

(N_{F_{n}/F_{n-1}}(\zeta_{q^{n+1}}))^{q}=N_{F_{n}/F_{n-1}}(\zeta_{q^{n}})=\zeta_{q^{n-1}}\in F_{n-1}.

However,
\zeta_{q^{n-1}}
has no
q
-th root in
F_{n}
by assumption. Hence,
\zeta_{q^{n+1}}
cannot lie in
F_{n}
, and by induction, the
F_{n}
form an infinite degree tower, except when
F(\zeta_{q})=F(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})
. The same idea works for
q=2
, only that a change in sign in the norm forces us to require
n\geq 3
. If we are not in one of the exceptional cases, the tower is of infinite degree, and the claim follows by taking the inverse limit of
\Gal(F_{n}/F)
.
∎

3.1.2.
Residues and generators in
p
-adic fields

Let
F
be a
p
-adic field, i.e., a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. In this section, if not stated otherwise, we will always write
v
for the unique valuation on
F
extending the
p
-adic valuation on
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and
\mathcal{O}_{F}
for its valuation ring.

The goal of this section is to study the multiplicative structure of
p
-adic fields by explicit means.
Our starting point is the filtration

F^{\times}\supseteq U_{F}^{(0)}\supseteq U_{F}^{(1)}\supseteq\ldots\supseteq U_{F}^{(k)}\supseteq\ldots

of the multiplicative group
F^{\times}
by higher unit groups.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
F
be a
p
-adic field with uniformiser
\pi
. The
k
-th higher unit groups
are defined as

\displaystyle U^{(0)}_{F}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}

\displaystyle U^{(k)}_{F}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigl\{x\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}:x\equiv 1\;(\text{mod }{\pi^{k}})\bigr\}\quad\text{for $k\geq 1$.}

Some of the calculations given below are standard; others may be less well-known.

Fact 3.8
.

Let
F
be a
p
-adic field with uniformiser
\pi
. Denote its prime-to-
p
roots of unity by
\mu^{\prime}_{F}
. The multiplicative group
F^{\times}
can be decomposed as the direct product

F^{\times}=\pi^{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mu^{\prime}_{F}\times U_{F}^{(1)}.

Fact 3.9
.

Let
F
be a
p
-adic field. For any
k\geq 1
, there exist isomorphisms

\displaystyle U_{F}^{(0)}/U_{F}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\cong((Fv)^{\times},\cdot)

\displaystyle U_{F}^{(k)}/U_{F}^{(k+1)}

\displaystyle\cong(Fv,+).

Proof.

The isomorphisms are given by

\displaystyle xU_{F}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\longmapsto x+\mathcal{M}_{F}

\displaystyle(1+\pi^{k}a)U_{F}^{(k+1)}

\displaystyle\longmapsto a+\mathcal{M}_{F},

see
[
43
, Chap. II, (3.10)]
.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

The fact that the above maps are indeed isomorphisms relies on the following rule linking additive and multiplicative congruences: for any
a,a^{\prime}\in U_{F}^{(0)}
,

a\equiv a^{\prime}\pmod{\pi^{k}}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad a\equiv a^{\prime}\quad\bigl(\mathrm{mod}\ U_{F}^{(k)}\bigr).

The equivalence is easily verified:

\exists b\in\mathcal{O}_{F}:a^{\prime}=a+b\pi^{k}\Longleftrightarrow\exists b\in\mathcal{O}_{F}:\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}=1+a^{-1}b\pi^{k}\Longleftrightarrow\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}\in U_{F}^{(k)}.

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
F
be a
p
-adic field and
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{F}
a set of representatives for
Fv
. Then any
x\in U_{F}^{(1)}/U_{F}^{(k+1)}
has a unique representation of the form
x=aU_{F}^{(k+1)}
, where

a=1+a_{1}\pi+\ldots+a_{k}\pi^{k},

and all coefficients
a_{i}
lie in
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Assume that
a=1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}\pi^{i}
and
a^{\prime}=1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}^{\prime}\pi^{i}
represent the same element modulo
U_{F}^{(k+1)}
. In other words, there exists
1+\pi^{k+1}b\in U_{F}^{(k+1)}
with
a=a^{\prime}(1+\pi^{k+1}b)
. Assume
a_{i}\neq a_{i}^{\prime}
for some
i
, in which case we choose the minimal such
i
. Then

v(a-a^{\prime})=v((a_{i}-a_{i}^{\prime})\pi^{i})=v(\pi^{i}),

which contradicts
a-a^{\prime}=a^{\prime}\pi^{k+1}b
. Therefore,
a_{i}=a_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
.

The fact that any element in
U_{F}^{(1)}/U_{F}^{(k+1)}
can be written as claimed follows from a counting argument. Observe that

\bigl|U_{F}^{(1)}/U_{F}^{(k+1)}\bigr|=|Fv|^{k}

by Fact
3.9
, which is exactly the number of distinct representatives we consider.
∎

The following residue calculation is crucial for the results that follow.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
F
be a
p
-adic field containing
\zeta_{p}
. Then

\frac{p}{\pi^{p-1}}\equiv-1\pmod{\pi},

where
\pi=\zeta_{p}-1
. In particular,
p/\pi^{p-1}
has residue
-1
in the residue field.

Proof.

Consider

f(X)=X^{p-1}+\ldots+X+1=\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}(X-\zeta_{p}^{i}).

Observe that

\frac{p}{\pi^{p-1}}=\frac{f(1)}{(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p-1}}=(-1)^{p-1}\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}\biggl(\frac{\zeta^{i}_{p}-1}{\zeta_{p}-1}\biggr)=(-1)^{p-1}\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}(1+\zeta_{p}+\ldots+\zeta_{p}^{i-1}).

For each factor, we compute

1+\zeta_{p}+\ldots+\zeta_{p}^{i-1}=1+(1+\pi)+\ldots+(1+\pi)^{i-1}\equiv i\pmod{\pi}.

Therefore, the claim follows from
p\in(\pi)
,
(-1)^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
, and Wilson’s Theorem

(p-1)!\equiv-1\pmod{p}.\qed

Lemma 3.13

(cf.
[
32
, Lem. 3.2]
)
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
be a finite extension with ramification index
e\coloneqq e(F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p}))
. Then

U_{F}^{(ep+1)}\subseteq\bigl(U_{F}^{(e)}\bigr)^{p}.

Moreover, for
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
, we have
U_{F}^{(p+1)}=\bigl(U_{F}^{(1)}\bigr)^{p}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is totally ramified of degree
p-1
(Lemma
3.2
). Let
\pi=\zeta_{p}-1
be a uniformiser for
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
and
\Pi
any uniformiser for
F
. If the valuation
v
on
F
is normalised such that
vp=1
, then
v\pi=\tfrac{1}{p-1}
and
v\Pi=\tfrac{1}{e(p-1)}
. In particular,

U_{F}^{(e)}=1+(\pi)\quad\text{and}\quad U_{F}^{(ep+1)}=1+(\pi^{p}\Pi).

Let
x=1+\pi^{p}a
with
a\in(\Pi)
be given. Then it suffices to show that

(1+\pi X)^{p}-(1+\pi^{p}a)=0

has a solution in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Consider

f(X)\coloneqq\pi^{-p}\Bigl[(1+\pi X)^{p}-(1+\pi^{p}a)\Bigr]=X^{p}-a+\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}\binom{p}{i}\pi^{i-p}X^{i}.

Noting that

\displaystyle v\left[\binom{p}{i}\pi^{i-p}\right]

\displaystyle=v(\pi^{i-1})>0

\displaystyle\text{ for $i=2,\ldots,p-1$},

\displaystyle p\pi^{1-p}

\displaystyle\equiv-1\pmod{\pi}\

by Lemma
3.12
,

we conclude that
f(X)
reduces to
\overline{f}(X)=X^{p}-X\in(Fv)[X]
. So the first claim follows by Hensel’s Lemma.

Now assume
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
and let
\Pi=\pi
. The above considerations then yield

(1+\pi b)^{p}\equiv 1+p\pi b+\pi^{p}b^{p}\equiv 1+\pi^{p}(b^{p}-b)\pmod{\pi^{p+1}}

for any
b\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Since
Fv=\mathbb{F}_{p}
, we have
b^{p}\equiv b\pmod{\pi}
, and therefore

\bigl(U_{F}^{(1)}\bigr)^{p}\subseteq U_{F}^{(p+1)}.\qed

The unit group
U_{F}^{(ep+1)}
will, from now on, play a central role.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
be a finite extension with uniformiser
\pi
in
F
. Write
e
for the ramification index and
f
for the degree of inertia of this extension. Assume further that
\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{f}\}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{F}
represent an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-basis for
Fv
. Then

\{\pi\}\cup\{1+b_{i}\pi^{k}:1\leq i\leq f,\,1\leq k\leq ep\}

represents a generating set in
F^{\times}/F^{\times p}
. Moreover, for
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
,

\{\pi\}\cup\{1+b_{1}\pi^{k}:1\leq k\leq p\}

represents an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-basis of
F^{\times}/F^{\times p}
.

Proof.

By the direct sum decomposition of Fact
3.8
, we obtain an isomorphism

F^{\times}/F^{\times p}\cong\pi^{\mathbb{Z}}/\pi^{p\mathbb{Z}}\times U_{F}^{(1)}/\bigl(U_{F}^{(1)}\bigr)^{p},

where the first factor is generated by
\overline{\pi}
. We now focus on the second factor.

The idea is to compare sets modulo
U_{F}^{(ep+1)}
, which we know lies in
\bigl(U_{F}^{(1)}\bigr)^{p}
by Lemma
3.13
. Therefore, it suffices to show that the elements
1+b_{i}\pi^{k}
generate
U_{F}^{(1)}
modulo
U_{F}^{(ep+1)}
.

Claim.
\langle 1+b_{i}\pi^{k}:1\leq i\leq f,\,l\leq k\leq ep\rangle\equiv U_{F}^{(l)}\;\bigl(\mathrm{mod}\ U_{F}^{(ep+1)}\bigr)
for any
l=1,\ldots,ep+1
.

Proof of Claim.

Via backward induction from
l=ep+1
to
l=1
. The case
l=ep+1
is trivial. By Fact
3.9
,

\langle 1+b_{1}\pi^{l},\ldots,1+b_{f}\pi^{l}\rangle U_{F}^{(l+1)}=U_{F}^{(l)},

for any
l=1,\ldots,ep+1
. This identity proves the inductive step
l+1\rightarrow l
. For
l=1
, we recover our original claim.

\diamond

Consider now the special case
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
. Recall that by Lemma
3.13
,

U_{F}^{(1)}/\bigl(U_{F}^{(1)}\bigr)^{p}=U_{F}^{(1)}/U_{F}^{(p+1)}.

By the same proof,
\{1+b_{1}\pi^{k}:1\leq k\leq p\}
is a generating set of cardinality
p
for
U_{F}^{(1)}/U_{F}^{(p+1)}
. Hence, it must be a basis, since

\bigl|U_{F}^{(1)}/U_{F}^{(p+1)}\bigr|=|Fv|^{p}=p^{p}.\qed

3.1.3.
Degree counting

The lemma we are about to prove counts the
q
-rank of a
p
-adic field
F
(the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-dimension of
F^{\times}/F^{\times q}
). It is most commonly proved via a direct sum decomposition based on the
p
-adic logarithm, which turns
F^{\times}
into an additive structure, see
[
43
, Chap. II, (5.8)]
. Here, we present an alternative proof, which has the added benefit of working in a much larger class of valued fields that includes
p
-adically closed fields.

Lemma 3.15

(Degree Lemma)
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a finite extension of degree
n
. Let
q
be a prime such that
\zeta_{q}\in F
. Then

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(F^{\times}/F^{\times q})=\begin{cases}2&\text{if }q\neq p\\
n+2&\text{if }q=p.\end{cases}

Proof.

We first consider the case
q\neq p
.
Let
f\coloneqq f(F/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be the degree of inertia.
Note that any element in
U^{(1)}\coloneqq U^{(1)}_{F}
has a
q
-th root by Hensel’s Lemma. Using the direct sum decomposition for
F^{\times}
(Fact
3.8
), we obtain

F^{\times}/F^{\times q}\cong\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}\times\mu^{\prime}_{F}/(\mu^{\prime}_{F})^{q}.

From
\zeta_{q}\in\mu^{\prime}_{F}=\mu_{p^{f}-1}
(Lemma
3.2
), we conclude that
q\mid(p^{f}-1)
, so the second factor is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/q\mathbb{Z}
and the claim follows.

Now consider the case
q=p
. Then
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})\subseteq F
, and let
e\coloneqq e(F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p}))
be the ramification index relative to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
. Hence,

(3.1)

n=e(F/\mathbb{Q}_{p})f(F/\mathbb{Q}_{p})=e(p-1)f.

Reasoning as in the first case, we obtain

F^{\times}/F^{\times p}\cong\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\times U^{(1)}/\bigl(U^{(1)}\bigr)^{p},

so it suffices to show that
U^{(1)}/\bigl(U^{(1)}\bigr)^{p}
has
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-dimension
n+1
. To calculate the dimension, we again work modulo
U^{(ep+1)}
. First of all, write

U^{(1)}/\bigl(U^{(1)}\bigr)^{p}\cong\frac{U^{(1)}/U^{(ep+1)}}{\displaystyle(U^{(1)})^{p}/U^{(ep+1)}}

using Lemma
3.13
. By Corollary
3.11
and (
3.1
), we have

\bigl|U^{(1)}/U^{(ep+1)}\bigr|=|Fv|^{ep}=p^{fep}=p^{n+\frac{n}{p-1}}.

Hence, it remains to prove

(3.2)

\bigl|(U^{(1)})^{p}/U^{(ep+1)}\bigr|=p^{\frac{n}{p-1}-1}.

Claim. The
p
-th power map
x\longmapsto x^{p}
induces a well-defined epimorphism

\Phi:U^{(1)}/U^{(e+1)}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow(U^{(1)})^{p}/U^{(ep+1)},

with image of cardinality
p^{n/(p-1)-1}
.

Proof of Claim.

Let
\pi
be a uniformiser in
F
. Using
vp=v(\pi^{e(p-1)})
, observe that

(1+b\pi^{e+1})^{p}\equiv 1\pmod{\pi^{ep+1}}\quad\text{for all $b\in\mathcal{O}_{F}$,}

which implies
(U^{(e+1)})^{p}\subseteq U^{(ep+1)}
. Thus,
\Phi
is well-defined and surjective. It suffices to show that
\Phi
has kernel of order
p
. To this end, let
1+a_{1}\pi+\ldots+a_{e}\pi^{e}
represent an element in
U^{(1)}/U^{(e+1)}
, where each
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
comes from a set of representatives for
Fv
(Corollary
3.11
). Without loss of generality, assume that
a_{i}=0
represents
0\in Fv
. Consider

(1+a_{1}\pi+\ldots+a_{e}\pi^{e})^{p}\equiv 1\quad\bigl(\mathrm{mod}\ U^{(ep+1)}\bigr),

or equivalently,

(3.3)

(1+a_{1}\pi+\ldots+a_{e}\pi^{e})^{p}-1\equiv 0\pmod{\pi^{ep+1}}.

We claim that
a_{i}=0
for all
i\leq e-1
. If not, let
i
be minimal such that
\overline{a_{i}}\neq 0
. Expanding the expression in (
3.3
), we see that
a_{i}^{p}\pi^{pi}
is the unique term of minimal value—indeed,
pa_{i}\pi^{i}
has value exceeding
v(\pi^{pi})
—, which cannot happen.
Therefore, (
3.3
) simplifies to

(1+a_{e}\pi^{e})^{p}-1\equiv a_{e}^{p}\pi^{ep}+pa_{e}\pi^{e}\equiv 0\pmod{\pi^{ep+1}},

which is equivalent to
\overline{a_{e}}\in Fv
being a solution of

(3.4)

X^{p}+\overline{\frac{p}{\pi^{e(p-1)}}}X=0.

Using Lemma
3.12
, one easily checks that

\{0\}\cup\left\{\overline{\frac{\zeta_{p}-1}{\pi^{e}}\zeta_{p-1}^{i}}:i=0,\ldots,p-2\right\}

is the solution set for (
3.4
). This proves that
\Phi
is
p
-to-1, implying (
3.2
).

∎

Remark 3.16
.

As the proof of the Main Theorem will require a somewhat stronger version of the Degree Lemma for
q=p
, we examine which properties of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
were used in the arguments leading up to Lemma
3.15
. We find that the following assumptions are needed:
(K,v)
is a henselian valued field of characteristic 0 satisfying

(1)

\zeta_{p}\in K
;

(2)

the value group has minimal positive element
1
and
v(p)=e(p-1)<\infty
;

(3)

the residue field is
\mathbb{F}_{p^{f}}
with
f<\infty
.

If
(K,v)
satisfies these conditions, then the following general degree formula holds:

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(K^{\times}/K^{\times p})=e(p-1)f+2

Let us briefly note that an alternative proof of this generalisation could proceed by reduction to the
p
-adic case: in the Standard Decomposition of
v_{K}
, the (0, ​0)-place does not contribute to the dimension of
K^{\times}/K^{\times p}
, and both the core field
Kv_{0}
and its completion (which is a
p
-adic field) have the same
p
-rank by Hensel’s Lemma.

Corollary 3.17
.

\mathbb{Q}_{p}
has only finitely many extensions of degree
n
for any positive integer
n
.

Proof.

Assume
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
has infinitely many extensions of degree
n
. Passing to normal hulls, this
gives infinitely many Galois extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
of degree
m
for some
m\leq n!
. If
L
is the finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
obtained by adjoining
\zeta_{q}
for each prime divisor
q
of
m
, then it has infinitely many Galois extensions of degree
k
for some
k\mid m
. By the Degree Lemma
3.15
and Kummer theory, any finite extension of
L
admits only finitely many
C_{q}
-extensions. As any Galois extension of
L
of degree
k
is obtained by iterated
C_{q}
-extensions (any such extension is solvable by the ramification filtration), there can only be finitely many of them: contradiction.
∎

We say that
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is
small
, i.e., has finitely many open subgroups of index
n
, for any fixed
n
.

Remark 3.18
.

The alternative (standard) approach is to use continuity of roots (Fact
2.15
), Krasner’s Lemma
2.33
, and the compactness of the space
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
of integral polynomials of degree
n
to deduce that there are finitely many totally ramified extensions of degree
n
. The general statement then follows by considering inertia subfields.

We will later need the following converse statement about fields that have small absolute Galois group.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
K
be a field and
q
be any prime. If
K
has small absolute Galois group and
\charK K\neq q
, then
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(K^{\times}/K^{\times q})<\infty
.

Proof.

Note that
L\coloneqq K(\zeta_{q})
is a Galois extension of
K
of degree
d\mid(q-1)
. The norm map induces an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-vector space homomorphism

\overline{N_{L/K}}:L^{\times}/L^{\times q}\longrightarrow K^{\times}/K^{\times q}

that is surjective, since for any
x\in K^{\times}
, we have

\langle N_{L/K}(x)\rangle=\langle x^{d}\rangle\equiv\langle x\rangle\pmod{K^{\times q}}.

Hence,

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(K^{\times}/K^{\times q})\leq\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(L^{\times}/L^{\times q})<\infty,

by Kummer theory, using the fact that
L
admits only finitely many
C_{q}
-extensions (since
G_{K}
is small).
∎

3.2.
Fields with finite
p
-rank

The finiteness of
K^{\times}/K^{\times p}
is a natural condition in the context of Kummer theory, and, as we have shown above, holds for
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. In
[
44
, Kor. 2.7]
, Pop discovered that this simple condition implies strong structural properties whenever
K
comes with a valuation. See also
[
17
, 12.5]
.

Lemma 3.20

(Pop’s Lemma)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Assume that
K^{\times}/K^{\times p}
is finite. Then:

(1)

Kv
is perfect.

(2)

If
v
is of rank 1, then
vK
is either discrete or
p
-divisible.

(3)

If
vK
is discrete, then
Kv
is finite.

Proof.

We have two identities,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times p}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}\cap K^{\times p}

\displaystyle(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})^{p}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times p}\cap(1+\mathcal{M}_{v}),

where the first one is immediate; for the second one, note that for any
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
,

(3.5)

x^{p}\equiv 1\pmod{\mathcal{M}_{v}}\Longrightarrow x\equiv 1\pmod{\mathcal{M}_{v}}.

Consequently, the two standard short exact sequences of (
2.1
) induce two additional short exact sequences by the nine lemma:

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times p}\longrightarrow K^{\times}/K^{\times p}\longrightarrow vK/pvK\longrightarrow 1

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})/(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})^{p}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times p}\longrightarrow(Kv)^{\times}/(Kv)^{\times p}\longrightarrow 1

The exactness of these sequences implies that all quantities in

|(Kv)^{\times}/(Kv)^{\times p}|\cdot|(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})/(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})^{p}|=|\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times p}|\leq|K^{\times}/K^{\times p}|

are finite.

To prove (i), it suffices to consider the case that
Kv
is infinite. In that case,
(Kv)^{p}
must be infinite as well.
Now fix any
x\in(Kv)^{\times}
. Observe that
\{x+a^{p}:a\in(Kv)^{\times}\}
is an infinite set, while
K^{\times}/K^{\times p}
is finite. By the pigeonhole principle, there are
a,b,c,d\in(Kv)^{\times}
,
a\neq c
,
b\neq d
, such that
(x+a^{p})b^{p}=(x+c^{p})d^{p}
. Solving for
x
, we obtain

x=\frac{(cd-ab)^{p}}{(b-d)^{p}}\in(Kv)^{\times p}.

Hence,
Kv
is perfect.

For (ii), recall that we know
(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})/(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})^{p}
is finite. We let
\{1+a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}\subseteq 1+\mathcal{M}_{v}
be a complete set of representatives for
(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})/(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})^{p}
and define

\delta\coloneqq\min\{va_{1},\ldots,va_{n},vp\}>0.

Consider an arbitrary element
x\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
. For some
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
y\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
, we may write

1+x=(1+a_{i})(1+y)^{p}

and therefore

(3.6)

vx=v\bigl(a_{i}(1+y)^{p}+[(1+y)^{p}-1-y^{p}]+y^{p}\bigr)\geq\min\{va_{i},vp,v(y^{p})\}.

If
vx<\delta
, then (
3.6
) implies
v(y^{p})<\delta
. In particular, we attain equality
vx=v(y^{p})\in pvK
in this case. Our consideration now splits into two cases: if
\delta
is minimal positive in
vK
, then
vK=\delta\mathbb{Z}
is discrete. Otherwise, the interval
(0,\delta)\subset vK
is
p
-divisible and non-empty. Since
vK
is of rank 1, the interval generates
vK
, and hence
vK
must be
p
-divisible itself.

Finally, for (iii), we assume
K
has a uniformiser. It is easy to see that
(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})^{p}\subseteq 1+\mathcal{M}_{v}^{2}
. In particular,

|(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})/(1+\mathcal{M}_{v}^{2})|\leq|(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})/(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})^{p}|<\infty.

Adapting Fact
3.9
, we see that the left-hand side equals
|Kv|
, so
Kv
must be finite.
∎

3.3.
Distinguishing characteristics of fields

In this section, we answer the question:
how does
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
know that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
has characteristic
\neq p
?

Galois cohomology provides an immediate answer if we consider cohomological
p
-dimensions:

\cd_{p}G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}=2,\text{ while }\cd_{p}G_{K}\leq 1

for any field
K
of characteristic
p
, see
[
52
, II. §4, II. §2.2]
. Since our goal is to give a self-contained answer, we present an elementary criterion—motivated by Galois cohomology—for distinguishing fields of characteristic zero from those of positive characteristic. This will make explicit the elementary ideas concealed by the cohomological formalism.

Idea 3.21
.

For a field
K
, consider the property of whether every
C_{p}
-extension of
K
embeds into a
C_{p^{2}}
-extension of
K
. We will show that this is true in general if
K
has characteristic
p
, and false for some
p
-adic field
K
.

Cyclic Galois extensions are well-understood by Hilbert 90, Kummer theory, and Artin-Schreier theory (in positive characteristic). For convenience, we state these classical results.

Theorem 3.22

(Hilbert 90)
.

Let
L/K
be a finite cyclic Galois extension and
\sigma
a generator of
\Gal(L/K)
. Then for any
a\in L
:

\displaystyle N_{L/K}(a)=1

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\exists b\in L^{\times}\

\displaystyle a=\sigma(b)\cdot b^{-1}

(“Multiplicative H90”)

\displaystyle\Tr_{L/K}(a)=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\exists b\in L\

\displaystyle a=\sigma(b)-b

(“Additive H90”)

One can deduce the following characterisations of
C_{p}
-extensions:

Theorem 3.23

(Kummer theory)
.

Let
K
be a field with
\charK K\neq p
and
\zeta_{p}\in K
. Then there exists a bijection

\displaystyle\{\text{one-dim.\,subspaces }\langle c\rangle K^{\times p}\leq K^{\times}/K^{\times p}\}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\{\text{$L/K$ Galois extensions of deg.\;$p$}\}

\displaystyle\langle c\rangle K^{\times p}

\displaystyle\longmapsto K(\sqrt[p]{c})

\displaystyle K^{\times}\cap L^{\times p}

\displaystyle\longmapsfrom L.

Theorem 3.24

(Artin-Schreier theory)
.

Let
K
be a field of positive characteristic
p
. Then there exists a bijection

\displaystyle\{\text{one-dim.\;subspaces }\langle c\rangle+\wp(K)\leq K/\wp(K)\}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\{\text{$L/K$ Galois extensions of deg.\;$p$}\}

\displaystyle\langle c\rangle+\wp(K)

\displaystyle\longmapsto K(\alpha),

where
\wp(X)=X^{p}-X
is the Artin-Schreier map and
\alpha
is a root of the Artin-Schreier polynomial
\wp(X)-c
.

The next three lemmas realise Idea
3.21
.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
K
be a field of positive characteristic
p
. Then any
C_{p}
-extension
L/K
is contained in a
C_{p^{2}}
-extension
M/K
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{p^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{p}}

Proof.

By Artin-Schreier theory,
L
is generated by a root
x
of an Artin-Schreier polynomial
X^{p}-X-a
. Dividing by
(-x^{p}a)
, we obtain

-a^{-1}+a^{-1}(x^{-1})^{p-1}+(x^{-1})^{p}=0,

which implies
\Tr_{L/K}(x^{-1})=-a^{-1}
. We also have
-x^{p-1}=-1-ax^{-1}
, yielding

(3.7)

\displaystyle\Tr(-x^{p-1})=\Tr(-1-ax^{-1})=-\Tr(1)-a\Tr(x^{-1})=-p+1=1.

Define
b\coloneqq-x^{p-1}
. In characteristic
p
,
\Tr(b^{p})=\Tr(b)^{p}
holds, and thus

\Tr(b^{p}-b)=\Tr(b^{p})-\Tr(b)=\Tr(b)^{p}-\Tr(b)=1-1=0.

Hence, we can apply (additive) Hilbert 90 to find
c\in L
such that

(3.8)

b^{p}-b=\sigma(c)-c

for some generator
\sigma
of
\Gal(L/K)
.
We claim that the Artin-Schreier extension
L(y)/L
, where
y
is a root of
X^{p}-X-c
, is the desired
C_{p^{2}}
-extension of
K
.
Indeed,
M\coloneqq L(y)
over
K
has degree
p^{2}
and is Galois by (
3.8
). So we are left to prove
\Gal(M/K)\not\cong C_{p}\times C_{p}
. Assume the contrary. By the Galois correspondence, there exists a
C_{p}
-extension
L^{\prime}\neq L
over
K
contained in
M
. By Artin-Schreier theory,
L^{\prime}=K(z)
where
z^{p}-z-d=0
for some
d\in K
. By construction, we have
L(z)=M
. Again, Artin-Schreier theory tells us that
c
and
d
generate the same
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-subspace in
L/\wp(L)
, so
c=id+e^{p}-e
for some
i\in\{1,\ldots,p-1\}
and
e\in L
. Therefore,

b^{p}-b=\sigma(c)-c=(\sigma(id)+\sigma(e)^{p}-\sigma(e))-(id+e^{p}-e)=(\sigma(e)-e)^{p}-(\sigma(e)-e).

In particular,
\sigma(e)-e=b+j
for some
j\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
. However,

\Tr(\sigma(e)-e)=0\neq 1=1+pj=\Tr(b)+\Tr(j)=\Tr(b+j),

by (
3.7
), which yields a contradiction.
∎

As indicated by Idea
3.21
, there is a
p
-adic field that admits a
C_{p}
-extension that does not embed into any
C_{p^{2}}
-extension. We will give an explicit example in Lemma
3.27
. To facilitate the construction, we will first give an equivalent criterion in the presence of a primitive
p
-th root of unity.

Lemma 3.26
.

Assume
K
is a field with
\charK K\neq p
and
\zeta_{p}\in K
. Let
L/K
be a
C_{p}
-extension. Then
L
can be embedded into a
C_{p^{2}}
-extension
M/K
if and only if
\zeta_{p}\in\im N_{L/K}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
. Assume that
L
can be embedded into a
C_{p^{2}}
-extension
M/K
. Then
M/L
is Galois with
\Gal(M/L)\cong C_{p}
. Therefore,
M=L(\sqrt[p]{d})
for some
d\in L^{\times}\setminus L^{\times p}
by Kummer theory. Let
\sigma
be a generator of
\Gal(L/K)
. As
L(\sqrt[p]{d})=L(\sqrt[p]{\sigma(d)})
, Kummer theory implies
\sigma(d)\in\langle d\rangle L^{\times p}
. Replacing
\sigma
by an appropriate power if necessary, we may assume
\sigma(d)\in dL^{\times p}
without loss of generality.
Let
e\in L^{\times}
be such that
\sigma(d)d^{-1}=e^{p}
. Then

(N_{L/K}(e))^{p}=N_{L/K}(e^{p})=N_{L/K}\left(\frac{\sigma(d)}{d}\right)=\frac{\sigma(d)}{d}\frac{\sigma(\sigma(d))}{\sigma(d)}\cdots\frac{\sigma^{p}(d)}{\sigma^{p-1}(d)}=1.

It suffices to show that
N_{L/K}(e)\neq 1
. If instead
N_{L/K}(e)=1
, then we could find
f\in L^{\times}
such that
e=\sigma(f)f^{-1}
by Hilbert 90. Thus,

\sigma(d)d^{-1}=e^{p}=\left(\sigma(f)f^{-1}\right)^{p}\Longrightarrow\sigma\left(df^{-p}\right)=df^{-p},

which implies
df^{-p}\in K
. But then
K(\sqrt[p]{df^{-p}})=K(\sqrt[p]{d}/f)\neq L
would be yet another
C_{p}
-extension of
K
contained in
M
, which is impossible by the Galois correspondence for
M/K
.

\Leftarrow
. The line of reasoning is very similar, but with inverted order. Assume that
N_{L/K}(e)=\zeta_{p}
. Then
N_{L/K}(e^{p})=(N_{L/K}(e))^{p}=1
, so there exists some
d\in L^{\times}
such that
\sigma(d)d^{-1}=e^{p}
. We claim that
d\notin L^{\times p}
. For otherwise, we could write
d=f^{p}
, so that

e^{p}=\sigma(d)d^{-1}=\left(\sigma(f)f^{-1}\right)^{p}\Longrightarrow e=\sigma(f)f^{-1}\zeta_{p}^{i}\phantom{a}

for some
i\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
, which implies

\zeta_{p}=N_{L/K}(e)=N_{L/K}\left(\sigma(f)f^{-1}\right)N_{L/K}(\zeta_{p}^{i})=1,

a contradiction.
Thus,
M\coloneqq L(\sqrt[p]{d})
is indeed a
C_{p}
-extension of
L
. Moreover, using
\sigma(d)=de^{p}
, we see that
L(\sqrt[p]{d})=L(\sqrt[p]{\sigma(d)})
, so
M/K
is a Galois extension of degree
p^{2}
(as
M
contains all conjugates of
\sqrt[p]{d}
). Note that
\Gal(M/K)
is either isomorphic to
C_{p^{2}}
or
C_{p}\times C_{p}
. We are left to exclude the latter case. So assume for a contradiction that
\Gal(M/K)\cong C_{p}\times C_{p}
. On account of the Galois correspondence, there exists a
C_{p}
-extension
L^{\prime}\neq L
of
K
inside
M
. By Kummer theory,
L^{\prime}=K(\sqrt[p]{c})
for some
c\in K
and
L(\sqrt[p]{c})=M
. Yet again, this means that
c
and
d
generate the same subgroup in
L^{\times}/L^{\times p}
. Thus,
d=c^{i}f^{p}
with
i\in\{1,\ldots,p-1\}
and
f\in L^{\times}
. But then

e^{p}=\sigma(d)d^{-1}=\frac{\sigma(c^{i}f^{p})}{c^{i}f^{p}}=\left(\sigma(f)f^{-1}\right)^{p},

contradicting our earlier observation that this equality cannot hold.
∎

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
p\neq 2
be a prime. Consider
K=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})
with uniformiser
\pi=\zeta_{p}-1
. Then,
L=K(\sqrt[p]{(1-p)\pi})
is a
C_{p}
-extension of
K
that is not contained in any
C_{p^{2}}
-extension.

Moreover,
L=\mathbb{Q}_{2}(\sqrt{-2})
is a quadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
not contained in any
C_{4}
-extension.

Proof.

We write
c\coloneqq 1-p\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
and let
\Pi\coloneqq\sqrt[p]{c\pi}
be a root of the Eisenstein polynomial
X^{p}-c\pi
. A fortiori, this is a uniformiser of
L
. By Lemma
3.26
, we only need to verify that
\zeta_{p}\notin\im N_{L/K}
. Since
K^{\times p}\subseteq\im N_{L/K}
, it suffices to show that the induced map

\overline{N_{L/K}}:L^{\times}/L^{\times p}\longrightarrow K^{\times}/K^{\times p}

does not contain
\zeta_{p}K^{\times p}
in its image. We will determine the image by direct calculation.

From Lemma
3.14
, we know that

R=\bigl\{\Pi,1+\Pi,1+\Pi^{2},\ldots,1+\Pi^{p^{2}}\bigr\}

is a set of representatives generating
L^{\times}/L^{\times p}
.
Consider an element
x_{k}=1+\Pi^{k}
for
p\nmid k
.
Note that
x_{k}
has minimal polynomial
(X-1)^{p}-(c\pi)^{k}
, and thus

N_{L/K}(x_{k})=1+(c\pi)^{k}.

For
k\geq p+1
, the norms
N_{L/K}(x_{k})=1+(c\pi)^{k}
already lie in
K^{\times p}
by Lemma
3.13
. Therefore,
\im\overline{N_{L/K}}
is generated by the set of representatives

S=\{c\pi,1+c\pi,\ldots,1+(c\pi)^{p-1}\}.

Hence,
\im\overline{N_{L/K}}\subseteq K^{\times}/K^{\times p}
is a subgroup of index
p
(by the second part of Lemma
3.14
).

Assume towards a contradiction that
\zeta_{p}=1+\pi\in\im N_{L/K}
. By Lemma
3.12
,

1+c\pi=1+\pi-p\pi\equiv 1+\pi+\pi^{p}\equiv 1+\pi+c^{p}\pi^{p}\pmod{\pi^{p+1}}

and hence, by Remark
3.10
,

\frac{1+c\pi}{1+\pi}\equiv\frac{1+\pi+c^{p}\pi^{p}}{1+\pi}\equiv 1+(c\pi)^{p}\pmod{U_{K}^{(p+1)}}.

Note that, by Lemma
3.13
,
U_{K}^{(p+1)}\subseteq K^{\times p}
. But this implies that
\overline{1+(c\pi)^{p}}
lies in the image of
\overline{N_{L/K}}
. This is impossible, since
S\cup\{1+(c\pi)^{p}\}
represents a basis of
K^{\times}/K^{\times p}
.

For
K=\mathbb{Q}_{2}
and
L=\mathbb{Q}_{2}(\sqrt{-2})
, we have
\im\overline{N_{L/K}}=\langle\overline{2},\overline{3}\rangle
. Therefore,
-1\notin\im N_{L/K}
, for otherwise
\mathbb{Q}_{2}^{\times}/\mathbb{Q}_{2}^{\times 2}=\langle\overline{2},\overline{3},\overline{5}\rangle=\langle\overline{2},\overline{3},\overline{-1}\rangle\subseteq\overline{N_{L/K}}
.
∎

3.4.
Galois characterisation of henselianity

The main result of this section provides a method for recovering certain henselian valuations—those we shall call
tamely branching
—from absolute Galois groups. We begin by stating the result. After giving some historical context, we motivate the definition of tamely branching valuations and then proceed to the proof.

Definition 3.28
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field and
p
any prime. We say
v
is
tamely branching at
p
if

(1)

\charK Kv\neq p
;

(2)

vK\neq pvK
;

(3)

p^{2}\mid|G_{Kv}|
, whenever
(vK:pvK)=p
.

Theorem 3.29

(Galois characterisation of henselianity)
.

A field
K
admits a henselian valuation, tamely branching at
p
, if and only if
G_{K}
has a Sylow
p
-subgroup
P\not\cong C_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rtimes C_{2}
that contains a non-trivial normal abelian closed subgroup
N
.

Recovering valuations lies at the very heart of extracting arithmetic information from Galois-theoretic data. Both Neukirch’s Theorem
1.2
and Pop’s Theorem
1.7
crucially rely on this principle. For example, Neukirch’s Theorem builds on the following result
[
42
, Satz 1]
, which can be viewed as an early precursor to the full Galois-theoretic characterisation of henselian valued fields.

Theorem 3.30

(Neukirch 1968)
.

Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{\alg}
be a field such that

(1)

G_{K}
is pro-solvable and

(2)

the Brauer group
\operatorname{Br}(K)
is not a
p
-group for any prime
p
and not a
\{2,3\}
-group (i.e., a group where each element has order
2^{n}3^{m}
).

Then
K
admits a henselian valuation.

Pop extended Neukirch’s result
[
44
, Satz 1.13]
, keeping the assumption of pro-solvability of
G_{K}
, but replacing condition (ii) with a more general one. Incidentally, in
[
30
]
, this generalised result was used to show that fields
K
with
G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
admit a henselian valuation, leading to a complete proof of our Main Theorem. Nevertheless, the full Galois characterisation of henselianity, as stated above, had not yet been established.

Further progress was made in
[
18
]
, where the authors proved a
p
-henselian analogue of Theorem
3.29
, extending earlier results in
[
19
,
16
]
for the case
p=2
. A criterion for henselian valuations was later formulated in
[
31
]
, although it required the additional assumption that the Sylow
p
-subgroups of
G_{K}
are solvable. This restriction was subsequently removed in
[
32
]
via the“henseling down” techniques discussed in Section
2.3.4
. The result and its proof were also presented in Engler and Prestel’s exposition
[
20
, Sec. 5.4]
, which our treatment here generally follows.
We adapt some of their arguments for clarity.

Before turning to the proof of Theorem
3.29
, we will motivate Definition
3.28
by indicating why we can only expect to recover tamely branching henselian valuations from the absolute Galois group.

Remark 3.31
.

(1)

Let
k
be a field of characteristic 0 that does not admit a non-trivial henselian valuation, e.g.,
k=\mathbb{Q}
. Further, let
(K,v)
be a henselian valued field with divisible value group
vK
and residue field
Kv=k
, e.g., the field of Puiseux series over
k
(see Example
1.6
).
Then
G_{K}\cong G_{k}
by Facts
2.21
and
2.22
. By our assumption on
k
, we cannot expect to recover
v
from the isomorphism class of
G_{K}
. Therefore, we require
vK
to be non-
p
-divisible for at least one prime
p
, motivating
2
in Definition
3.28
.

(2)

Consider
K=\mathbb{C}(\!(t)\!)
with the
t
-adic valuation. We saw in Example
2.28
that
G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}
for any prime
q
. Therefore, we cannot expect to recover the
t
-adic valuation from the isomorphism class of
G_{K}
. In general, if
\charK Kv\neq p
,
(vK:pvK)=p
, and
p\nmid G_{Kv}
, then
G_{K}
has Sylow
p
-subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. However, this is the absolute Galois group of
\bigcup_{p\nmid n}\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}}
, which admits only the trivial valuation, hence motivating
3
.

(3)

If
p>2
, then
p\mid G_{Kv}
implies
p^{2}\mid G_{Kv}
by the Artin-Schreier Theorem
1.1
. There is yet another issue if
p=2
and
2\mid G_{Kv}
, but
4\nmid G_{Kv}
.
The field
K=\mathbb{R}(\!(t)\!)
, endowed with the
t
-adic valuation
v
, is henselian with
\charK Kv\neq 2
,
(vK:2vK)=2
, and
G_{Kv}\cong C_{2}
.
By Theorem
2.27
, its absolute Galois group is
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\rtimes C_{2}
(which has Sylow 2-subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rtimes C_{2}
). However, it is possible to construct a field
L
that does not admit a non-trivial henselian valuation with
G_{L}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rtimes C_{2}
as follows.
Let
L
be the intersection of two distinct real closures of
\mathbb{Q}
in
\mathbb{Q}^{\alg}
, and let
\sigma
and
\tau
be the involutions in
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
corresponding to the two real closures; together they topologically generate
G_{L}
. By Artin-Schreier, they do not commute and
\sigma\tau
generates an infinite cyclic group
Z
, where
\sigma
acts on
Z
by conjugation via
\sigma\tau\mapsto\tau\sigma=(\sigma\tau)^{-1}
. Hence,
G_{L}=\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle
is isomorphic to
\overline{Z}\rtimes\langle\sigma\rangle
. If we choose the two real closures such that
\sigma
and
\tau
lie in the same Sylow
2
-subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
, then
G_{L}
will be of the form
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rtimes C_{2}
.

Furthermore, note that condition
1
on the characteristic of the residue field allows for a natural choice of the group
N
in Theorem
3.29
(see below).

For the remainder of this section, we use the following notation. Given a field
K
and a closed subgroup
H\leq G_{K}
, we write
\Fix H
for the (absolute) fixed field of
H
inside
K^{\mathrm{sep}}
.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let
K
be a field with a henselian valuation
v
, tamely branching at
p
, and
w
its unique extension to
L\coloneqq\Fix P
, where
P
is Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G_{K}
. Then, the absolute inertia group
I_{L}
is a non-trivial normal abelian closed subgroup of
P
. Moreover,
P\not\cong C_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rtimes C_{2}
.

Proof.

By assumption and Fact
2.21
,
D_{L}=G_{L}=P
and
I_{L}
is a normal closed subgroup of
P
. Since
\charK Kv\neq p
, the absolute ramification subgroup
R_{L}
is trivial by Fact
2.22
. Using Theorem
2.27
, we compute

(3.9)

\displaystyle I_{L}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}wL/pwL}

(3.10)

\displaystyle P=D_{L}

\displaystyle\cong I_{L}\rtimes G_{Lw}.

As
P
is a Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G_{K}
, the extension
L/K
is prime-to-
p
, and hence
p\nmid[Lw:Kv]
and
(wL:pwL)=(vK:pvK)
. Therefore,
w
is tamely branching at
p
as well, so
I_{L}
is indeed non-trivial abelian by (
3.9
) and
P\not\cong C_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rtimes C_{2}
by (
3.10
).
∎

This lemma proves the “only if” part in Theorem
3.29
; we will now focus on the other direction. We prove it in two steps: First, we establish the theorem in the “classical” case where
G_{K}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
(the name stems from the fact that any Sylow
p
-subgroup of
G_{\mathbb{Q}_{q}}
is of the shape
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for
p\neq q
, see Proposition
2.29
). In a second step, we reduce the general case to the “classical” one.

Proposition 3.33

(Classical case)
.

Let
K
be a field with
G_{K}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Assume additionally that
\sqrt{-1}\in K
if
p=2
. Then
K
admits a henselian valuation tamely branching at
p
.

Some of the ideas in the proof of the classical case are inspired by cohomological considerations; as such, they will look similar to those in Section
3.3
. Otherwise, our main technical tool is the notion of rigid elements, as introduced in Section
2.4
(Theorem
2.68
will yield the desired valuation on
K
).

Before we establish the classical case, we prove the following basic lemma, which allows us to pass to finite extensions of
K
without changing the shape of the Galois group.

Lemma 3.34
.

Let
K
be a field with
G_{K}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Then for every finite extension
L/K
, also
G_{L}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
(with possibly different action in the semi-direct product).

Proof.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

be the short exact sequence associated to the semidirect product for
G_{K}
. As
L/K
is a finite extension,
G_{L}
is a closed subgroup of finite index in
G_{K}
. Hence,
\iota^{-1}(G_{L})
and
\pi(G_{L})
will also be closed of finite index in their respective copies of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Therefore,
\iota^{-1}(G_{L})
and
\pi(G_{L})
are themselves isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. The associated short sequence with maps
\iota|_{\iota^{-1}(G_{L})}
and
\pi|_{G_{L}}
remains exact and split, so the claim follows.
∎

Recall that an element
x\in K^{\times}\setminus K^{\times p}
is called
p
-rigid with respect to
K^{\times p}
if

(3.11)

K^{\times p}+xK^{\times p}\subseteq\bigcup_{i=0}^{p-1}x^{i}K^{\times p}.

If all such
x
are
p
-rigid and
(K^{\times}:K^{\times p})>p
, then Theorem
2.68
allows us to create a valuation for the choice of parameters
S=T_{S}=K^{\times p}
. The key idea of the proof is that the right-hand side of (
3.11
) is given by the image of the norm map for
K(\sqrt[p]{x})/K
.

We organise the proof into six steps.

Step 1.

Show
\charK K\neq p
and
\zeta_{p}\in K
. Deduce
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}K^{\times}/K^{\times p}=2
.

Step 2.

Show that every
x\in K\setminus K^{\times p}
is
p
-rigid via Kummer theory.

Step 3.

Create a valuation
v
satisfying
vK\neq pvK
using Theorem
2.68
.

Step 4.

Show that
\charK Kv\neq p
.

Step 5.

Show that
v
is henselian.

Step 6.

Show that
v
is tamely branching at
p
.

Proof of Proposition
3.33
.

We follow the six steps outlined above.

Step 1.

Note that
K
admits precisely two linearly disjoint
C_{p}
-extensions because
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
has rank two.
Suppose for now that
\charK K=p
. Then, by Artin-Schreier theory (Theorem
3.24
),

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}K/\wp(K)=2.

Let
a\wp(K)
and
b\wp(K)
be a basis for
K/\wp(K)
. The splitting fields of
X^{p}-X-a
and
X^{p}-X-b
are then linearly disjoint. If
x
is a root of
X^{p}-X-a
, then
\alpha\coloneqq-x^{p-1}
satisfies
\Tr(\alpha)=1
(as in (
3.7
) in the proof of Lemma
3.25
), where
\Tr=\Tr_{K(x)/K}
.

We claim that the elements
a\alpha
,
b\alpha
, and
b
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linearly independent in
K(x)/\wp(K(x))
. Let
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
be such that

i_{1}a\alpha+i_{2}b\alpha+i_{3}b=y^{p}-y

for some
y\in K(x)
. Taking the trace on both sides of the equation, we obtain

i_{1}a+i_{2}b+p(i_{3}b)=i_{1}a+i_{2}b=\Tr(y^{p})-\Tr(y)=\Tr(y)^{p}-\Tr(y),

which implies
i_{1}=i_{2}=0
by our choice of
a
and
b
. Hence,
y^{p}-y-i_{3}b=0
. By our choice of
b
, this implies
i_{3}=0
, and therefore,
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}K(x)/\wp(K(x))\geq 3
. Recall that Lemma
3.34
tells us that
G_{K(x)}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, which contradicts our initial observation applied to
K(x)
. Therefore,
\charK K\neq p
and
\zeta_{p}\in K^{\sepc}
.

Note that
[K(\zeta_{p}):K]\leq p-1
and the assumption that
G_{K}
is a pro-
p
group imply
\zeta_{p}\in K
. In particular,
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}K^{\times}/K^{\times p}=2
by Kummer theory (Theorem
3.23
).

Step 2.

Let
x\in K^{\times}\setminus K^{\times p}
and set
L\coloneqq K(\sqrt[p]{x})
. Choose two cosets
xK^{\times p}
,
yK^{\times p}
that form an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-basis for
K^{\times}/K^{\times p}
. By Kummer theory,
\sqrt[p]{x}L^{\times p}
and
yL^{\times p}
are linearly independent in
L^{\times}/L^{\times p}
and hence form a basis by Lemma
3.34
. Thus, we may write

L^{\times}=\bigcup_{0\leq i,j<p}(\sqrt[p]{x})^{i}y^{j}L^{\times p}.

For any
a,b\in K^{\times}
, there exist
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
and
z\in L^{\times}
such that

a+b\sqrt[p]{x}=(\sqrt[p]{x})^{i}y^{j}z^{p}.

Note that
a+b\sqrt[p]{x}
has minimal polynomial
(X-a)^{p}-b^{p}x
. Applying the norm map to both sides yields

(-1)^{p-1}N_{L/K}(a+b\sqrt[p]{x})=(-1)^{p-1}a^{p}+xb^{p}=x^{i}y^{jp}N_{L/K}(z)^{p}\in x^{i}K^{\times p}.

This proves the inclusion (
3.11
):
x
is
p
-rigid. (Note that we assumed
\sqrt{-1}\in K
if
p=2
.)

Step 3.

By Step 2, any
x\in K^{\times}\setminus K^{\times p}
is
p
-rigid. Moreover,
(K^{\times}:K^{\times p})=p^{2}>p
. By the creation of valuations from
p
-rigid elements (Theorem
2.68
) for parameters
S=T_{S}=K^{\times p}
, we obtain a non-trivial valuation
v
with
vK\neq pvK
. Additionally, we replace
v
by the coarsening with respect to the maximal
p
-divisible convex subgroup. Then,
v
will not contain any non-trivial
p
-divisible convex subgroups and
v
remains non-
p
-divisible.

Step 4.

We now prove
\charK Kv\neq p
. Assume towards a contradiction that
\charK Kv=p
.
Then,
\Conv(vp)
is a non-trivial convex subgroup. By our choice of
v
, we know that
\Conv(vp)
and hence
(0,vp]\subset vK
is not
p
-divisible. So we may choose an element
x\in K
such that

0<vx\leq vpvx\notin pvK.

In particular,
x\in K^{\times}\setminus K^{\times p}
is
p
-rigid, so there exist
i\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
and
y\in K^{\times}
such that
1+x=x^{i}y^{p}
. Note that
v(1+x)=0
implies

i=0,\ vy=0{,}\text{ and }y\equiv 1\pmod{\mathcal{M}_{v}},

the latter as in (
3.5
). If we write
y=1+z
with
z\in\mathcal{M}_{v}
, then

vx=v((1+z)^{p}-1)\geq\min\{vp+vz,v(z^{p})\}.

But
vx<vp+vz
implies
vx=v(z^{p})
, and hence
vx\in pvK
, contrary to our choice of
x
.

Step 5.

Assume that
v
is not henselian. By Lemma
2.37
, there exists a Galois extension
L/K
of degree
p
with pairwise inequivalent prolongations
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}
of
v
to
L
with
r>1
. By the fundamental equality (Corollary
2.25
),
p=refd
and therefore

e=f=d=1r=p,

where
v_{1},\ldots,v_{p}
are thus immediate, incomparable prolongations (cf. Fact
2.12
). By Kummer theory,
L=K(\sqrt[p]{x})
for some
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
(otherwise, we would have
e=p
). Set
x_{0}\coloneqq\sqrt[p]{x}\notin L^{\times p}
.

Applying the weak approximation theorem (Fact
2.13
), we may find for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
,

(3.12)

x_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{v_{i}}\cap\bigcap_{j\neq i}\mathcal{O}_{v_{j}}^{\times}.

As all
v_{i}
are immediate, we have
v_{i}L=vK
for each
i
.
We may assume without loss of generality, that
v_{i}x_{i}\notin pvK
, as follows: if
v_{i}x_{i}\in pvK
, use the fact that
\Conv(v_{i}x_{i})
is non-trivial to find
y\in K
with
vy\in(0,v_{i}x_{i})
and
vy\notin pvK
(otherwise, as in Step 4,
(0,v_{i}x_{i}]\subset vK
would be
p
-divisible, which cannot happen). Replacing
x_{i}
by
x_{i}+y
, gives the desired element satisfying (
3.12
) and
v_{i}x_{i}\notin pvK
.

We claim that
L(\sqrt[p]{x_{0}},\sqrt[p]{x_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt[p]{x_{p}})/L
has Galois group
C_{p}^{p+1}
. By Kummer theory, it suffices to show that

x_{0}^{\vphantom{i_{p}}i_{0}}x_{1}^{\vphantom{i_{p}}i_{1}}\cdots x_{p}^{i_{p}}\in L^{\times p}\text{ with $i_{0},\ldots,i_{p}\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}$}\Longrightarrow i_{0}=\ldots=i_{p}=0.

Evaluating this product by
v_{j}
yields
i_{j}=0
for every
j=1,\ldots,p
. Recall that
x_{0}\notin L^{\times p}
, so
i_{0}=0
as well.
By Step 1 and Lemma
3.34
, we know that
L
admits at most two linearly disjoint
C_{p}
-extensions, so our construction is impossible. We conclude that
v
must be henselian.

Step 6.

Assume
(vK:pvK)=p
and let
x\in K
be such that
vx\notin pvK
. It remains to verify property
3
, that is,
p^{2}\mid|G_{Kv}|
. If
G_{Kv}=1
, then
\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}=\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times p}
, since
(Kv)^{\times}=(Kv)^{\times p}
and
1+\mathcal{M}_{v}=(1+\mathcal{M}_{v})^{p}
by henselianity. This would imply that there is only one
C_{p}
-extension, namely
K(\sqrt[p]{x})
. Hence
G_{Kv}\neq 1
(and we are done by the Artin-Schreier Theorem
1.1
in the case
p\neq 2
).

Let
L\coloneqq K(\sqrt[p]{y})
, where
y\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
is such that
yv\notin(Kv)^{\times p}
. In particular,
L/K
is unramified of degree
f=p
. As before, we cannot have
G_{Lv}=1
, because
L
admits more than one
C_{p}
-extension. This shows that
p^{2}\mid G_{Kv}
. ∎

From this, we are able to deduce the general case. Our main tool will be “Henseling Down” (Theorem
2.54
). In order to apply this theorem to a tamely branching valuation
v
, we require
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in H(K)
and moreover, that no proper coarsening of
v
has real closed residue field. Conveniently, we may assume this extra condition without loss of generality:

Lemma 3.35

(
[
20
, Lem. 5.4.6]
)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a henselian valued field,
v
tamely branching at
p
. Then there exists a valuation ring in
H(K)
tamely branching at
p
as well.
If
p=2
, we can find a henselian valuation on
K
, tamely branching at
2
, such that furthermore, no proper coarsening has real closed residue field.

Proof.

We only need to consider the case
\mathcal{O}_{v}\in H_{2}(K)
, that is, when
Kv
is separably closed. We claim that the canonical henselian valuation
v_{K}
is tamely branching at
p
. By definition,
v_{K}
is coarser than
v
, so consider the decomposition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v}}

We see that
\charK(Kv_{K})\neq p
. Moreover,
\overline{v}(Kv_{K})
is divisible as
Kv_{K}
is separably closed. Thus,

(v_{K}K:pv_{K}K)=(vK:pvK),

and therefore,
v_{K}K
cannot be
p
-divisible.
Note that since
Kv
is separably closed,
p^{2}\nmid|G_{Kv}|
and hence,
(v_{K}K:pv_{K}K)=(vK:pvK)\neq p
.

For
p=2
, let
w
be the coarsest coarsening of
v
with real closed residue field, which exists by Fact
2.55
. Then,
\mathcal{O}_{w}\in H(K)
automatically satisfies the required property, and it remains to show that
w
is tamely branching at
2
. We proceed as before, with
w
taking the place of
v_{K}
and real closedness of
Kw
playing the role of separable closedness of
Kv_{K}
.
Since
Kw
is real closed, it follows that
\charK(Kw)=0
and that the value group
\overline{v}(Kw)
is divisible (for any
x\in Kw
, we have that
x
or
-x
has a
q
th root for any prime
q
).

Again,
(wK:2wK)=(vK:2vK)
holds as in the previous argument. The assumption that
v
is tamely branching at
2
implies
(vK:2vK)\geq 4
(since
|G_{Kv}|=2
, cf. Fact
2.55
). It follows that
w
is also tamely branching at
2
.
∎

Similarly, we need to ensure that the process of henseling down also preserves the property of being tamely branching at a prime
p
.

Lemma 3.36
.

Let
(L,w)
be a valued field,
w
tamely branching at
p
. If
L/K
is an algebraic extension of fields, then
v=w|_{K}
is tamely branching at
p
as well.

Proof.

Recall Fact
2.11
, which says that
wL/vK
is a torsion group and
Lw/Kv
is an algebraic extension. In particular,
p^{2}\mid G_{Lw}
implies
p^{2}\mid G_{Kv}
.
The rest of the claim follows immediately from the following general fact about abelian groups: if
\Delta\leq\Gamma
are
p
-torsion-free abelian groups such that
\Gamma/\Delta
is a torsion group, then

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\Delta/p\Delta)\geq\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\Gamma/p\Gamma).

See
[
20
, Ex. 5.5.2]
for a proof of this inequality.
∎

Corollary 3.37
.

Let
K
be a field and
p
any prime. Assume
G_{K}
has a Sylow
p
-subgroup
P\not\cong C_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{p},\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rtimes C_{2}
that contains a non-trivial normal abelian closed subgroup
N
. Then
K
admits a henselian valuation, tamely branching at
p
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.1
, we note that
N
does not have torsion elements unless
p=2
and
\sqrt{-1}\notin\Fix N
. However,
K(\sqrt{-1})/K
is a finite extension of degree at most 2. By Henseling Down (Theorem
2.54
(ii)) and Lemma
3.36
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\sqrt{-1}\in K
and
P\not\cong\{1\},\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Hence, we may assume without loss of generality that
N
is torsion-free. Therefore, we can view
N
as a torsion-free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module.

Case 1.
Let us first consider the easier case that
N
is not topologically generated by a single element (i.e.,
N\not\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
), so there exists
N_{0}\vartriangleleft N
with
N_{0}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. By the “classical case” (Proposition
3.33
), there exists a henselian valuation
w
on
M\coloneqq\Fix N_{0}
, tamely branching at
p
, which we may assume to lie in
H(M)
by Lemma
3.35
. Now, we may “hensel down” as indicated by the left diagram.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\sepc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\sepc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\hbox to0.0pt{$\,=\Fix N_{0}$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\hbox to0.0pt{$\,=\Fix N$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fix N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\hbox to0.0pt{$\,=\Fix\langle N,\sigma\rangle$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\hbox to0.0pt{$\;=\Fix P$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L\hbox to0.0pt{$\;=\Fix P$\hss}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
Case 1.
Case 2.
normal
normal
normal
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}\text{max.}\\
\text{prime-to-$\scriptstyle p$}\end{subarray}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{subarray}{c}\text{max.}\\
\text{prime-to-$\scriptstyle p$}\end{subarray}}

More precisely, two applications of Henseling Down (Theorem
2.54
(i) and Lemma
3.36
) show that
w|_{L}
is in
H(L)
and tamely branching at
p
, where
L\coloneqq\Fix P
. By Lemma
3.35
, again, we may additionally assume that
L
admits a henselian valuation
w^{\prime}
, tamely branching at
p
, such that no proper coarsening has real closed residue field if
p=2
. Therefore,
w^{\prime}|_{K}
is the desired valuation by Henseling Down, Theorem
2.54
(iii).

Case 2.
Let us now consider the case that
N\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. As
P\not\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, we may find some
\sigma\in P\setminus N
such that
\langle\sigma\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\langle N,\sigma\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. By Proposition
3.33
, there exists a valuation
w
on
M\coloneqq\Fix\langle N,\sigma\rangle
, tamely branching at
p
, which we may assume to lie in
H(M)
. For
p=2
, we additionally require that
w
does not have any proper coarsenings with real closed residue field. Let
u
denote the unique prolongation of
w
to
F\coloneqq\Fix N
.
If
\mathcal{O}_{u}\in H(F)
, we see that
u|_{K}
is the desired valuation by Henseling Down (as indicated by the right diagram).

It remains to consider the case that
u,v_{F}\in H_{2}(F)
with
\mathcal{O}_{u}\subsetneq\mathcal{O}_{v_{F}}
. By definition,
Fv_{F}
is separably closed,
\charK Fv_{F}\neq p
(as
Fu
is a residue field of
Fv_{F}
), and
v_{F}F
is not
p
-divisible (otherwise
N=G_{F}
would be trivial). Further,
\mathcal{O}_{u}\subsetneq\mathcal{O}_{v_{F}}
implies
\mathcal{O}_{w}\subsetneq\mathcal{O}_{v_{F}|_{M}}
(Fact
2.12
), therefore,
\mathcal{O}_{v_{F}|_{M}}\in H_{1}(M)
since
\mathcal{O}_{w}\in H(M)
. By Henseling Down,
u^{\prime}\coloneqq v_{F}|_{L}
lies in
H(L)
and is tamely branching at
p
(since
\charK Fv_{F}\neq p
,
v_{F}F\neq pv_{F}F
, and
Mv_{F}|_{M}
is not separably closed and
\sqrt{-1}\in Lu^{\prime}
).
Finally, applying Henseling Down to
L/K
shows that
v_{F}|_{K}=u^{\prime}|_{K}
is henselian and tamely branching at
p
.
∎

This completes the proof of the Galois characterisation of henselianity (Theorem
3.29
).

4.
Transfer between fields of different characteristic

We pursue two goals in this chapter. First, we give two separate proofs of the
Transfer Lemma
, which will be a key component in the proof of the Main Theorem.

Transfer Lemma
.

Let
(K,v)
be a henselian valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
.
Assume that

(1)

G_{K}
is small;

(2)

vK=\Conv(vp)
and
vK
is
p
-divisible.

Then, there exists a field
F
of positive characteristic
p
such that
G_{K}\cong G_{F}
.

Secondly, we aim to provide a systematic treatment of the Saturation-Decomposition Method—a non-standard (i.e., model-theoretic) transfer method for valued fields.
We give some general background on transfer methods in Section
4.1
.

The original, model-theoretic strategy for proving the Transfer Lemma was a precursor to the Saturation-Decomposition Method for non-discrete valuations. In his Habilitation, the second author asked for a purely algebraic proof of the Transfer Lemma, noting that the lemma

“is one of those rare examples of ‘applied model theory’, where the only proof known so far uses model theory in an essential way.”
[
33
, p. 24]

In
[
33
]
, all arguments were based on the smallness of the absolute Galois group
G_{K}
, which obscured the connection to the truncated valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
, the algebraic object at the heart of transfer from mixed characteristic to equal positive characteristic.

We give a new proof of the Transfer Lemma based on the
tilting construction
as presented by Scholze
[
48
]
. We should note that apart from its definition and most basic properties, we only use the Fontaine-Wintenberger Theorem
4.8
, for which we will provide the necessary background in Section
4.2
.

Motivated by the theory of perfectoid fields, Jahnke and Kartas developed and greatly generalised the Saturation-Decomposition Method in
[
25
]
, further extending it to include non-standard treatments of perfectoid fields, the Fontaine-Wintenberger Theorem, and almost mathematics.

With this in mind, what we present in Section
4.3
as the Saturation-Decomposition Method is, in fact, a hybrid between
[
33
]
and
[
25
]
. Apart from changing emphasis, we prove an optimal version of Jahnke and Kartas’ “Taming Theorem”
[
25
, 1.5]
—we show that it holds precisely for the class of roughly deeply ramified (rdr) fields in the sense of Gabber/Kuhlmann-Rzepka (Theorem
4.23
). The Almost Purity Theorem for rdr fields (Corollary
4.26
) and the Transfer Lemma (Corollary
4.28
) follow as immediate corollaries.

In Section
4.4
, we briefly explain what extra ingredients are needed to recover the theorems of Jahnke-Kartas and how their work exemplifies the above-mentioned method. This will complete our picture of transfer, as sketched in the thematic diagram on p.
4.1.2
.
We hope that our presentation shows that the arithmetic and model-theoretic proofs of the Transfer Lemma are two sides of the same coin, and that model theory brings more than just a new language to the table.

4.1.
Two methods of transfer

The purpose of the Transfer Lemma is to exhibit an isomorphism of absolute Galois groups between fields of characteristic 0 and characteristic
p
. More generally,
transfer
is achieved when arithmetic properties or invariants can be translated between fields of different characteristics. Usually, both fields in question belong to a common family, e.g., local/global fields.
In many natural settings, transfer can be observed.

There are at least two rigorous settings for transfer—both of which are asymptotic in nature—as is indicated by their respective slogans “
p\rightarrow\infty
” and “
e\rightarrow\infty
”, see
[
49
]
.

4.1.1.
p\rightarrow\infty

Consider the local fields
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)
with
p
varying over the set of primes.
Ax and Kochen
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
established the following rigorous transfer principle: any first-order property
\varphi
(in the language of rings) holds in
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
if and only if it holds in
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)
for all
p
sufficiently large. In short, there is a bound
N(\varphi)\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbb{Q}_{p}\models\varphi\Longleftrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)\models\varphi\quad\text{for all $p\geq N(\varphi)$}.

Indeed, this transfer principle was one of the main goals of their series of papers. Ax and Kochen were motivated by a concrete question of Artin: does any homogeneous polynomial over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
of degree
d\in\mathbb{N}
in
n>d^{2}
variables have a non-trivial solution? Any field for which this statement holds is said to have the
C_{2}
-property
. Artin expected a positive answer since a result of Lang says that
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)
is
C_{2}
