for all primes
p
. The Ax-Kochen transfer described above implies that the
p
-adic fields
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
are “asymptotically”
C_{2}
, that is to say, for any
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a bound
N(d)\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
p\geq N(d)
, all
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
satisfies the
C_{2}
-property for homogeneous polynomials of degree
\leq d
(this is a first-order property for any given
d
). It is known that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is not
C_{2}
for any prime
p
.

Results obtained from this type of transfer are necessarily
asymptotic
in nature. Looking at the proof of Ax-Kochen transfer, this is to be expected, as it uses the existence of non-principal ultrafilters in a crucial way. Essentially, Ax-Kochen transfer is a formal consequence of the elementary equivalence of the ultraproducts

(4.1)

\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\mbox{\larger\big/}\mathcal{U}\equiv\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)\mbox{\larger\big/}\mathcal{U}\eqqcolon\mathcal{S}

for any non-principal ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on the set of primes
\mathbb{P}
. This is because ultraproducts allow one to interpolate first-order properties: a first-order property
\varphi
holds for almost all fields in the families
\{\mathbb{Q}_{p}\}_{p\in\mathbb{P}}
or
\{\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)\}_{p\in\mathbb{P}}
if and only if
\varphi
holds in the corresponding ultraproduct valued field
\mathcal{Q}
or
\mathcal{S}
(Łoś’s theorem).

The fact that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
and
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)
have the same value group and residue field implies that the same is true for
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{S}
—their value groups and residue fields are isomorphic to

\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{Z}\mbox{\larger\big/}\mathcal{U}\prod_{p\in\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{F}_{p}\mbox{\larger\big/}\mathcal{U},

respectively.
The elementary equivalence of
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{S}
in (
4.1
) is then a special case of the following theorem that lies at the heart of the model theory of valued fields:

Theorem 4.1

(Ax-Kochen-Ershov)
.

Let
(K,v)
and
(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
be two henselian valued fields of equicharacteristic
(0{,}0)
. Then:

(K,v)\equiv(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow vK\equiv v^{\prime}K^{\prime}Kv\equiv K^{\prime}v^{\prime}.

4.1.2.
e\rightarrow\infty

In contrast to the previous method, let
p
now be a fixed prime.
Krasner
[
35
]
, Kazhdan
[
27
]
, and Deligne
[
13
]
observed that by increasing the absolute ramification index
e=v(p)
of a local field
F
, it more and more resembles a positive characteristic local field
\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)
.
The simplest instance of this phenomenon is, perhaps, the following observation of Kazhdan’s:

Fact 4.2
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be a totally ramified extension of degree
e=e(F/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. If
\pi
is a uniformiser in
F
, then

\mathcal{O}_{F}/(\pi^{e})=\mathcal{O}_{F}/(p)\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}[t]/(t^{e}).

If
e
is a power of
p
, the right-hand side can be written isomorphically as
\mathbb{F}_{p}[t^{1/e}]/(t)
.

For a simple proof of this fact, see
[
26
, Lem. 6.1.3]
. We lose the dependence on
e
by passing to a direct limit. For example, if we adjoin a compatible system of
p
-power roots

p^{1/p^{\infty}}=\bigl\{p^{1/p^{n}}\bigr\}_{n\geq 1}

to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, i.e., setting
K=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(p^{1/p^{\infty}})
, this yields

\mathcal{O}_{K}/(p)\cong\varinjlim\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(p^{1/p^{n}})}/(p)\cong\varinjlim\mathbb{F}_{p}\bigl[t^{1/p^{n}}\bigr]\mbox{\larger\big/}(t)\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}[t]^{1/p^{\infty}}/(t).

Examining the right-hand side, we see that the Frobenius map
x\mapsto x^{p}
is surjective. By taking an inverse limit with the Frobenius map acting as the transition maps, one recovers an integral domain that is the valuation ring of an algebraic extension of
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)
.

\mathbb{F}_{p}\llbracket t\rrbracket^{1/p^{\infty}}\cong\varprojlim_{x\mapsto x^{p}}\Bigl(\mathbb{F}_{p}[t]^{1/p^{\infty}}/(t)\Bigr),

This is the valuation ring of the
t
-adic valuation on the perfect hull
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)^{1/p^{\infty}}
of
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)
. The latter is what is called the
tilt
of
K
.
Fontaine and Wintenberger furthermore showed that for this construction, one gets a canonical isomorphism of absolute Galois groups between
K
and its tilt:

(4.2)

G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(p^{1/p^{\infty}})}\cong G_{\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)^{1/p^{\infty}}}.

In
[
48
, Thm. 1.3]
, Scholze vastly generalised this isomorphism to an equivalence of categories over perfectoid fields, algebras, and spaces and their respective tilts. We will only use the Fontaine-Wintenberger Theorem for perfectoid fields, which we explain in the next section.

In a completely distinct fashion, Koenigsmann
[
33
]
constructed an absolutely unramified
(0,p)
-place
\varphi:K\longrightarrow F
for certain valued fields
K
with small absolute Galois group,
p
-divisible value group, and that are sufficiently
7
7

7

For example, it suffices to consider non-principal ultrapowers of valued fields—these are
\aleph_{1}
-saturated.
saturated (in the sense of model theory). By ramification theory, this means that
\varphi
induces an isomorphism

G_{K}\cong G_{F}

of absolute Galois groups of fields of characteristic 0 and
p
. This was a new instance of transfer for valued field with “infinite” absolute ramification index
e=v(p)
. By this, we mean that the interval
(0,vp]\subset vK
has infinitely many points because
vK
is
p
-divisible.

As we have mentioned before, the Saturation-Decomposition Method, within the formalism of Jahnke-Kartas (Sections
4.3
and
4.4
), will link these two interpretations of “
e\rightarrow\infty
”.
To summarise, this chapter roughly follows the outline of the following thematic diagram.

Perfectoid fields

(concrete) infinitely

ramified valued fields

Abs. Galois groups
Model theory

Scholze

2012

Jahnke-Kartas

2025

Fontaine-

Wintenberger

1979

Koenigsmann

2004

4.2.
Perfectoid fields

In this section, we consider valued fields
(K,v)
that are complete and of rank 1 (this is the familiar setup of algebraic number theory). This is essential because the constructions involved are analytic in nature. We further assume that
(K,v)
has mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. In particular, the residue map

\res:\mathcal{O}_{v}\longrightarrow Kv,\quad\alpha\longmapsto\overline{\alpha},

reduces elements that live in characteristic 0 to elements that live in characteristic
p
. If we wish to exhibit a structural connection between
K
and
Kv
, it would be pertinent if the residue map had a section. Certainly, it cannot be additive (since the characteristics differ).
However, if
p
is a uniformiser, there is a unique multiplicative section
\tau
of the residue map called the
Teichmüller lift
. Its construction is based on the following basic fact:

Fact 4.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. Then for any
\alpha,\beta\in R
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\alpha\equiv\beta\mod{(p)}\Longrightarrow\alpha^{p^{n}}\equiv\beta^{p^{n}}\mod{\left(p^{n+1}\right)}.

Assume that
p
is a uniformiser. Then the above fact provides a source of canonicity for lifts: if
\alpha
and
\beta
are different representatives of the same residue class in
Kv
, their
p^{n}
-th powers will lie closer and closer together as
n
grows. Following this idea, we define

\tau:Kv\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v},\quad a_{0}\longmapsto\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\alpha_{n}^{p^{n}},\quad\text{where }\alpha_{n}\in\res^{-1}(a_{n}),\ a_{n}\coloneqq a_{0}^{1/p^{n}}=\Phi^{-n}(a_{0}),

and
\Phi
denotes the Frobenius on
Kv
.
Crucially,
K
needs to be complete for the limit to be well-defined. Using the language of inverse limits, we can rewrite this in a more suggestive fashion. The Teichmüller lift
\tau
is given as the composition

(4.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\varprojlim_{\Phi}Kv=\varprojlim\left(\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longleftarrow}}\cdots\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a_{n})_{n\geq 0}={\bigl(a_{0},a_{0}^{1/p},a_{0}^{1/p^{2}},\ldots\bigr)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\alpha_{n}^{p^{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp}

of the canonical isomorphism
\iota
(that sends
a_{0}
to the unique tuple of iterated
p
-th roots) and a map denoted by
\sharp
, which is called the
sharp map
. If we leave aside
\iota
and weaken our assumption on
\Phi
, requiring only surjectivity rather than bijectivity, we are naturally led to the following new objects and definitions:

Definition 4.4

(
[
48
, Def. 3.1]
)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a complete valued field of rank 1 and residue characteristic
p
. We call
K

perfectoid
if the value group is non-discrete and the truncated valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect, i.e., the Frobenius on it is surjective. We write

\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}\coloneqq\displaystyle\varprojlim_{\Phi}\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}=\varprojlim\left(\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longleftarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi}}{{\longleftarrow}}\cdots\right)

for the
tilt
of the valuation ring and
\sharp:\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v}
for the sharp map defined above.

Remark 4.5
.

(1)

Perfectoid fields have a
p
-divisible value group (we will see the short argument presented as Lemma
4.14
) and perfect residue fields since
\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{v}\cong(\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v})_{\Red}
, see Theorem
2.46
3
.

(2)

By definition,
\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}
has a ring structure, whereas
\sharp
is just a multiplicative map.

Example 4.6
.

(1)

Perfectoid fields of characteristic
p
are precisely perfect fields endowed with a complete rank 1 valuation. A basic example is the completion of
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)^{1/p^{\infty}}
.

(2)

Typical examples of perfectoid fields of characteristic
0
are complete valued fields that arise from adjoining a compatible system of
p
-power roots to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. For instance, the completions of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(p^{1/p^{\infty}})
and
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})
,

\widehat{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(p^{1/p^{\infty}})}\widehat{\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})},

are perfectoid. Here,
\zeta_{p^{\infty}}=\{\zeta_{p^{n}}\}_{n\geq 1}
denotes a compatible system of
p^{n}
-th primitive roots of unity.

We summarise the basic properties of the tilt, which, on their own, are not very hard to prove (see the original paper
[
48
, Lem. 3.4]
or Bhatt’s lecture notes
[
10
, Chap. 3.2]
for a more detailed treatment).

Fact 4.7
.

Let
K
be a perfectoid field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Then

(1)

\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}
is a valuation ring. The corresponding valuation on
K^{\flat}\coloneqq\Frac(\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat})
is given by the composition

v^{\flat}:K^{\flat}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sharp}}{{\longrightarrow}}K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Gamma_{v}\cup\{\infty\},

where
\sharp
is extended multiplicatively to the quotient field. Moreover, any element
x^{\sharp}
in the image of
\sharp
admits a compatible system of
p^{n}
-th roots
(x^{1/p^{n}})^{\sharp}
.

(2)

There exists
t\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}
with
v(t^{\sharp})=vp
such that
\sharp
induces an isomorphism

\overline{\sharp}:\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}/t\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}\cong\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}.

(3)

(K^{\flat},v^{\flat})
is a perfectoid field of characteristic
p
, and it has the same value group and residue field as
K
.

The main Galois-theoretic theorem about perfectoid fields due to Kedlaya-Liu and Scholze, generalising (
4.2
), is the following:

Theorem 4.8

(Generalised Fontaine-Wintenberger)
.

For any perfectoid field
K
, we have

G_{K}\cong G_{K^{\flat}}.

4.2.1.
Direct proof of the Transfer Lemma

The following lemma—the proof of which will be genuinely valuation-theoretic—is a useful source for perfectoid fields. The Transfer Lemma follows as an immediate corollary.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
such that

(1)

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}K^{\times}/(K^{\times})^{p}<\infty
;

(2)

the value group
vK
is
p
-divisible and of rank 1;

(3)

the residue field
Kv
is perfect.

Then,
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect. In particular, the completion of
(K,v)
is perfectoid.

Proof.

Let
(\widehat{K},\hat{v})
be the completion of
(K,v)
. The second part of the conclusion follows immediately from

\displaystyle\hat{v}\widehat{K}

\displaystyle\cong vK,

\displaystyle\widehat{K}\hat{v}

\displaystyle\cong Kv,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{O}_{\hat{v}}/p\mathcal{O}_{\hat{v}}.

To prove that
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect, fix any
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
. We will define a sequence of increasingly better approximations to a
p
-th root of
x
.

Let
\{a_{1}(K^{\times})^{p},\ldots,a_{n}(K^{\times})^{p}\}
be a set of representatives for
K^{\times}/(K^{\times})^{p}
. Using the condition that
vK
is
p
-divisible, we can assume without loss of generality that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\times}
. We may further assume
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in 1+\mathcal{M}_{v}
, again without loss of generality, since
Kv
is perfect. Set
\gamma\in vK
to be the value

\gamma\coloneqq\min_{1\leq i\leq n}v(a_{i}-1)>0.

Using that
vK
is archimedean, we choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
m\gamma\geq vp
.

We can now construct our approximations
8
8

8

We found that a similar approximation argument was used in
[
37
, Prop 4.4]
.
to a
p
-th root of
x
. Choose
y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{m}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
and
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{m}\in\{a_{1}-1,\ldots,a_{n}-1\}
inductively such that

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=y_{1}^{p}(1+z_{1})

\displaystyle z_{i}

\displaystyle=y_{i+1}^{p}(1+z_{i+1})

for
i=1,\ldots,m-1
. Therefore, we obtain the sum decomposition

x=y_{1}^{p}(1+y_{2}^{p}(1+\ldots(1+z_{m})\ldots))=y_{1}^{p}+y_{1}^{p}y_{2}^{p}+\ldots+y_{1}^{p}y_{2}^{p}\cdots y_{m}^{p}+y_{1}^{p}y_{2}^{p}\cdots y_{m}^{p}z_{m}.

By construction,

v(y_{1}^{p}y_{2}^{p}\cdots y_{m}^{p}z_{m})=v(x)+v(z_{1})+v(z_{2})+\ldots+v(z_{m})\geq m\gamma\geq vp,

so we have
y_{1}^{p}y_{2}^{p}\cdots y_{m}^{p}z_{m}\in p\mathcal{O}_{v}
. The Frobenius map (which is additive on
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
) thus sends

y_{1}+y_{1}y_{2}+\ldots+y_{1}y_{2}\cdots y_{m}

to our chosen
x
modulo
p
.
∎

Corollary 4.10

(Transfer Lemma)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a henselian valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Assume that

(1)

G_{K}
is small;

(2)

vK=\Conv(vp)
and
vK
is
p
-divisible.

Then, there exists a field
F
of positive characteristic
p
such that
G_{K}\cong G_{F}
.

Proof.

Smallness of
G_{K}
implies that
\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}K^{\times}/(K^{\times})^{p}<\infty
by Lemma
3.19
.
Consider the standard coarsening
v_{p}
, which, by our assumptions on
vK
, has
p
-divisible value group
v_{p}K
of rank 1. By Pop’s Lemma
3.20
(i),
Kv_{p}
is perfect. Therefore,
(K,v_{p})
satisfies all the conditions of the preceding lemma, making
(\widehat{K},\widehat{v_{p}})
a perfectoid field. Hence

G_{\vphantom{\widehat{K}}K}\cong G_{\widehat{K}}\cong G_{\widehat{K}^{\flat}}

by Proposition
2.34
and the Generalised Fontaine-Wintenberger Theorem. So we may choose
F=\widehat{K}^{\flat}
as our characteristic
p
field.
∎

For the purpose of proving our Main Theorem, this is all we need. In particular, no saturated models were involved in the construction of
F
— instead, we provided a purely arithmetic description via completions and tilts.

4.3.
The Saturation-Decomposition Method

For the rest of the chapter, we will assume familiarity with the basic concepts of model theory (such as theories, types, saturation, and ultraproducts).

To the best of our knowledge, the Saturation-Decomposition Method originates in Kochen’s individual account
[
29
]
of his work with Ax. The idea of the method is roughly the following:

Slogan
.

Given a valued field
(K,v)
, study its structure in two steps:

(1)

Saturate
by passing to an extension with strong closure properties whilst preserving all elementary properties of the valued field (i.e., pass to a saturated elementary extension).

(2)

Decompose
the saturated extension into places that can each be analysed individually.

4.3.1.
The discrete case

We start with a classic example. The theorem below characterises the first-order theory of
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},v_{p})
. This serves a dual purpose: it is the simplest example of the Saturation-Decomposition Method at work, and, at the same time, it characterises fields elementarily equivalent to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
via four natural axioms. It is precisely these four axioms that we will have to check in the proof of the Main Theorem.

Theorem 4.11
.

A valued field
(K,v)
is elementary equivalent to
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},v_{p})
if and only if

(1)

(K,v)
is henselian of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
;

(2)

vp
is minimal positive;

(3)

vK\equiv\mathbb{Z}
;

(4)

Kv=\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Proof.

Properties (i)–(iv) clearly hold in
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},v_{p})
and it is not difficult to verify that (i)–(iv) can be expressed by a set of first-order sentences. Any valued field elementarily equivalent to
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},v_{p})
will thus satisfy (i)–(iv) as well.

It now suffices to show that (i)–(iv) form a complete theory. To this end, let
(K^{*},v^{*})
and
(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
be two
\aleph_{1}
-saturated models of (i)–(iv). Our aim is to show
(K^{*},v^{*})\equiv(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
. First, decompose
v^{*}
and
v^{\prime}
(Remark
2.47
):
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{*}v^{*}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\prime}v^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{*}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v^{\prime}}}
\mathrel{\vtop{$\cong$\cr\hfil{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\rule[0.77496pt]{1.00006pt}{6.02773pt}\hskip 1.99997pt\rule[0.77496pt]{1.00006pt}{6.02773pt}}\hfil\cr}}
=

Properties (ii) and (iii) imply that
\overline{v^{*}}
and
\overline{v^{\prime}}
have value groups isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
, whereas the value groups of
v^{*}_{0}
and
v^{\prime}_{0}
are non-trivial and divisible (cf. Theorem
2.46
1
and Fact
5.2
). In particular, the completeness of the theory of non-trivial divisible ordered abelian groups implies
v^{*}_{0}K^{*}\equiv v^{\prime}_{0}K^{\prime}
. Moreover, saturation implies that
(K^{*}v^{*}_{0},\overline{v^{*}})
and
(K^{\prime}v^{\prime}_{0},\overline{v^{\prime}})
are complete. By a well-known structure theorem (
[
51
, Chap. II, §5.3]
),
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is the only complete valued field with value group
\mathbb{Z}
, minimal positive value
vp
, and residue field
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, up to isomorphism. Therefore,
K^{*}v^{*}_{0}\cong K^{\prime}v^{\prime}_{0}
. By the Ax-Kochen-Ershov Theorem
4.1
, we conclude

(K^{*},v^{*}_{0})\equiv(K^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{0}).

While
v^{*}_{0}
and
v^{\prime}_{0}
are not the valuations we started with, note that
\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}
are quantifier-free definable via J. Robinson’s formula

(4.4)

\varphi(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\exists y\,(1+2x^{3}=y^{3})&\text{if $p=2$}\\
\exists y\,(1+px^{2}=y^{2})&\text{if $p\neq 2$},\end{cases}

so
K^{*}\equiv K^{\prime}
(as rings) implies
(K^{*},v^{*})\equiv(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
by Fact
2.69
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

It might be tempting to consider just one saturated model
(K^{*},v^{*})
and compare its theory to
(\mathbb{Q}_{p},v_{p})
directly. This does not work, however, since the (0, ​0)-place in the Standard Decomposition is trivial and thus not elementarily equivalent to
v^{*}_{0}
. Working instead with the field of Puiseux series over
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
as a “canonical model” of (i)–(iv) would do the trick.

Remark 4.13
.

This method is the standard approach to reducing problems in mixed characteristic
(0,p)
to equicharacteristic (0, ​0) in model theory and remains widely used. For the above strategy, it is crucial that we are able to determine the core field via structure theorems for Witt rings or, more generally, Cohen rings (for the case the residue field is imperfect, see
[
2
]
). Note that in the above proof of Theorem
4.11
, we used the structure theorem for the Witt ring over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. These tools only become available if the core field is
\mathbb{Z}
-valued, or equivalently, if the original valued field is finitely ramified (the interval
(0,vp]\subset vK
contains finitely many points).
The strongest model-theoretic results one could hope for were established in
[
1
]
, which handles this general case (henselian, finitely ramified valued fields).

4.3.2.
The non-discrete case

The case when
(0,vp]\subset vK
has infinitely many points is handled quite differently. Here, it is not the core field that plays the central role, but rather the positive characteristic residue field
Kv_{p}
(from the Standard Decomposition
2.47
).
The key assumption we need to handle this case is the semi-perfectness of
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
.

We now present a model-theoretic criterion for semi-perfectness. The proof will use a preservation lemma along the Standard Decomposition.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. If
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect and
vp
is not minimal positive, then
\Conv(vp)
is
p
-divisible.

Proof.

Use the following trick (cf.
[
48
, Lem. 3.2]
): whenever

x\equiv y^{p}\pmod{p\mathcal{O}_{v}},\quad 0\leq vx<vp,

we must have
vx=p\cdot vy
. Hence, the interval
[0,vp)\subset vK
is
p
-divisible. Moreover,
vp\in pvK
, which can be seen by writing
vp=\gamma+(vp-\gamma)
for some
\gamma\in(0,vp)
.
But
[0,vp]
generates all of
\Conv(vp)
, which must then be
p
-divisible as well.
∎

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Assume that
vp
is not minimal positive in its archimedean class (equivalently,
v_{p}p
is not minimal positive in
v_{p}K
). Let
v=\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\circ\overline{v_{p}}\circ v_{0}
be decomposed as in the Standard Decomposition. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect;

(2)

\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v}}/p\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v}}
is semi-perfect;

(3)

\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}/p\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}
is semi-perfect;

(4)

\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v_{p}}}/p\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v_{p}}}
is semi-perfect.

Proof.

We may view (iii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv) as a special case of (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii).

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Note that
\mathcal{O}_{v}
projects onto
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v}}=\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}
.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(iii). Let
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}\supseteq\mathcal{O}_{v}
. There is nothing to show if
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
, so assume
x\notin\mathcal{O}_{v}
. This implies
x^{-1}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
; in particular,
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}^{\times}
and
x^{1-p}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
. Hence, we may find
y_{0},z_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
with
x^{1-p}=y^{p}+pz
. But then
x=y^{\prime p}+pz^{\prime}
for
y^{\prime}=xy\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}
and
z^{\prime}=x^{p}z\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Let
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{v_{0}}
. There is nothing to show if
x\in p\mathcal{O}_{v}
, so assume
x\notin p\mathcal{O}_{v}\supset\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}
. This implies
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{0}}^{\times}
, so we can find
y,z\in\mathcal{O}_{v}\cap\mathcal{O}_{v_{0}}^{\times}
such that
\overline{x}=\overline{y}^{p}+p\overline{z}
in
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{v}}=\mathcal{O}_{v}/\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}
.
However, since
\mathcal{M}_{v_{0}}\subset p\mathcal{O}_{v}
, we must have
x-y^{p}\in p\mathcal{O}_{v}
.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Write
\Gamma=vK
and recall that
\Gamma_{p}
is the maximal convex subgroup not containing
vp
. By assumption and Lemma
4.14
, the value group
v_{p}K=\Gamma/\Gamma_{p}
is
p
-divisible.
Let
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}
and find
y_{0},z_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}
such that
x=y_{0}^{p}+pz_{0}
. A posteriori, we observe that
y_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
because
pz_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
by Theorem
2.46
4
. If
z_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
, we are done; otherwise
z_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{v}
, i.e.,
vz_{0}<0
and
vz_{0}\in\Gamma_{p}
. We would like to use assumption (iii) a second time, but this will not be successful if we apply it to
pz_{0}
directly. Instead, let
c\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
be any constant of value
v_{p}c=\frac{1}{p^{2}}v_{p}p\in\Gamma/\Gamma_{p}
, so that
v(pc^{-p})\gg-vz_{0}
and hence
pc^{-p}z_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
.
In a second pass, we may write
pc^{-p}z_{0}=y_{1}^{p}+pz_{1}
, again with
y_{1}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
and
z_{1}\in\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}
. Therefore,

x=y_{0}^{p}+pz_{0}=y_{0}^{p}+(cy_{1})^{p}+pc^{p}z_{1}.

Finally, note that
y_{0}^{p}+(cy_{1})^{p}\equiv(y_{0}+cy_{1})^{p}
modulo
p\mathcal{O}_{v}
and
c^{p}z_{1}\in\mathcal{O}_{v}
.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

The somewhat unusual condition about the value
vp
is essential (it is not enough to require that
vp
is not minimal positive in
vK
), as the following example shows (suggested to us by Margarete Ketelsen). Consider the fraction field of the Witt vector ring
K=\Frac(W(k))
over the field
k=\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)^{1/p^{\infty}}
, the perfect hull of
\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)
. Endow
K
with the composite valuation
v=v_{t}\circ w
, where
w
is the valuation corresponding to the Witt ring and
v_{t}
is the
t
-adic valuation on
k
; in particular,
w=v_{p}
. Even though
vp
is not minimal positive in
vK
,
v_{p}p=wp
is minimal positive in
v_{p}K=\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}/p\mathcal{O}_{v_{p}}\cong k
is perfect. However,
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is not semi-perfect: the element
t^{-1}p
does not admit a
p
-th root modulo
p
—any such root would have value
\frac{1}{p}v(t^{-1}p)
, which reduces to
\frac{1}{p}v_{p}p
in
v_{p}K
. This would contradict the minimality of
v_{p}p
.

Theorem 4.17

(Criterion for semi-perfectness)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect and
vp
is not minimal positive in
vK
.

(2)

K^{*}v^{*}_{p}
is perfect and
\overline{v^{*}}(K^{*}v^{*}_{0})
is
p
-divisible for any (possibly trivial) elementary extension
(K^{*},v^{*})\succcurlyeq(K,v)
.

(3)

K^{*}v^{*}_{p}
is perfect and
\overline{v^{*}_{p}}(K^{*}v^{*}_{0})
is
p
-divisible for some
\aleph_{1}
-saturated elementary extension
(K^{*},v^{*})\succcurlyeq(K,v)
.

Proof.

For improved readability, we abbreviate
(F,w)\coloneqq(K^{*}v^{*}_{0},\overline{v^{*}_{p}})
, which is, we recall, the “middle” rank 1 place in the Standard Decomposition.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F=K^{*}v_{0}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{*}v_{p}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{*}v^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{*}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w=\overline{v^{*}_{p}}}

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Both properties in (i) are first-order and are, hence, preserved in elementary extensions. We may write the residue field
K^{*}v_{p}^{*}=Fw
as

Fw=\Frac((\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}/p\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}})_{\Red})

by Theorem
2.46
3
. Consequently, if the Frobenius map on
\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}/p\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}
is surjective, then so is the Frobenius on
Fw
.
The value group
\overline{v^{*}}F
is given by
\Conv(vp)
, cf. Theorem
2.46
1
, and hence
p
-divisible by Lemma
4.14
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii). Note that
\overline{v^{*}_{p}}(K^{*}v^{*}_{0})=wF
must be
p
-divisible as a quotient of
\Conv(vp)
, which itself is
p
-divisible.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(i). It suffices to show that
\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}/p\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}
is semi-perfect and
v^{*}p
is not minimal positive, since (i) is a first-order property.
If
wF
is
p
-divisible, then in particular,
v^{*}_{p}p
is not minimal positive in
v^{*}_{p}K^{*}
. Therefore, by applying Lemma
4.15
, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{O}_{w}/p\mathcal{O}_{w}
is semi-perfect.

The remaining argument is a transfinite version of the construction of an approximate
p
-th root (similar to the proof of Lemma
4.9
).

Let
a\in\mathcal{O}_{w}
. By transfinite recursion, we define a sequence
\{a_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\omega_{1}}
in
\mathcal{O}_{w}
satisfying

(4.5)

w(a_{\beta}^{p}-a)>w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a)\geq 0\quad\text{if $w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a)<wp$}

for all ordinals
\alpha<\beta<\omega_{1}
, as follows:

(0)

Set
a_{0}=0
.

(1)

For any
a_{\alpha}
previously defined, set
a_{\alpha+1}=a_{\alpha}
if
w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a)\geq wp
. Otherwise, using the assumption that
wF
is
p
-divisible and
Fw
is perfect, choose
b\in\mathcal{O}_{w}
such that

(4.6)

w(b^{p})=w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a)\frac{b^{p}}{a_{\alpha}^{p}-a}\equiv 1\pmod{\mathcal{M}_{w}}.

Set
a_{\alpha+1}=a_{\alpha}-b
. We obtain

\displaystyle a_{\alpha+1}^{p}-a=(a_{\alpha}-b)^{p}-a\equiv a_{\alpha}^{p}-a-b^{p}\pmod{p\mathcal{O}_{w}}

\displaystyle w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a-b^{p})>w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a),

the latter implied by (
4.6
). Together, these yield

w(a_{\alpha+1}^{p}-a)\geq\min\{w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a-b^{p}),wp\}>w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a).

(2)

Let
\lambda<\omega_{1}
be a limit ordinal. As before, set
a_{\lambda}=a_{\alpha}
if
w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a)\geq wp
for some
\alpha<\lambda
. Otherwise, first lift
a
and each
a_{\alpha}
from
\mathcal{O}_{w}=\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}_{p}}/\mathcal{M}_{v^{*}_{0}}
to elements
c,c_{\alpha}
in
\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}_{p}}\subseteq K^{*}
. It then follows from
\mathcal{M}_{v_{p}^{*}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{v^{*}}
and (
4.5
) that

v^{*}(c_{\beta}^{p}-c)>v^{*}(c_{\alpha}^{p}-c)\quad\text{for all $\alpha<\beta<\lambda$.}

Therefore, the set of formulas

p(x)=\{v^{*}(x^{p}-c)>v^{*}(c_{\alpha}^{p}-c)\}_{\alpha<\lambda}

defines a 1-type over constants
\{c\}\cup\{c_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha<\lambda}
. Using saturation of
(K^{*},v^{*})
, we may find a realisation
d\in K^{*}
. In particular,
d\in\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}_{p}}
, and so we may set
a_{\lambda}=d\mathcal{M}_{v^{*}_{0}}\in\mathcal{O}_{w}
.

The sequence defined above satisfies (
4.5
) by construction. If
w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a)<wp
for all
\alpha<\omega_{1}
, we would obtain an uncountable, strictly increasing sequence in the interval
[0,wp]
. But this is impossible because any interval in an archimedean ordered abelian group is second-countable. The only other option is that
w(a_{\alpha}^{p}-a)\geq wp
for some countable ordinal
\alpha
, which yields a
p
-th root of
a
modulo
p
, as required.
∎

In a second step, we characterise the property that
\Gamma/\Gamma_{0}
is divisible. To this end, define:

Definition 4.18
.

Let
\Gamma
be an ordered abelian group and
\gamma\in\Gamma_{>0}
a fixed constant. If
\Gamma
satisfies the axiom scheme

\forall x>n\gamma\,\exists y\exists z\,(x=ny+z\wedge 0\leq z<n\gamma)

for all integers
n>1
, we say that
\Gamma
is
regular above
\gamma
.

Remark 4.19
.

This is compatible with the usual notion of
regularity
for ordered abelian groups, as it is used by Robinson and Zakon
[
46
]
. Indeed, an ordered abelian group
\Gamma
is regular if and only if
\Gamma
is regular above
\gamma
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{>0}
.

Proposition 4.20

(Characterisation of regularity above
\gamma
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be an ordered abelian group and
\gamma\in\Gamma_{>0}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\Gamma
is regular above
\gamma
;

(2)

\Gamma/\Conv(\gamma)
is divisible;

(3)

\Gamma^{*}/\Conv(\gamma)
is divisible for any elementary extension
(\Gamma^{*},\gamma)\succcurlyeq(\Gamma,\gamma)
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii), since being regular above
\gamma
is a first-order property.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). Write
\Delta\coloneqq\Conv(\gamma)
. Let
x\in\Gamma\setminus\Delta
be positive and
n\geq 2
. By regularity, we may find
y\in\Gamma
and
z\in\Delta
such that
x=ny+z
, so
\overline{x}=n\overline{y}\in\Gamma/\Delta
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). Let
n\geq 2
and
x>n\gamma
be given. By divisibility of
\Gamma/\Delta
, we can find
y^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
z^{\prime}\coloneq x-ny^{\prime}\in\Delta
. Choose
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
k\cdot n\gamma\leq z^{\prime}<(k+1)\cdot n\gamma
and define
z\coloneq z^{\prime}-k\cdot n\gamma
, where
0\leq z<n\gamma
. Setting
y=y^{\prime}+k\gamma
, we obtain the desired expression
x=ny+z
.
∎

Fact 4.21
.

Let
K
be a field endowed with a non-trivial valuation
v
. Assume that
v
decomposes as
v=v^{\prime\prime}\circ w\circ v^{\prime}
with
w
of rank 1.
If
(K,v)
is
\aleph_{1}
-saturated, then
w
has value group
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
w
is henselian defectless (cf. Definition
2.26
).

Proof.

Let
E
be the residue field of
v^{\prime}
on which
w
is defined. Without loss of generality, we may assume
wE
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
and
1\in wE
. Choose
\varpi\in\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}^{\times}
such that
w\overline{\varpi}=1
.

Claim 1.
wE
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof of Claim 1.

If
wE
is discrete, then
wE\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Otherwise,
wE
is dense. For any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, the set

p(x)=\left\{s\cdot v\varpi<v(x)<t\cdot v\varpi\right\}_{s,\>\!t\;\!\in\;\!\mathbb{Q},\,\gamma\;\!\in\>\!(s,t)}

defines a 1-type in
(K,v)
by density. Any realisation
x
of
p(x)
lies in
\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}^{\times}
and satisfies
w\overline{x}=\gamma
. A realisation exists by saturation and thus
\gamma\in wE
.

\diamond

By a similar argument,
(E,w)
is complete of rank 1 and hence henselian
[
43
, Chap. II, (4.6), (6.7)]
.

We present the proof of defectlessness only in the case that
E
is perfect (this is the only case we need later for our applications; see Remark
4.22
below for the general case).

Claim 2.
(E,w)
is algebraically maximal.

Proof of Claim 2.

Let
E(\alpha)/E
be an immediate finite simple extension and
\widetilde{w}
the unique prolongation of
w
to
E^{\sepc}
.
Assume towards a contradiction that
\alpha
has minimal polynomial
f(X)\in E[X]
with roots
\alpha=\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\notin E
.
By the Conjugacy Theorem
2.14
,

\widetilde{w}(c-\alpha_{1})=\ldots=\widetilde{w}(c-\alpha_{n})\quad\text{for any $c\in E$,}

and therefore,
w(f(c))=n\cdot\widetilde{w}(c-\alpha)
.
Observe that
w(f(E))
is bounded, as otherwise, for any
c\in E
with
\widetilde{w}(c-\alpha)=\frac{1}{n}w(f(c))
sufficiently large,
\alpha\in E(c)=E
by Krasner’s Lemma
2.33
. Moreover, we can show that the maximum is attained in
wE\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\gamma\coloneqq\sup w(f(E))\in\mathbb{R}
and let
g(X)\in\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}[X]
be a lift of the polynomial
f(X)
. Consider the 1-type

q(x)\coloneqq\left\{r\cdot v\varpi<v(g(x))\right\}_{r\>\!\in\>\!(-\infty,\gamma)\;\!\cap\;\!\mathbb{Q}}

Any realisation
x\in K
satisfies
x\in\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}
and
w(f(c_{0}))=\gamma
for
c_{0}\coloneqq\overline{x}\in E
.
But since
E(\alpha)/E
is immediate, we can find
a,b\in E
such that

\widetilde{w}(c_{0}-\alpha)=w(a)\widetilde{w}\left(\frac{c_{0}-\alpha}{a}-b\right)>0.

Hence, taking
c_{1}\coloneqq c_{0}-ab\in E
yields

\widetilde{w}(c_{1}-\alpha)>\widetilde{w}(c_{0}-\alpha),

and therefore,

w(f(c_{1}))>w(f(c_{0}))=\gamma,

which contradicts our choice of
\gamma
. Thus, the extension
E(\alpha)/E
is trivial.

\diamond

Claim 3. Any finite extension
F/E
is algebraically maximal.

Proof of Claim 3.

To this end, let
L
be an unramified extension of
(K,v^{\prime})
with residue field
F
. Repeat the proof of Claim 2 by replacing
K
and
E
with
L
and
F
, noting that
L
must be saturated as a finite extension of
K
.

\diamond

Claim 4. Any finite extension
F/E
is defectless.

Proof of Claim 4.

It suffices to consider Galois extensions
F/E
.

If
wE=\mathbb{R}
, we must have
e(F/E)=1
. Let
E_{1}/E
be the inertia subfield of
F/E
, so that
[E_{1}:E]=f(F/E)
and
F/E_{1}
is an immediate extension. By Claim 3, we have
F=E_{1}
, and the conclusion follows.

If
wE\cong\mathbb{Z}
, consider instead the tower of field extensions (cf.
[
38
, Cor. 2.10]
)

E=E_{0}\subseteq E_{1}\subseteq E_{2}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq E_{r}=F,

where
E_{1}
is the ramification subfield of
F/E
and each
E_{i+1}/E_{i}
is a
C_{p}
-extension for
i=1,\ldots,r-1
, obtained by applying the Galois correspondence to a composition series of the
p
-group
R(F/E)=\Gal(F/E_{1})
. By Claim 3, none of the extensions
E_{i+1}/E_{i}
can be immediate, so they are all defectless instead. Finally, this means that
F/E
can be written as a tower of defectless extensions and hence must be defectless itself.

∎

Remark 4.22
.

The standard strategy for proving this fact is the following: it is a formal consequence of
\aleph_{1}
-saturation that
(E,w)
is spherically complete, i.e., any decreasing filtration of non-empty open balls has non-empty intersection (cf.
[
25
, Lem. 2.3.7]
). In general, spherical completeness implies defectlessness (this is, in particular, true without the assumption that
E
is perfect, see
[
56
, Thm. 31.14./31.21.]
). This implication is related to the more widely known fact that complete rank 1 valued fields are defectless (cf. the remark following
[
43
, Chap. II, (6.8)]
).
Note that in our proof, which is a simplified implementation of this strategy, we only used finitely many constants for our types (except when arguing that
w
is henselian). Hence, we can make do with the weaker assumption of
\aleph_{0}
-saturation to show that
w
is defectless (at least whenever
E
is perfect and
w
is henselian).

As a consequence of the Saturation-Decomposition Method, we obtain the following application: a sharpened version of the Taming Theorem of Jahnke-Kartas
[
25
, Thm. 6.2.3(I)]
. In particular, the conclusion about absolute Galois groups can be seen as a non-standard, partial version of the almost purity theorem.

Theorem 4.23

(Taming Theorem)
.

Let
(K,v)
be an
\aleph_{1}
-saturated valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Consider the valuations
v_{0}
and
\overline{v_{p}}
in the decomposition
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v:K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}

Assume
v_{0}
is henselian. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

v_{0}
is tame.

(2)

v_{0}
is absolutely unramified if and only if
vK
is regular above
vp
.

(3)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

\overline{v_{p}}
is tame.

(b)

\overline{v_{p}}
is absolutely unramified.

(c)

\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect and
vp
is not minimal positive in
vK
.

(d)

\overline{v_{p}}
has
p
-divisible value group and perfect residue field.

In particular, if the equivalent conditions for (ii) and (iii) are satisfied, then there exist canonical isomorphisms

G_{K}\cong G_{Kv_{0}}\cong G_{Kv_{p}}.

Proof.

By ramification theory (Fact
2.22
),
v_{0}
is tame outright and
v_{0}
is absolutely unramified if and only if
v_{0}K
is divisible. By Proposition
4.20
, this is precisely the case when
vK
is regular above
vp
.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a). Immediate.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(d). If
\overline{v_{p}}
is tame, then
Kv_{p}
is perfect and
\overline{v_{p}}(Kv_{0})
is
p
-divisible by ramification theory.

(d)
\Rightarrow
(b). By Fact
4.21
, we know that
\overline{v_{p}}
is henselian defectless and has value group
\mathbb{R}
. The absolute ramification subgroup of
\overline{v_{p}}
is trivial: if not, condition (d) implies that
(Kv_{0})^{\sepc}
is a non-trivial immediate extension of its ramification subfield; one can then find a finite defect extension of
Kv_{0}
(cf. Definition
2.24
). Hence,
\overline{v_{p}}
is tame, and therefore absolutely unramified.

(c)
\Leftrightarrow
(d). This follows immediately from Theorem
4.17
, our criterion for semi-perfectness.

If
v_{0}
and
\overline{v_{p}}
are indeed absolutely unramified, then their absolute inertia groups are trivial and the absolute Galois groups of
K
,
Kv_{0}
, and
Kv_{p}
are canonically isomorphic by Fact
2.21
.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

The situation trivialises in the case that
vp
is minimal positive in
vK
, because then
\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}
is trivial and
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}\cong Kv
. If
Kv
is perfect, then
Kv_{0}\cong\Frac(W(Kv))
by the usual Witt structure theorem we already used in the proof of Theorem
4.11
, see also Remark
4.13
.
Moreover, we note that the above theorem is also true for
\aleph_{0}
-saturated valued fields
(K,v)
. However, this does not follow directly from our treatment.

Following Kuhlmann-Rzepka, we give a name to condition
3c
in the theorem above:

Definition 4.25
.

Let
(K,v)
be a valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. We call it
roughly deeply ramified
if
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect and
vp
is not minimal positive in
vK
.

In
[
37
]
, this is the weakest notion of deep ramification. Using the sharp version of the Taming Theorem above, we recover a result of Kuhlmann-Rzepka on deeply ramified fields. The proof below is strikingly short and follows the non-standard strategy of Jahnke-Kartas, who employ it to show that perfectoid fields (a subclass of the class of deeply ramified fields) are closed under finite extensions.

Corollary 4.26

(Almost Purity,
[
37
, Thm. 1.5]
)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a roughly deeply ramified valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Let
L/K
be an algebraic extension and
w
a prolongation of
v
to
L
. Then
(L,w)
is roughly deeply ramified.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case that
L/K
is finite, since the valuation ring of
L
is the union of the valuation rings of all finite subextensions. Consider the ultrapowers
K^{*}
and
L^{*}
of
K
and
L
, with respect to the same non-principal ultrafilter over
\mathbb{N}
. These ultrapowers are automatically
\aleph_{1}
-saturated
[
12
, Thm. 6.1.1]
. Moreover,
L^{*}/K^{*}
is a finite extension of degree equal to
[L:K]
. The Standard Decomposition now reads:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$w^{*}:\;$}L^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{*}w^{*}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{*}w^{*}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{*}w^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$v^{*}:\;$}K^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{*}v^{*}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{*}v^{*}_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{*}v^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{*}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{w^{*}_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{w^{*}}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{w^{*}}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{w^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{w^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{w^{*}}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{w^{*}}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{w^{*}}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{w^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{w^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{w^{*}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{*}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v^{*}_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v^{*}}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v^{*}}}}

As we have noted before, being roughly deeply ramified is a first-order property, so it holds for
(K^{*},v^{*})
. By the Taming Theorem
4.23
, the valuation
\overline{v^{*}_{p}}
has
p
-divisible value group and perfect residue field. Consequently,
\overline{w^{*}_{p}}
will have
p
-divisible value group and perfect residue field as well (cf. Fact
2.11
). Therefore, yet again by the Taming Theorem, the valued field
(L^{*},w^{*})
is roughly deeply ramified, and so must be
(L,w)
.
∎

In contrast, the proof in
[
37
]
is purely algebraic and proceeds by an analysis of defect.

4.3.3.
Non-standard proof of the Transfer Lemma

With the Taming Theorem at hand, we can now give a second proof of the Transfer Lemma. This relies only on one additional fact: small Galois groups are encoded in the theory of fields. The Transfer Lemma then follows as an immediate corollary.

Lemma 4.27

(Klingen,
[
28
]
)
.

Let
K
be a field with small absolute Galois group. Then for any field
F
,

K\equiv F\Longrightarrow G_{K}\cong G_{F}.

Proof (Sketch).

Let
n\geq 1
be a natural number. By definition,
K
admits only finitely many extensions of degree at most
n
. Let
K_{n}
denote the compositum of all of these, and set
G_{n}\coloneqq\Gal(K_{n}/K)
(which is finite too). Then
K
satisfies the first-order sentences
\varphi_{n}
expressing “there exists an isomorphism between
G_{n}
and the Galois group of the compositum of all extensions of degree
\leq n
, restricting to an isomorphism between
G_{n-1}
and the Galois group of the compositum of all extensions of degree
\leq n-1
”. Any field
F\equiv K
satisfies all
\varphi_{n}
. From this, an isomorphism of inverse systems can be recovered. Therefore,

G_{K}=\varprojlim_{n}G_{n}\cong G_{F}.\qed

Corollary 4.28
.

Let
(K,v)
be a henselian valued field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Assume further that

(1)

G_{K}
is small;

(2)

vK
is regular above
vp
and
p
-divisible.

Then, there exists a field
F
of characteristic
p
satisfying
G_{K}\cong G_{F}
. In particular, the case
vK=\Conv(vp)
recovers the Transfer Lemma.

Proof.

By the preceding lemma, we can pass to an
\aleph_{1}
-saturated elementary extension
(K^{*},v^{*})
, preserving (ii) and without changing the (small) absolute Galois group.
Recall Lemma
3.19
showed that
(K^{*})^{\times}/(K^{*})^{\times p}
is finite, implying
K^{*}v_{p}^{*}
is perfect by Pop’s Lemma
3.20
(i). Lastly, since
v^{*}
has
p
-divisible value group, so does
\overline{v^{*}_{p}}
.
Hence, by the Taming Theorem
4.23
, we obtain a chain of canonical isomorphisms

G_{K}\cong G_{K^{*}}\cong G_{K^{*}v_{0}^{*}}\cong G_{K^{*}v_{p}^{*}}.

So we may choose
F=K^{*}v_{p}^{*}
as our characteristic
p
field.
∎

4.4.
The work of Jahnke-Kartas

We have now sufficiently developed the Saturation-Decomposition Method in the non-discrete case in order to give an exposition of the ideas of Jahnke and Kartas. Their main technical result is:

Theorem 4.29

(
[
25
, Thm. 1.7.3]
)
.

Let
(K,v)\subseteq(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
be two henselian valued fields with regular dense value groups and residue fields of characteristic
p
. Suppose that one may choose
\varpi\in\mathcal{M}_{v}\setminus\{0\}
such that

(1)

\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}
and
\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}
are semi-perfect, and

(2)

\mathcal{O}_{v}[\varpi^{-1}]
and
\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}[\varpi^{-1}]
are algebraically maximal coarsenings.

Then

(K,v)\preccurlyeq(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}.

Let us sketch a proof of Theorem
4.29
. Two additional ingredients are needed.

Ingredients
.

(1)

Add a constant symbol for
\varpi
to our language. Then, the class of valued field
(K,v)
described by Theorem
4.29
is elementary
[
25
, Prop. 4.1.4]
. On its own, the property that
\mathcal{O}_{v}[\varpi^{-1}]
is algebraically maximal is not first-order axiomatisable—it is crucial that we additionally know that
\mathcal{O}_{v}/p\mathcal{O}_{v}
is semi-perfect.

(2)

What enables us to use methods from logic are Kuhlmann’s results on the model theory of tame valued fields
[
39
]
. In particular, we will use the following Ax-Kochen/Ershov principle:

Let
(K,v)\subseteq(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
be tame valued fields. Then:

(K,v)\preccurlyeq(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow vK\preccurlyeq v^{\prime}K^{\prime}\quad\text{{and}}\quad Kv\preccurlyeq K^{\prime}v^{\prime}.

Moreover, this AKE principle is
resplendent
, that is to say, it still holds if we add extra structure on the residue field sort. For instance, we may add a predicate for a valuation ring to the residue field sort of
(K,v)
.

Proof of Theorem
4.29
(Sketch).

We can interpret
\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}
in
(K,v)
, so the implication from left to right follows immediately. Conversely, assume
\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}
.

The first part is saturating. Let
(K^{*},v^{*})
and
(K^{\prime*},v^{\prime*})
be ultrapowers of
(K,v)
and
(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
, with respect to the same non-principal ultrafilter on
\mathbb{N}
. By Łoś’s Theorem,

\displaystyle(K,v)\preccurlyeq(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow(K^{*},v^{*})\preccurlyeq(K^{\prime*},v^{\prime*})

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime*}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime*}}.

Crucially, Ingredient
1
says that the assumptions on
\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}
and
\mathcal{O}_{v}[\varpi^{-1}]
are preserved in the ultrapower. Since ultrapowers are
\aleph_{1}
-saturated, we may thus assume, without loss of generality, that we are working with an instance of the theorem where the given valued fields
(K,v)
and
(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
are
\aleph_{1}
-saturated.

The second part of the proof is decomposition: Theorem
2.46
yields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{\varpi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v_{\varpi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}

where
v_{\varpi}=\overline{v_{\varpi}}\circ v_{0}
has divisible value group by regularity of
vK
. Thus,
v_{\varpi}
is tame by the proof of the Taming Theorem
4.23
; the same holds for
v^{\prime}_{\varpi}
. By saturation,
9
9

9

There are several ways to make this precise. One is to use the Keisler-Shelah Theorem
[
12
, 6.1.15]
to chose an ultrafilter at the start of the proof that makes
\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime*}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime*}}
(plainly) isomorphic. This simplifies things considerably at the cost of a model-theoretic black box. A different approach is to observe that the nilradical of
\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}
is uniformly
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1},\omega}
-definable. This suffices, because
\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}\preccurlyeq_{\omega_{1},\omega}\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime*}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime*}}
is a formal consequence of the structures being
\aleph_{1}
-saturated. For more details, see
[
22
]
.

\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v}\preccurlyeq\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}\Longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}=(\mathcal{O}_{v}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v})_{\Red}\preccurlyeq(\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}/\varpi\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}})_{\Red}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}^{\prime}},

and consequently,
(Kv_{\varpi},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}})\preccurlyeq(K^{\prime}v^{\prime}_{\varpi},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}^{\prime}})
.
Ingredient
2
provides the resplendent tame AKE principle. It follows that

(4.7)

(K,v_{\varpi},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}})\preccurlyeq(K^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{\varpi},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}^{\prime}}).

We can recover our original valuation
v=\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\circ v_{\varpi}
definably—as per Section
2.3.1
—via

x\in\mathcal{O}_{v}\Longleftrightarrow xv_{\varpi}=\res(x)\in\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}.

Hence, (
4.7
) implies
(K,v)\preccurlyeq(K^{\prime},v^{\prime})
by Fact
2.69
.
∎

We describe two main consequences. The first one is a new class of examples for the AKE philosophy. Prior to Jahnke and Kartas’ work, such examples were inaccessible due to our notoriously limited understanding of the first-order theory of positive characteristic fields (see
[
3
]
) and of the defect.

Corollary 4.30
.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)^{h}
be the henselisation of
\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)
inside
\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)
. Then

(\mathbb{F}_{p}(t)^{h})^{1/p^{\infty}}\preccurlyeq\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)^{1/p^{\infty}}\preccurlyeq\widehat{\mathbb{F}_{p}(\!(t)\!)^{1/p^{\infty}}}.

Proof.

Simply pick
\varpi=t
and observe that, by a density argument, all three valued fields have the same truncated valuation ring
\mathcal{O}/\varpi\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{F}_{p}[t]^{1/p^{\infty}}/(t)
and trivial coarsenings
\mathcal{O}[t^{-1}]
. The value groups are isomorphic to
\frac{1}{p^{\infty}}\mathbb{Z}
and therefore regular dense. Now apply Theorem
4.29
.
∎

As a second application, one recovers the Generalised Fontaine-Wintenberger Theorem
4.8
.

Theorem 4.31

(Model-theoretic Fontaine-Wintenberger,
[
25
, Thm. 6.2.3(II)]
)
.

Let
(K,v)
be a perfectoid field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
. Let
(K^{*},v^{*})=(K,v)^{\mathbb{N}}/\mathcal{U}
be an ultrapower of
(K,v)
with respect to a non-principal ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

The valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}
embeds into
\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}
multiplicatively via

(4.8)

\natural:\mathcal{O}_{v}^{\flat}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}},\quad x\longmapsto\ulim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(x^{\sharp})^{1/p^{n}}

and induces an elementary embedding
\tau:(K^{\flat},v^{\flat})\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow(K^{*}v^{*}_{p},\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v^{*}}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v^{*}}})
.

(2)

The canonical isomorphism of Theorem
4.23
,

G_{K^{*}}\cong G_{K^{*}v_{p}^{*}}\text{ restricts to }\ G_{K}\cong G_{K^{\flat}},

the Fontaine-Wintenberger isomorphism.

Let us briefly explain the main ideas of the beautiful proof.

The musical maps
\sharp
and
\natural
defined in (
4.3
) and (
4.8
) are both given by limits of a certain kind. Though
\sharp
is only multiplicative and additive modulo
p
, by passing to an ultrapower, the induced embedding into the canonical residue field
K^{*}v_{p}^{*}
becomes a genuine valued field embedding, see
[
25
, Lem. 6.1.2]
.

Perhaps an even more subtle observation is elementarity. Let
\varpi^{\flat}
be such
\varpi=(\varpi^{\flat})^{\sharp}
has value
v\varpi=vp
(Fact
4.7
) and write
\pi\coloneqq(\varpi^{\flat})^{\natural}\in\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}
. Then the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\overline{\natural}:\;$}\mathcal{O}_{v^{\flat}}/(\varpi^{\flat})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{v}/(\varpi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}/(\varpi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{v^{*}}/(\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x/(\varpi^{\flat})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\sharp}/(\varpi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\ulim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}x^{\sharp}\mbox{\larger\big/}(\varpi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\ulim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(x^{\sharp})^{1/p^{n}}\mbox{\larger\big/}(\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\preccurlyeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\;\Phi^{*}}

is equal to the map
\overline{\natural}
induced by
\natural
on
\mathcal{O}_{v^{\flat}}/(\varpi^{\flat})
. Here,
\iota
is induced by the diagonal embedding into the ultrapower (elementary by Łoś) and
\Phi^{*}
is the inverse of a non-standard Frobenius isomorphism. Loosely speaking,
\overline{\natural}
is an elementary embedding because it is a diagonal embedding, twisted twice by “cancelling” Frobenius isomorphisms
[
25
, Thm. 6.1.3]
.
One then shows that the embedding
\tau
is itself elementary by carefully checking that the assumptions of Theorem
4.29
are satisfied. The main difficulty in this step is in showing that
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v^{*}}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v^{*}}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v^{*}}}}[\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{\pi}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{\pi}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{\pi}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{\pi}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{\pi}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\displaystyle\overline{\pi}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\textstyle\overline{\pi}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\overline{\pi}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overline{\pi}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{\pi}}^{-1}]
is algebraically maximal, cf.
[
25
, Lem. 4.2.5]
.

The final conclusion in (ii) uses the special property of perfectoid fields that any finite extension generated by a polynomial of discriminant of value
<vp
can be generated by polynomials of arbitrarily small discriminant (essentially, by taking
p
-th roots of the coefficients iteratively). As a consequence,
\natural
interpolates between extensions of
K^{\flat}
, generated by polynomials of (potentially) arbitrarily small discriminant, and finite extensions of
K^{*}
coming from
K
, generated by polynomials of (actually) infinitesimal discriminant, i.e., of value
\ll vp
, making the latter unramified with respect to
v^{*}_{p}
, cf.
[
25
, Cor. 6.2.2]
.

We have described a proof of the Fontaine-Wintenberger Theorem via the Saturation-Decomposition Method. At the same time, recall that we used this theorem to give our first proof of the Transfer Lemma. Methodically, we have thus come full circle in our diagram on p.
4.1.2
.

Remark 4.32

(Limitations of the method)
.

If we follow the strategy of the proof of Theorem
4.29
, the results will be limited by the strength of the model-theoretic input that enters as Ingredient
2
. Indeed, Jahnke-Kartas show that Theorem
4.29
still holds if we replace the regularity assumption on value groups by
\Gamma_{v}\preccurlyeq\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}
, see
[
25
, Thm. 1.7.3]
.
This is done by adapting Kuhlmann’s model theory of tame valued fields to
roughly tame
valued fields (those
(K,v)
for which the core field
(Kv_{0},\overline{v})
is tame). It turns out that this weakening of assumptions is harmless, essentially because the (0, ​0)-place in the Standard Decomposition is always tame by the Lemma of Ostrowski
2.24
(cf.
[
25
, 3.3.7]
). In upcoming work of Jahnke and van der Schaaf, Theorem
4.29
is generalised even further using AKE principles for separably tame
valued fields due to Kuhlmann-Pal
[
36
]
and Anscombe (also upcoming).

5.
Proof of Main Theorem

We are finally ready to prove Theorem
Main Theorem
. One direction is straightforward:

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
K\equiv\mathbb{Q}_{p}
in the language of rings. Then
G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
has small absolute Galois group (Corollary
3.17
). Small absolute Galois groups are encoded in the theory of a field, so the conclusion follows (see Lemma
4.27
).
∎

For the other direction, we now assume
G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. Recall that by Proposition
4.11
, to prove
K\equiv\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, it suffices to find a valuation
v
on
K
satisfying the following properties:

(1)

(K,v)
is henselian of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
;

(2)

vK\equiv\mathbb{Z}
;

(3)

v(p)
is minimal positive;

(4)

Kv=\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Additionally, note that (ii) admits the following well-known algebraic description:

Fact 5.2

(
[
41
, Chap. 3]
)
.

For an ordered abelian group
\Gamma
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\Gamma\equiv\mathbb{Z}
;

(2)

\Gamma
has a convex subgroup
\Delta\cong\mathbb{Z}
such that
\Gamma/\Delta
is divisible;

(3)

\Gamma
has a minimal positive element and
[\Gamma:n\Gamma]=n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We will show that the canonical henselian valuation
v_{K}
, introduced in Section
2.3.3
, satisfies all the required properties.
The strategy is organised into four main steps:

Step 1.
.

Show that the canonical henselian valuation
v_{K}
has mixed characteristic
(0,p)
.

Step 1.1.
.

Find henselian valuations on
K
using the Galois characterisation of henselianity (Theorem
3.29
). Infer that the canonical henselian valuation
v_{K}
is non-trivial and satisfies
Kv_{K}\neq(Kv_{K})^{\sepc}
and
(v_{K}K:qv_{K}K)=q
for all
q\neq p
.

Step 1.2.
.

Deduce that
\charK K=0
using Idea
3.21
(Lemma
3.25
and Lemma
3.27
).

Step 1.3.
.

Prove that the prime-to-
p
roots of unity of
K
and
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
coincide using Lemma
3.4
.

Step 1.4.
.

Show that
\charK Kv_{K}\neq q
for all primes
q\neq p
via cyclotomic extensions.

Step 1.5.
.

Deduce that
\charK Kv_{K}=p
using the previous steps and ramification-theoretic considerations (Section
2.2
).

Step 2.
.

Apply the Standard Decomposition (Section
2.3.2
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{K}:K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,\>\!0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v_{p}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,\,p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}\lx@text@overline{\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\overline{v}$}}{\textstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\overline{v}$}}{\scriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\displaystyle 3\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\overline{v}$}\kern-0.5pt}}\phantom{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p,\,p)}

to decompose
v_{K}
into three places that can be treated separately. Show that
(Kv_{0},\overline{v_{p}})
has value group
\mathbb{Z}
and finite residue field.

Step 2.1.
.

Show
G_{Kv_{0}}
cannot be realised as an absolute Galois group in characteristic
p
. Use the Transfer Lemma
4.10
to conclude that
\overline{v_{p}}(Kv_{0})
is not
p
-divisible.

Step 2.2.
.

Deduce
\overline{v_{p}}(Kv_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
Kv_{p}
is finite using Pop’s Lemma
3.20
.

Step 3.
.

Conclude that
(K,v_{K})
is
p
-adically closed.

Step 3.1.
.

Observe that
Kv_{K}=\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Step 3.2.
.

Prove that
v_{K}K\equiv\mathbb{Z}
using the elementary characterisation of
\mathbb{Z}
(Fact
5.2
).

Step 3.3.
.

Prove that
v_{K}(p)
is minimal positive using a counting argument (Lemma
3.15
and Remark
3.16
).

Step 1.

To say anything about the canonical henselian valuation
v_{K}
, we first need to find at least one sufficiently non-trivial henselian valuation on
K
.

Step 1.1.

For primes
q\neq p
, any Sylow
q
-subgroups of
G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is isomorphic to a semi-direct product
\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}
(Proposition
2.29
). The Galois characterisation of henselianity (Theorem
3.29
) gives rise to henselian valuations
v_{q}
on
K
, tamely branching at
q
. In particular,
\charK Kv_{q}\neq q
and
v_{q}K\neq qv_{q}K
, i.e.,

r_{q}\coloneqq\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(v_{q}K/qv_{q}K)\geq 1.

For any
q\neq p
, let
K_{q}
be a fixed field of some Sylow
q
-subgroup of
G_{K}
. By henselianity, every
v_{q}
has a unique prolongation
w_{q}
to
K_{q}
. The
q
-rank
r_{q}
does not change when we pass from
K
to
K_{q}
. By Fact
2.22
, the ramification subgroup
R_{K_{q}}
is trivial (since
\charK K_{q}w_{q}\neq q
), and

\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{r_{q}}\cong I_{K_{q}}\vartriangleleft G_{K_{q}}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}

by Theorem
2.27
. Using the explicit description in Remark
2.30
, one can check that the only non-trivial closed normal subgroups of
\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}
are of the shape
q^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times\{1\}
and
q^{s_{1}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes q^{s_{2}}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Since the latter are non-abelian, we conclude
r_{q}=1
,
I_{K_{q}}\neq G_{K_{q}}
, and
K_{q}w_{q}
is not separably closed.

We can further see that
G_{K_{q}w_{q}}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}
as follows. If

I_{K_{q}}\cong q^{s}\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times\{1\}
, then

G_{K_{q}w_{q}}\cong G_{K_{q}}/I_{K_{q}}\cong C_{q^{s}}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}.

We are done if
s=0
, so assume for a contradiction that
s\geq 1
. By the Artin-Schreier Theorem
1.1
,
C_{q^{s}}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{q}
can only occur as an absolute Galois group of a field if
q=2
and
s=1
(in which case
C_{2}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is a direct product). Write
k\coloneqq K_{q}w_{q}
for this field satisfying
G_{k}\cong C_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and fix an algebraic closure
k^{\alg}
of
k
. Observe that
G_{k}
has a unique involution; therefore, there exists a unique real closed field
k\subset k^{\rc}\subset k^{\alg}
. Let
k(\sqrt{x})\coloneqq(k^{\rc})^{2\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
, with
x\in(k^{\rc})^{2}
, be the unique quadratic extension of
k
in
k^{\rc}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\alg}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\rc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k(\sqrt{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\mathbb{Z}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}

There are two embeddings of
k(\sqrt{x})
into
k^{\rc}
that fix
k
and thus two different induced orderings;
\sqrt{x}
is positive for one and negative for the other. This contradicts the uniqueness of
k^{\rc}
, as this field can only be the real closure of one of the two orderings on
k(\sqrt{x})
.
We conclude
I_{K_{q}}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}\cong G_{K_{q}w_{q}}
in all cases.

While we will continue working with
(K,v_{q})
and
(K_{q},w_{q})
until
Step 1.4.
, let us already make some observations about the canonical henselian valuation
v_{K}
. As the fields
K_{q}w_{q}
are not separably closed, we have
\mathcal{O}_{v_{K}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{v_{q}}
for all
q\neq p
by Lemma
2.51
. In particular,
v_{q}K
is a quotient of
v_{K}K
by a convex subgroup, so
v_{K}K
cannot be
q
-divisible. By repeating the above reasoning for
v_{K}
, we deduce that

r_{q}=\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(v_{K}K/qv_{K}K)=1,

so
(v_{K}K:qv_{K}K)=q
and
Kv_{K}
is not separably closed.

Step 1.2.

From
\charK Kv_{q}\neq q
, it follows that
\charK K\neq q
for all primes
q\neq p
. Therefore, to show
\charK K=0
, it only remains to exclude the case
\charK K=p
.

By Lemma
3.27
, the field
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
satisfies the following property: it has a
C_{p-1}
-extension (namely,
\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{p})/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
), which itself admits a
C_{p}
-extension that is not embeddable into a
C_{p^{2}}
-extension. As
G_{K}\cong G_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
, the same property holds for
K
. However, this cannot happen in characteristic
p
by Lemma
3.25
.

Step 1.3.

Determining the prime-to-
p
roots of unity of
K
(from Galois theory alone) is a somewhat subtle affair. We would like to use Lemma
3.4
, which gives a Galois characterisation of the roots of unity of certain valued fields
(K,v)
. Condition
3
in this lemma requires knowledge of the Galois group of the maximal pro-
q
Galois extension of
Kv
, which we would typically construct (say, over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
) by looking at the appropriate cyclotomic extension. Such a direct argument is not available to us due to circularity (we still do not know the prime-to-
p
roots of unity of
K
yet). So instead, we will use an auxiliary construction.

Consider the field

\mathbb{Q}_{p,q}\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}_{p}\bigl(\zeta_{q^{\infty}},\!\!\sqrt[q^{\infty}]{p}\bigr)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\bigl(\zeta_{q^{n}},\!\!\sqrt[q^{n}]{p}\bigr).

We compute its Galois group as

\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p,q}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})=\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p,q}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{q^{\infty}}))\rtimes\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})/\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times C),

where
C=\Gal(\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\zeta_{q})/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is a cyclic group of order dividing
q-1
(the precise order depends on
p
and
q
) and the semi-direct product is again given by the Frobenius action as in Remark
2.30
. Hence, there exists a Galois extension
L/K
with

\Gal(L/K)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times C).

Let
u_{q}
be the unique prolongation of
v_{q}
to
L
. By considering possible closed normal abelian subgroups isomorphic to
I_{u_{q}}(L/K)
as in

Step 1.1.
, we obtain

\Gal(Lu_{q}/Kv_{q})\cong C_{q^{s}}\rtimes(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times C).

Therefore,
Kv_{q}
admits a Galois extension with Galois group
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, which must be maximal pro-
q
since
G_{K_{q}w_{q}}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}
(this also implies
s=0
). Similarly, one shows that the same holds true for the residue field
Kv_{K}
.

These considerations show that
(K,v_{q})
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
3.4
, and hence,

\displaystyle\zeta_{q^{r}}\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}

\displaystyle\iff\text{$\mathbb{Q}_{p}$ admits a $C_{q^{r}}\times C_{q^{r}}$-extension}

\displaystyle\iff\text{$K$ admits a $C_{q^{r}}\times C_{q^{r}}$-extension}\iff\zeta_{q^{r}}\in K,

for any prime
q\neq p
and
r\geq 1
. In particular, the set of prime-to-
p
roots of unity in
K
is
\mu_{p-1}
for
p\neq 2
and
\mu_{2}
for
p=2
by Corollary
3.3
.

Step 1.4.

Assume that
\charK Kv_{K}=q
for some prime
q\neq p
. As
v_{K}
is a refinement of
v_{q}
, there exists an induced valuation
\overline{v_{K}}
on
Kv_{q}
with residue field
Kv_{K}
and of mixed characteristic
(\charK Kv_{q},q)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kv_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{v_{K}}}

Clearly,
\charK Kv_{q}\neq q
implies
\charK Kv_{q}=0
. We now derive a contradiction by considering the cyclotomic extension
K(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})/K
. By Lemma
3.6
, such a cyclotomic extension can be finite only in one of two exceptional cases, namely
K(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})=K(\zeta_{q})
or
K(\zeta_{2^{\infty}})=K(\zeta_{4})
. Neither of these occur: indeed, this follows by applying Lemma
3.4
to
K(\zeta_{q})
and
K(\zeta_{4})
; see also Remark
3.5
. Hence
K(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})/K
is an infinite cyclotomic extension. By henselianity, this extension is unramified with respect to
v_{q}
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.6
,

\Gal(K(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})/K)\cong\Gal(Kv_{q}(\zeta_{q^{\infty}})/Kv_{q})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}\times C.
